A Kronecker algorithm for locally closed sets over a perfect field
Nardo Giménez, Joos Heintz, Guillermo Matera, Luis Miguel Pardo, Mariana Pérez, Melina Privitelli

Abstract
We develop a probabilistic algorithm of Kronecker type for computing a Kronecker representation of a zero-dimensional linear section of an algebraic variety V V defined over a perfect field k k . The variety V V is the Zariski closure of the set of common zeros { F 1 = 0 , … , F r = 0 , G ≠ 0 } \{F_{1}=0,\ldots,F_{r}=0,G\not=0\} of multivariate polynomials F 1 , … , F r ∈ k ⁡ [ X 1 , … , X n ] F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}] outside a prescribed hypersurface { G = 0 } \{G=0\} . We assume that F 1 , … , F r F_{1},\ldots,F_{r} satisfy natural geometric conditions, such as regularity and radicality, in the local ring k ​ [ X 1 , … , X n ] G k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G} . Our approach combines homotopic deformation techniques with symbolic Newton-Hensel lifting and elimination. We discuss the concept of lifting curves as intermediate geometric objects that enable efficient computation. The complexity of the algorithm is expressed in terms of the degrees and arithmetic size of the input and achieves soft-quadratic complexity in these parameters. We provide detailed complexity analyses for arbitrary perfect fields, as well as for two important cases in computer algebra: finite fields and the field of rational numbers. For each case, we obtain sharp bounds on the size of the base field or required primes.



keywords
Polynomial systems, perfect fields, locally closed sets, complexity

1
Introduction

This research was promoted, supported, and occasionally conducted in
collaboration with Joos Heintz during his final years. We consider
it his posthumous research work, which is why we have included his
name as one of the co-authors.

Our main objective is to present a version of an algorithm for
solving multivariate polynomial systems that works over a general
perfect field
k
. This algorithm belongs to a family of algorithms
known as “Kronecker” algorithms. Therefore, we begin by discussing
the general concept of a Kronecker algorithm. More precisely, we
will see that a Kronecker algorithm can be viewed as a procedure
consisting of a sequence of
iterative deformations within a
solution variety
.

We believe it is useful to contextualize the main contributions of
this work by providing an overview of the TERA-Kronecker algorithm
suite and illustrating where our contributions fit within it. We do
not present the algorithm in the customary way. On the contrary, we
have attempted to offer an unconventional presentation (probably not
in accordance with Heintz’s taste), intended not only to serve the
aforementioned purpose but also provides a more didactic view —at
least as much as possible— of how and why these algorithms differ
from others in the standard mathematical literature and why they are
more efficient.

We aim to present the algorithm in a high-level language,
emphasizing the geometric and algebraic elements involved in its
iterations, while relegating the more technical details to the
remainder of the manuscript. This approach should also offer the
reader a clearer view of the specific contributions of the
TERA-Kronecker suite to the problem of solving systems of polynomial
equations.

1.1
Outline of the TERA-Kronecker suite

To simplify the discussion, throughout this section we consider an
algebraically closed field
\overline{k}
. We denote by
\mathbb{A}^{n}
the

n
-dimensional affine space
\overline{k}{}^{n}
. We begin with a
generalization of the notion of
solution variety
of

Shub and Smale 1993
to the field
\overline{k}
.

1.1.1
The solution variety

For any pair
(n,d)
of positive
integers, let

\mathcal{P}_{d}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}):=\overline{k}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]_{d}

be the
\overline{k}
-vector space of polynomials in the variables

X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
with coefficients in
\overline{k}
and degree at
most
d
. This vector space has dimension

\dim_{\overline{k}}(\mathcal{P}_{d}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))=\binom{d+n}{n}.

We denote by
\mathcal{P}_{d,\boldsymbol{0}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
the vector
subspace of polynomials of
\mathcal{P}_{d}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
that
vanish at the origin
\boldsymbol{0}:=(0,\ldots,0)\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
, namely:

\mathcal{P}_{d,\boldsymbol{0}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}):=\{F\in\mathcal{P}_{d}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}):F(\boldsymbol{0})=0\}.

Clearly,
\mathcal{P}_{d,\boldsymbol{0}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
is a subspace of
codimension 1 of
\mathcal{P}_{d}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
.

For every
r
-tuple of degrees
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}:=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})
, we
consider the following
\overline{k}
-vector spaces:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{P}_{d_{i}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r},\boldsymbol{0}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{P}_{d_{i},\boldsymbol{0}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}).

For simplicity, we fix
n
and drop the variables from the notation,
writing
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r},\boldsymbol{0}}

instead. It is clear that
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r},\boldsymbol{0}}
is

\overline{k}
-vector subspace of
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
of
codimension
r
. The dimension of
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
is

N_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}:=\dim_{\overline{k}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\binom{d_{i}+n}{n},

so that

\dim_{\overline{k}}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r},\boldsymbol{0}}=N_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}-r.

With these notations, we define the solution variety over

\overline{k}
associated with the degree sequence
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}
in
n

variables as follows:

V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}:=V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}^{\overline{k}}(n):=\{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\in\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}:\boldsymbol{F}_{r}(\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{0}\}.

Next, we examine some elementary properties of
V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
.
Consider the
evaluation map

\displaystyle ev_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}:\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{A}^{r},

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{F}_{r})

\displaystyle\mapsto\boldsymbol{F}_{r}(\boldsymbol{x}).

Observe that
V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
is the fiber
ev_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{0}\})
. Moreover, it is easy to see that
ev_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
is
surjective. In fact, this follows from the alternative expression

V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}=\{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{G})\in\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{r}\times\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r},\boldsymbol{0}}:\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{x})+\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{0}\}.

This identity shows that
V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
is, up to a permutation of
coordinates, the graph of the polynomial map

\displaystyle\varphi_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}:\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r},\boldsymbol{0}}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{A}^{r},

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{G})

\displaystyle\mapsto\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{x}).

Thus, there is a biregular isomorphism between
V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
and
the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r},\boldsymbol{0}}
. In
particular, we obtain the following:

Remark 1
.

V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
is a smooth, irreducible affine variety of Krull dimension

\dim V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}=N_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}+n-r.

Next we consider the canonical projections

\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{n}}

\textstyle{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{2}}
\textstyle{\boldsymbol{F}_{r}}
\textstyle{\boldsymbol{x}}

The projection
\Pi_{2}
is an onto mapping whose fiber at a given point
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
is the set
of
r
-tuples of polynomials vanishing on
\boldsymbol{x}
. More precisely, given
W\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
, let
I(W)\subset\overline{k}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
be the vanishing ideal of

W
, that is, the ideal of all polynomials in

\overline{k}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
vanishing at all the points of
W
.
Then the fiber
\Pi_{2}^{-1}(W)
is

\Pi_{2}^{-1}(W)=\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\cap(I(W)^{r}),

where
I(W)^{r}:=\prod_{i=1}^{r}I(W)
. In the case that
W
is the
singleton
W=\{\boldsymbol{x}\}
, we denote its vanishing ideal as

\mathfrak{m}_{\boldsymbol{x}}:=I(\{\boldsymbol{x}\})
and the corresponding fiber
is

\Pi_{2}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{x})=\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\cap(\mathfrak{m}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{r})=\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\mathcal{P}_{d_{i}}\cap\mathfrak{m}_{\boldsymbol{x}}).

Observe that
\mathcal{P}_{d_{i}}\cap\mathfrak{m}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
is a
vector subspace of codimension 1 of
\mathcal{P}_{d_{i}}
. Hence, the
fiber at any point
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
can be identified with a vector
subspace of
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
of codimension
r
, i.e.,

\dim\big(\Pi_{2}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{x}\})\big)=N_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}-r.

Finally, we may define the problem of solving a system of
r

polynomial equations of given degrees in terms of the projections
\Pi_{1}

and
\Pi_{2}
.

Problem 2

(Solving
r
Polynomial Equations in
n
Variables)
.

Given an algebraically closed field
\overline{k}
and an
r
-tuple

\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
of polynomials in
n
variables
of degrees bounded by the list
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}:=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})
, compute

\Pi_{2}\big(\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\})\big)
.

A problem with solving in this sense is that the mapping
\Pi_{1}
is
not onto for
r\geq 2
as it can be easily verified. Nevertheless,
there is a Zariski open subset

\mathcal{U}_{0}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
such that for any
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
, the fiber
\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\})
is not
empty. This may be proved considering multivariate resultants or the
effective Nullstellensatz, which we omit. In particular, the mapping

\Pi_{1}
is dominant and we always assume that
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{U}_{0}\subset\Pi_{1}(V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}})
.

In
Giménez et al. 2023
, the set of “defective”
r
-tuples
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
with
r\leq n
are studied. In
particular,
(
Giménez et al. 2023
, Theorem 3.6)
shows that there exists a
nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}\subset\mathcal{U}_{0}

with the following property: for any
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}:=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
,

({\sf H}_{1})

the polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
form a regular sequence,

({\sf H}_{2})

the ideal
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})
is radical.

In other words, any
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
defines an
ideal-theoretic complete intersection. Due to the fact that these
conditions hold for a generic
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
,
we now restrict our discussion about solving to this case.

Problem 3

(Solving a System Defining an Ideal-Theoretic Complete Intersection)
.

Given an algebraically closed field
\overline{k}
and an
r
-tuple

\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
of polynomials in
n
variables
of degrees bounded by the sequence
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}:=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})
,
defining an ideal-theoretic complete-intersection (i.e., satisfying

({\sf H}_{1})
-
({\sf H}_{2})
), compute
\Pi_{2}\big(\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\})\big)
.

1.1.2
The left action of
GL(n,\overline{k})
on the solution
variety

Let
r,n
be positive integers with
1\leq r\leq n
, and let
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}:=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})
be a degree sequence with
d_{i}\geq 1
for

1\leq i\leq r
. Let
V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
be the solution variety associated
with
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}
over
\overline{k}
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
be
a nonempty Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
such that
any
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
defines an ideal-theoretic
complete intersection.

Now consider the general linear group
GL(n,\overline{k})
and its
left action, given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}:GL(n,\overline{k})\times V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}

\displaystyle\to V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}},

\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda},(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\circ\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{-1}).

Since any
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
defines an
ideal-theoretic complete intersection, the Noether normalization
theorem guarantees the existence of a nonempty Zariski open subset

\mathcal{U}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\subset GL(n,\overline{k})
such that for any

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{U}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
, the following map is a finite
morphism:

\displaystyle\pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}:V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{A}^{n-r},

(1.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{x})_{n-r},

where
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
denotes the zero set of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
in
\mathbb{A}^{n}

and
(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{x})_{n-r}
denotes the first
n-r

coordinates of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}
. In algebraic terms, let

Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
be new variables defined via

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
Y_{n}\\
\end{array}\right):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X_{1}\\
\vdots\\
X_{n}\\
\end{array}\right),

and let
\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]
be the
\overline{k}
-algebra of
polynomial functions defined on
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
. Then we have an
integral ring extension

R_{r}:=\overline{k}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-r}]\hookrightarrow\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})].

(1.2)

We will show that, for a given
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}
, there exists a finite,
effectively computable subset

\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{d}_{r})\subset\overline{k}

such that, for every

\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
and for most choices of

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{S}^{n\times n}
, the inclusion above
is an integral ring extension.

Now consider the
r\times r
Jacobian matrix

J_{r}\boldsymbol{F}_{r}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial Y_{n-r+1}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial Y_{n}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial F_{r}}{\partial Y_{n-r+1}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial F_{r}}{\partial Y_{n}}\\
\end{array}\right)\in(\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})])^{r\times r}.

If
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
and

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{U}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
, then the determinant

\det(J_{r}\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
is not a zero-divisor in
\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]
. In particular, when
r=n
, the zero-dimensional variety

V(\boldsymbol{F}_{n})\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
is smooth.

Moreover,
\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]
is a Cohen-Macaulay ring, and
a free
R_{r}
-module of finite rank (see
Iversen 1973
, or the
inductive argument using the Quillen-Suslin theorem of

Giusti et al. 1993
). Therefore, by localizing at the multiplicative
set
S:=R_{r}\setminus\{0\}
, we obtain an integral
ring extension of function fields

K_{r}:=\overline{k}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-r})\hookrightarrow K_{r}\otimes_{R_{r}}\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]=S^{-1}\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})],

where
S^{-1}\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]
is a reduced, smooth Artin
ring (i.e., it has Krull dimension 0). Additionally, under these
conditions, we have

\mathrm{rank}_{R_{r}}\big(\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]\big)=\dim_{K_{r}}\big(S^{-1}\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]\big).

According to
Heintz 1983
, these quantities are bounded by the
degree of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
, that is,

\mathrm{rank}_{R_{r}}\big(\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]\big)\leq\deg\big(V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\big)\leq\mathcal{B}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}:=\prod_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}.

Here
\mathcal{B}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
is the
Bézout number associated
with the degree sequence
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}
. Furthermore,

Heintz 1983
shows that for generic
\lambda\in\mathcal{U}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
, the
first inequality is an equality. More precisely, there is a nonempty
Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\subset\mathcal{U}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
such that, for any
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
,

\mathrm{rank}_{R_{r}}\big(\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]\big)=\deg\big(V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\big).

The projection
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}:V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\to\mathbb{A}^{n-r}
of
(
1.1
) is generically unramified, and for
a generic point
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-r}
, the fiber

\pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{y}\})
is a smooth, zero-dimensional
variety. Moreover, by
Heintz 1983
,

\#\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{y}\})\big)=\deg\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{y}\})\big)=\deg(V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})).

Later, we will exhibit bounds (and probabilistic algorithms) to find
a
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\in GL(n,\overline{k})
and
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-r}

such that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{U}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
and the fiber

\pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{y}\})
is generically unramified.
Specifically, we will show that there exists a finite set

\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{d}_{r})\subset\overline{k}
such that,
for any
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
,
most choices of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{S}^{n\times n}
and
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathcal{S}^{n-r}
satisfy:

•

The extension
R_{r}\subset\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]
is integral,
\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]
is a free

R_{r}
-module,
\det J_{r}\boldsymbol{F}_{r}
is not a zero-divisor in

\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]
and the following inequality holds:

\mathrm{rank}_{R_{r}}\big(\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]\big)\leq\deg\big(V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\big).

•

The fiber
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{y}\})
is a smooth,
zero-dimensional variety, whose ideal is defined by the polynomials

F_{1}(\boldsymbol{y},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n}),\ldots,F_{r}(\boldsymbol{y},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n})
, and is unramified, with

\#\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{y}\})\big)=\deg\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{y}\})\big)=\mathrm{rank}_{R_{r}}\big(\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})]\big).

1.1.3
First approach: a procedure by jumping through solution
varieties

With notations as before, given a degree sequence
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}:=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})
, we consider the solution variety
V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
and the canonical projections

\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{n}}

Let
\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
be the
nonempty Zariski open set such that any
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
defines an ideal-theoretic complete
intersection (i.e., satisfies
({\sf H}_{1})
and
({\sf H}_{2})
). We
now discuss a first approach to solving the system
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})=\Pi_{2}\big(\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\})\big)
by means of an
iterative algorithm.

To introduce the algorithm, we further assume that the list of
polynomials
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}:=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})
satisfies:

({\sf H}_{3})

For
1\leq s\leq r
, the ideal
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s})
satisfies

({\sf H}_{1})
and
({\sf H}_{2})
.

Note that
({\sf H}_{3})
implies both
({\sf H}_{1})
and
({\sf H}_{2})
; that is, we assume that the list
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}:=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})
forms a
smooth regular sequence
, as in

Giusti et al. 1995
or
Pardo 1995
. This assumption is not overly
restrictive. As shown in
Krick and Pardo 1996
(see also

Jouanolou 1983
), given
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
, one
can iteratively obtain linear combinations
G_{1},\ldots,G_{r-1}
of

F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
such that the sequence
G_{1},\ldots,G_{r-1},F_{r}

satisfies
({\sf H}_{3})
. Nevertheless, we avoid such a construction
and simply assume from the outset that
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}
satisfies
({\sf H}_{3})
.

We introduce the following notations to express the iterative
structure of the algorithm.

•

For
1\leq s\leq r
, let
\boldsymbol{d}_{s}:=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{s})

be the truncated degree sequence.

•

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{s}}:=\prod_{i=1}^{s}\mathcal{P}_{d_{i}}
be
the
\overline{k}
-vector space of polynomial lists of length

s
.

•

For
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}:=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
,
denote
\boldsymbol{F}_{s}:=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s})
.

•

Let
V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{s}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{s}}
be the
corresponding solution variety.

•

Let
\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{s}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{s}}
be the nonempty
Zariski open set of lists satisfying hypothesis

({\sf H}_{3})
.

We can now formulate our goal in this context as follows:

Problem 4

(Solving a System Defining a Smooth Regular Sequence)
.

Given an algebraically closed field
\overline{k}
and an
r
-tuple

\boldsymbol{F}_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
of polynomials in
n
variables
of degrees bounded by
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}:=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})
, defining a
smooth regular sequence (i.e., satisfying hypothesis
({\sf H}_{3})
),
compute

\Pi_{2}\big(\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\big).

The approach proposed in
Giusti et al. 1995
and
Pardo 1995

rediscovered a forgotten idea originally introduced by L. Kronecker
in
Kronecker 1882
. This idea can be illustrated using the
following diagram, which represents a sequence of jumps between
solution varieties:

\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+F_{2}}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+F_{3}}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+F_{r}}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\cong V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}

Each jump
\curvearrowright
represents an “elimination”
step, where a new equation is added to the system. That is, at the

s
th step, we use the data computed for the variety
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})\cong\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
, and add the equation defined by

F_{s+1}
to solve
\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{s+1})
. This process is what
we called an “elimination step” of the algorithm.

To perform such a step, we must first define what we mean by a
“solution” of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})\cong\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
. Our
presentation differs slightly from those of
Kronecker 1882
,

Giusti et al. 1995
or
Pardo 1995
, though the underlying concepts
remain consistent.

Since each sequence
\boldsymbol{F}_{s}

(1\leq s\leq r)
satisfies hypothesis

({\sf H}_{3})
, the previous arguments show the existence of a
nonempty Zariski open set
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\subset GL(n,\overline{k})
such that for all
1\leq s\leq r
, and any

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
, we have an
integral ring extension

R_{s}:=\overline{k}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}]\hookrightarrow\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})]=\overline{k}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}]/(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s}).

(1.3)

We say that the matrix
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
puts the variables into
simultaneous Noether position
for all the sequences
\boldsymbol{F}_{s}

(1\leq s\leq r)
. A probabilistic method for computing such a

\boldsymbol{\lambda}
was introduced in
Krick and Pardo 1996
. In Section

3.1
, we present a refined version
with improved bounds.

Assuming such a
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is available, we present the classical
notion of solving of
Kronecker 1882
, and also
of
Giusti et al. 1995
and
Pardo 1995
.
Solving a smooth
complete intersection
like
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})

(1\leq s\leq r)
involves
computing:

({\sf S}_{1})

A linear change
of variables
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
such that:

–

The variables are in
Noether position with respect to
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
for all
1\leq s\leq r
.

–

The mapping
\Pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{s}:\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})\to H_{s}
, defined by
\Pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{s}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{F}_{s}):=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-s+1})
, is birational onto a hypersurface

H_{s}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-s+1}
, for all
1\leq s\leq r
:

({\sf S}_{2})

The minimal polynomial
M_{s}\in\overline{k}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}]

defining
H_{s}
.

({\sf S}_{3})

The rational inverse

\Phi_{s}:\mathfrak{O}_{s}\to\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})

of
\Pi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{s}
, defined over a
nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathfrak{O}_{s}\subset H_{s}
.

We show that there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset

\mathcal{U}^{*}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})

such that any
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{U}^{*}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
satisfies

({\sf S}_{1})
. Furthermore, for any

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{U}^{*}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
there exists:

•

A nonzero polynomial

\rho_{s}\in\overline{k}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}]
,

•

Polynomials

V_{n-s+2}^{s},\ldots,V_{n}^{s}\in\overline{k}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s},T]
,

such that

•

\mathfrak{O}_{s}:=\mathfrak{O}(\rho_{s}):=\{(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-s+1})\in H_{s}:\rho_{s}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-s})\not=0\}
is a distinguished
non-empty Zariski open subset, dense in
H_{s}
.

•

The inverse mapping
\Phi_{s}:\mathfrak{O}_{s}\to\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})

is given by:

\Phi_{s}(\boldsymbol{y})=\big(\boldsymbol{y},(\rho_{s}^{-1}\cdot V_{n-s+2}^{s})(\boldsymbol{y}),\ldots,(\rho_{s}^{-1}\cdot V_{n}^{s})(\boldsymbol{y}),\boldsymbol{F}_{s}\big).

The nonzero polynomial
\rho_{s}
is the
discriminant
of
M_{s}

with respect to
Y_{n-s+1}
.

This triple of data —
M_{s}
,
\rho_{s}
, and

V_{n-s+2}^{s},\ldots,V_{n}^{s}
— constitutes a
Kronecker
representation
(also called a
geometric solution
) of the
variety
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
. Given such a representation for
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})

and the polynomial
F_{s+1}
, via “elementary” linear algebra
computations in dimension
\deg(V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s}))\cdot d_{s+1}
, one can
compute a Kronecker representation of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s+1})
, completing
an elimination step, that jumps from fibers in
V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{s}}
to
fibers in
V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{s+1}}
.

This process forms the basis of the following iterative algorithm.

Algorithm 5

(Kronecker algorithm - first approach)
.

Input:
A list
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}:=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})\in\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
, such that
\boldsymbol{F}_{s}:=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s})\in\mathcal{U}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{s}}
for all
1\leq s\leq r
.

Output:
A Kronecker representation
of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
.

1
Set
s:=1
.

2
Compute a Kronecker representation
of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{1})
.

3
For
s=2,\ldots,r
, do

3.1
Use the representation of

V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
and the polynomial
F_{s+1}
,
to compute a Kronecker
representation of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s+1})
.

4
Return the Kronecker representation of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
.

This was the approach in
Kronecker 1882
, but it has a number of
drawbacks that we now discuss.

Remark 6
.

Let
K_{s}:=\overline{k}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})
. The “elementary”
linear algebra mentioned above involves a sequence of
tensor
matrices

M_{X_{n-s+1}},\ldots,M_{X_{n}}\in M_{D_{s}}(K_{s})
, associated
with the homothecies

\displaystyle\eta_{X_{k}}:=K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})]

\displaystyle\to K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})]

\displaystyle\overline{H}

\displaystyle\mapsto\overline{X_{k}H},

where
\delta_{s}:=\deg(V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s}))
, for
n-s+1\leq k\leq n
. The
“elimination step” consist in computing the minimal polynomial of
the matrix

M_{F_{s+1}}:=F_{s+1}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s},M_{X_{n-s+1}},\ldots,M_{X_{n}})\in M_{\delta_{s}}(K_{s}).

Remark 7
.

To carry out this “elementary” linear algebra computations, we
need the polynomials

•

\rho_{s}\in K_{s}
,

•

The minimal defining polynomial of
H_{s}
, and

•

V_{n-s+2}^{s},\ldots,V_{n}^{s}\in K_{s}[T]
,

all represented by their coefficients in
K_{s}
. This corresponds to

•

\mathcal{O}((s+1)\delta_{s})
polynomials of degree bounded by

\delta_{s}
;

•

One polynomial of degree
\delta_{s}^{2}
.

If these polynomials in
K_{s}
are written using dense encoding (i.e.,
as lists of all their coefficients in
\overline{k}
), the amount of
information becomes roughly

\mathcal{O}\left(s\cdot\binom{\delta_{s}^{2}+n-s}{n-s}\right)\cong\mathcal{O}\left(s\cdot\delta_{s}^{2(n-s)}\right),

which is impracticable, and leads to algorithms with arithmetic
complexity at least

\Omega(s\cdot d^{2s(n-s)}),

where
d:=\max\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{s}\}
. In the case
s=\theta(n)
, this yields
complexity greater than
d^{2n^{2}}
, which is unfeasible in practice.
This is the main reason why Kronecker’s original approach was
ultimately abandoned—it is computationally intractable, even on
modern hardware.

Remark 8
.

To address the issue discussed in Remark
7
, the proposal in
Giusti et al. 1995
and
Pardo 1995

was to avoid dense representations, encoding instead polynomials in

K_{s}:=\overline{k}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}]
using
division-free nonscalar straight-line
programs
. In modern terms, a division-free nonscalar straight-line program can be seen as a

neural network
over
\overline{k}
with activation function

\varphi(T):=T^{2}
(see
Pardo and Sebastián 2026
). This representation was not new—it had already
been used in
Fitchas et al. 1995
or
Krick and Pardo 1996
for instance. In

Krick and Pardo 1996
a detailed discussion of such a
data structure
was
provided.

Remark 9
.

While the use of straight-line programs allows a more efficient
implementation of the elimination step, it is still not sufficient
to make the Kronecker algorithm efficient overall. Assume that the
coefficients of the Kronecker representation of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
in

R_{s}
are given by a straight-line program of size
L_{s}
. Then, in a
number of arithmetic operations polynomial in

L_{s},n,d_{s+1},\delta_{s},

we can compute the
\mathcal{O}((s+1)\delta_{s+1})
coefficients of the
polynomials that form a Kronecker representation of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s+1})
, encoded as a straight-line program of size
L_{s+1}
.
Unfortunately, the size of the resulting straight-line programs
grows multiplicatively, i.e.,

L_{s+1}\cong(d_{s+1}\delta_{s})^{\mathcal{O}(1)}L_{s}.

This phenomenon was called
nesting of straight-line programs

in
Pardo 1995
. In the zero-dimensional case (i.e.,
s=n
), such
an approach leads to an algorithm with complexity

d^{\mathcal{O}(n^{2})}
, where
d:=\max\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\}
, which is
worse (in terms of the number of arithmetic operations) than those
in
Fitchas et al. 1995
or
Krick and Pardo 1996
, which work in time

(nd)^{\mathcal{O}(n)}
.

In conclusion, the approach based on jumping between solution
varieties has critical shortcomings:

•

Kronecker’s naive method is too costly computationally,

•

Using straight-line
programs simplifies individual steps but leads to exponential
blow-up due to nesting,

•

As a result, the total complexity remains

d^{\mathcal{O}(n^{2})}
,
worse than competing algorithms with complexity
(nd)^{\mathcal{O}(n)}
.

In
Pardo 1995
or
Giusti et al. 1995
, a first idea on how to break
this nesting was suggested: use a
Newton-Hensel lifting
in the
non-Archimedean case. This was made precise in

Giusti et al. 1997a
,
Giusti et al. 1997b
and
Giusti et al. 1998
. We
discuss this idea in the next section.

1.1.4
Second approach: deformations through zero-dimensional
smooth varieties

The problem with the previous approach—jumping between solution
varieties by successively adding equations—is not only its high
complexity but its
somewhat unnatural
nature. In

Giusti et al. 1997a
,
Giusti et al. 1998
and
Giusti et al. 1997b
, a
new technique was introduced that not only breaks the nesting
phenomenon but also transforms Algorithm
5
into one that
deforms the input polynomial
system within the solution variety of zero-dimensional varieties
.

To explain how it works, we continue with notations and assumptions
from the previous sections. Let
r,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
r\leq n
, and let

\boldsymbol{d}_{r}:=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})
be a degree sequence. Let
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}:=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
be a system that
defines a smooth complete intersection, namely, it satisfies
hypothesis
({\sf H}_{3})
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{\widetilde{U}}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
be a linear
change of coordinates such that the new variables
\boldsymbol{Y}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
ensure that the ring extension
(
1.3
) is integral for all

1\leq s\leq r
. We now search for a point
\boldsymbol{p}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1})\in\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
and define the following
sections of the varieties associated with the input systems
\boldsymbol{F}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{F}_{r}
under the change of coordinates

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{\widetilde{U}}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
:

\begin{array}[]{l}\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{1}:=(Y_{1}-p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-1}-p_{n-1},F_{1})\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}},\\[4.30554pt]
\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{2}:=(Y_{1}-p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-2}-p_{n-2},F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\\
\qquad\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r}:=(Y_{1}-p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-r}-p_{n-r},F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}},\end{array}

(1.4)

where
{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}:=(1,\ldots,1,d_{1},\ldots,d_{s})
is the
combined degree sequence. We have
r
linear sections of the input
varieties:

V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s}):=V(Y_{1}-p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}-p_{n-s})\cap V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})\quad(1\leq s\leq r),

which define
r
jumps to finally compute a Kronecker representation of
the final section
V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r})
.

Under the given assumptions, it is easy to see that there is a
nonempty Zariski open set
\mathfrak{O}:=\mathfrak{O}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r},\boldsymbol{\lambda})\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
such that the following hold for

1\leq s\leq r
:

({\sf L}_{1})

V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s})
is a smooth zero-dimensional variety;

({\sf L}_{2})

\#V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s})=\deg V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s})=\mathrm{rank}_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})]
.

We will show that such a point
\boldsymbol{p}
can be found by a
probabilistic algorithm, which selects
\boldsymbol{p}
randomly in a
distinguished Zariski open set
\mathfrak{D}(H):=\{\boldsymbol{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-1}:H(\boldsymbol{p})\not=0\}\subset\mathfrak{O}
, with sharp
degree bounds on
H
.

It is worth noting that the solution variety
V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
has the
property that a generic fiber
\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G})
is a smooth
zero-dimensional variety (outside the discriminant variety

\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
, or equivalently, for systems in
the open set
\mathcal{U}({\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}})
). This
corresponds to the claim of
({\sf L}_{1})
–
({\sf L}_{2})
.

The sequence of varieties
V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{1}),\ldots,V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r})
can
be viewed as a homotopic deformation within the solution variety

V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
. We consider any curve
\{\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{(t)}\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}:t\in[0,1]\}
such
that the following conditions hold:

\begin{array}[]{l}\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{(t_{0})}:=G_{r}^{1}:=(Y_{1}-p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-1}-p_{n-1},F_{1}),\\
\qquad\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{(t_{s-1})}:=G_{r}^{s}:=(Y_{1}-p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}-p_{n-s},\boldsymbol{F}_{s})\textrm{ for }1\leq s\leq r-2,\\
\qquad\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{(t_{r-1})}:=\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r}:=(Y_{1}-p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-r}-p_{n-r},\boldsymbol{F}_{r}),\end{array}

with
t_{0}:=0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{r-2}<t_{r-1}:=1
. The curve may be chosen
as polygonal chain. We then have a sequence of zero-dimensional
varieties:

\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{(t_{0})})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{(t_{1})})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{(1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}

Using the parameter
t\in[0,1]
, this becomes a continuous path in
the solution variety
V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
:

\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}}
\textstyle{\{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{(t)}):t\in[0,1]\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}

The question becomes how to follow this path through a sequence of
iterations. In numerical settings, this is typically done using
Newton iterations (see
Shub and Smale 1993
or
Beltrán and Pardo 2009
,
Beltrán and Pardo 2011

and the references therein). In our symbolic setting, we replace the
Newton method with a
non-Archimedean Newton-Hensel (NH) lifting
method
. In this vein, the sequence of varieties
V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{1}),\ldots,V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r})
are called
lifting fibers
in

Giusti et al. 1997a
.

Under the assumptions
({\sf H}_{3})
,
({\sf L}_{1})
and
({\sf L}_{2})
,
this lifting method works reliably. The process is visualized in the
following diagram:

\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{2}}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
Intersect
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{3}}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Intersect
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{r}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\cong V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{2}}
NH lifting
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate
}F_{3}}
NH lifting
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{r}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
NH lifting
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supseteq}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supseteq}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supseteq}

The Newton-Hensel lifting is detailed in Section
4.2
. In this diagram, the elimination step from
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})\cong\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
to
\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s+1})
is
done by evaluating the variables
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s-1}
in the
polynomials that form the Kronecker representation of

\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
, and the polynomial
F_{s+1}
, at

p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s-1}
, and performing the intersection step in
dimension one.

This approach has significant advantages:

•

It breaks the
nesting
of straight-line programs from
the naive approach: at each step, coefficients become constants
in
\overline{k}
since the lifting fibers are zero-dimensional.

•

The total complexity of the procedure is polynomial
in:

n,d,\delta,L,

where

–

\delta:=\max\{\deg V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s}):1\leq s\leq r\}\leq\prod_{s=1}^{r}d_{s}
,

–

L
is the complexity of evaluating the input polynomials
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}
.

Hence the overall complexity becomes
L(nd\delta)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
,
which represents a substantial improvement over the naive Kronecker
approach. Many refinements of the bounds and algorithms appear in
the literature (see, e.g.,
Giusti et al. 2001
,
Heintz et al. 2001
,

Lecerf 2003
,
Cafure and Matera 2006
,
Durvye and Lecerf 2008
,
van der Hoeven and Lecerf 2021
).

We note that in
Giusti et al. 1997a
,
Giusti et al. 1998
, and

Giusti et al. 1997b
, the point
\boldsymbol{p}
is computed iteratively. A
probabilistic process for choosing a
single
point
\boldsymbol{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-1}

such that all
V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{i})
satisfy
({\sf L}_{1})
and
({\sf L}_{2})
was first provided in
Heintz et al. 2001
(see also

Cafure and Matera 2006
and
Giménez and Matera 2019
). We present improved bounds and
algorithms in Section
3.2
.

There is, however, a drawback: the elimination step still jumps
between solution varieties, which is somewhat unnatural, as in the
naive approach. Additionally, the algorithm encodes multivariate
polynomials by straight-line programs, and not many readers like
this encoding. This was independently solved in
Giusti et al. 2001
and

Heintz et al. 2001
, introducing intermediate curves and avoiding the
full jump between higher-dimensional varieties.

1.1.5
Third approach: homotopic deformations by
smooth curves

In
Giusti et al. 2001
and
Heintz et al. 2001
, a new approach yields a
three level solution: it benefits from the existence of lifting
fibers, but these authors observed that performing the elimination
step to compute the next lifting fiber does not require
complete access to the full intermediate variety;
an
intermediate curve suffices
. These are the
lifting curves

that we now introduce.

With the notations and assumptions from the previous sections, given

r,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
r\leq n
and a degree sequence
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}:=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})
, we fix an
r
-tuple
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}:=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
which defines a
smooth complete intersection, namely, which satisfies hypothesis

({\sf H}_{3})
. Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{\widetilde{U}}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})

be a linear change of coordinates such that the new variables
\boldsymbol{Y}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
ensure that the ring extension
(
1.3
) is integral for
1\leq s\leq r
. Let
\boldsymbol{p}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1})\in\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
be a point
such that
V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{1}),\ldots,V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r})
are lifting fibers of

V(\boldsymbol{F}_{1}),\ldots,V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
, where
\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r}
are defined as in (
1.4
), and thus
satisfy assumptions
({\sf H}_{3})
,
({\sf L}_{1})
and
({\sf L}_{2})
.

Associated with the degree sequence
\boldsymbol{d}_{r}
, we introduce a new
degree sequence of length
n-1
in the following way:

(\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}):=(\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{n-r-1},d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}.

We also consider the solution variety
V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}

associated with this degree sequence. Further, we consider the
following sequence of
n-1
polynomials of
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
:

\begin{array}[]{l}\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{1}:=(Y_{1}-p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-2}-p_{n-2},F_{1}),\\[4.30554pt]
\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{2}:=(Y_{1}-p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-3}-p_{n-3},\boldsymbol{F}_{2}),\\
\qquad\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{r}:=(Y_{1}-p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-r-1}-p_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{F}_{r}).\end{array}

We denote
C_{s}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r}):=V(\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{s})\cong\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{s})
, where
\Pi_{1}:V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\to\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
is the canonical projection.

One readily observes that
C_{s}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
is a section of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
and, hence, its degree is bounded by
\deg V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
for

1\leq s\leq r
. On the other hand,
C_{s}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
contains a lifting
fiber
V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s})
, and therefore

\deg C_{s}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})=\deg V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s})=\deg V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})

(see Section
4.1
for details).
Moreover, as
V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s})
contains
enough information to recover
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
through a Newton-Hensel
lifting, it also contains enough information to obtain a Kronecker
representation of
C_{s}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
, also using a Newton-Hensel
lifting. The variety
C_{s}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
has pure dimension 1—namely
it is a curve—and
is therefore called a
lifting curve
. More importantly, the
lifting curve
C_{s}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
not only contains information to recover
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
, but also to perform an elimination step–not for recovering

V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s+1})
but for computing the next lifting fiber
V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s+1})
.

We also have a homotopic deformation in
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}
whose intermediate steps are the systems

H_{r}^{1},H_{r}^{2},\ldots,H_{r}^{r}
,

\{\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{(t)}\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}:t\in[0,1]\}.

Thus the algorithm takes the form shown in the following diagram:

\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{2}}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{3}}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{r}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\cong V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{2}}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
Intersect
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{3}}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Intersect
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{r}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{2}}
NH lifting
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate
}F_{3}}
NH lifting
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{r}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
NH lifting
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supseteq}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supseteq}
\textstyle{\quad\cdots\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supseteq}

The dotted arrows indicate that we could lift the information
contained in
V(\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{s})
to compute a Kronecker representation of the
corresponding variety
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})
. The point here is that we do
not do it, because we do not need to do it in order to compute the
next lifting fiber.

The final output of the algorithm is the lifting fiber
V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r})
given by a Kronecker representation (or by the van der
Waerden
U
-resultant), where coefficients are represented by
straight-line programs. If someone wants to compute a Kronecker
representation of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
, then a Newton-Hensel lifting from
V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r})
would allow obtaining such data.

Note that this also means that the Newton-Hensel lifting, plus the
knowledge of the
U
-resultant of
V(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r})
, yields a sharp
approximation of the
U
-resultant of
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
with respect to
the standard non-Archimedean metric in the local ring
\overline{k}[\![Y_{1}-p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-r}-p_{n-r}]\!]
. This approximation is a
nonscalar straight-line program with divisions of size
\mathcal{O}(\deg V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r}))
and depth

\log_{2}\log_{2}(\deg V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r}))
.

In conclusion, the final approach consists in following a
polygonal chain of homotopic deformations of smooth complete intersection
varieties of dimension 0 or 1, which is shown in the following
diagram that represents a single step in both homotopy paths:

\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r-1},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Intersect
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate
}F_{s}}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{H}_{r}^{s+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Eliminate }F_{s}}
NH lifting
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
NH lifting
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supseteq}
\textstyle{V_{\boldsymbol{1}_{n-r},\boldsymbol{d}_{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supseteq}

(1.5)

To establish the correctness of all these steps and the selection of
the coordinates of the point
\boldsymbol{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
, and the matrix

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{A}^{n\times n}
such that the linear change of
variables
\boldsymbol{Y}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
defines a simultaneous
Noether normalization of all the varieties
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})

(1\leq s\leq r)
, requires a detailed analysis. We have chosen to perform this
analysis with the most advanced techniques to exhibit the sharpest
bounds in the study with the most up-to-date methods.

1.2
Main results and outline of the paper

In what follows, we will consider an algorithmic problem that
slightly generalizes the one discussed in the previous
sections. We now describe it in detail.

Let
k
be a perfect field
1
1

1

As a referee suggested, it might be possible to replace the
assumption that the base field
k
is perfect by a weaker condition
requiring that the Jacobian determinants of all intermediate systems
intersect their zero sets properly, so that the systems define
radical ideals over
\overline{k}
. While this formulation could
indeed generalize our setting, it is considerably more technical and
less natural from an algebraic-geometric viewpoint. Moreover, it is
not clear that the degree bounds established in this paper for the
hypersurfaces governing the genericity conditions (both on the
linear change of variables and on the choice of lifting points)
would remain valid under that generalization. The assumption
that
k
is perfect provides a unified and simpler framework
encompassing the main cases treated in the literature (
k=\mathbb{Q}
and
finite fields), and allows the relevant nonvanishing discriminant
conditions to be expressed in a clean and intrinsic way, avoiding
inseparable phenomena. Finally, some subroutines of the algorithm
require extracting
p
-th roots, an operation that is naturally well
defined and computationally manageable
when
k
is perfect.
, and let
\overline{k}
be its algebraic closure. Given positive
integers
r,n
with
r\leq n
, let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be indeterminates
over
k
, and consider polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
satisfying the following conditions:

({\sf H}_{1})

The polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
form a regular sequence in the
localization
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G}
,

({\sf H}_{2})

For all
1\leq s\leq r
, the ideal
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s})_{G}
is radical in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G}
.

We will be interested in the set of common zeros
V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}

outside the hypersurface

\{G=0\}
. An example arises when
G
is the determinant of an
(r\times r)
-minor of the Jacobian matrix
J_{r}
of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
: in such
a case, our goal is to compute the set of common zeros of

F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
that are regular with respect to certain linear projection.

More precisely, if
V
denotes the Zariski closure of

V(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})\setminus\{G=0\},

our objective is to design and analyze a Kronecker-type algorithm
for computing a
Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber of
V
.
This algorithm follows the methodology introduced in Section
1.1.5
: it proceeds via a sequence of jumps between
one-dimensional and zero-dimensional sections of the intermediate
varieties defined as

V_{s}:=\overline{V(\boldsymbol{F}_{s})\setminus\{G=0\}}:=\overline{\{F_{1}=0,\ldots,F_{s}=0,G\not=0\}}\quad(1\leq s\leq r),

(1.6)

where
\overline{W}
denotes the Zariski closure of a
locally closed set
W\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
. Since the input polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G
are defined
over
k
, there exist Kronecker representations of
lifting fibers of
V:=V_{r}
defined over
k
, except for the case when
k
is a finite
field of small cardinality. Accordingly, we will compute a
Kronecker representation defined over
k
,
unless
k
is a finite field of small cardinality—in
which case we will work over a suitable finite extension of
k
with sufficiently
large cardinality.

We assume that the polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G
are given by
a straight-line program in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
. The cost of the
algorithm will be expressed in terms of the number of arithmetic
operations in
k
, along with two additional operations: identity tests and
extractions of
p
-th roots (the latter only relevant when

\mathrm{char}(k)=p>0
).

We will then specialize to two important cases: when
k=\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
, a finite field,
and when
k=\mathbb{Q}
, the field of rational
numbers. In both cases, we express the cost of the algorithm
in terms of
bit complexity
. However, neither case allows a trivial
adaptation: for
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
, one must determine the minimal value of
q
for
which the algorithm succeeds directly over the base field;
for
k=\mathbb{Q}
, we analyze the growth of intermediate results and
find a prime
p
such that the algorithm succeeds modulo
p
,
allowing a
p
-adic lifting step to recover a
Kronecker representation defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Now we summarize the structure of the paper. Section
3
addresses the problem of computing a change of
coordinates
\boldsymbol{Y}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
such that the new
variables are in a simultaneous Noether position with respect to all
the varieties
V_{s}
defined in (
1.6
), for
1\leq s\leq r
. We also seek a
single point

\boldsymbol{p}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1})\in\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
such that the associated
lifting fibers (
1.4
) satisfy properties
({\sf L}_{1})
–
({\sf L}_{2})
of Section
1.1.4
.

An additional subtlety arises when choosing the point
\boldsymbol{p}
: as
shown in Diagram
1.5
, the algorithm
involves computing a Kronecker representation of the
(s+1)
th
lifting fiber
\Pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s+1})
, via the intersection of
the
s
th lifting curve
C_{s}
with the hypersurface defined by

F_{s+1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},Y_{n-s},\ldots,Y_{n})
, where
\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s-1})
. As discussed in Section

1.1.3
, the Kronecker representation of
C_{s}

is only valid in a
nonempty Zariski open dense subset
of

C_{s}
, namely, where the discriminant
\rho_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},Y_{n-s})

does not vanish.

Thus, it is essential that the hypersurface
\{F_{s+1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},Y_{n-s},\ldots,Y_{n})=0\}

intersects properly the
discriminant locus

\{\rho_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},Y_{n-s})=0\}
within

C_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq r-1
. We have the following result (see Theorem

23
and Corollary
24
for precise statements).

Theorem 10
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}:=(\Lambda_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
be a matrix of
indeterminates over
\overline{k}
, and let

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
. There exists a
nonzero polynomial
B\in\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-1}]
, of degree at most

2n^{2}rd\delta^{3}
, where
\delta:=\max\{\deg V_{1},\ldots,\deg V_{r}\}
and

d:=\max\{\deg F_{1},\ldots,\deg F_{r},\deg G\}
, such
that for any
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathbb{A}^{n^{2}}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}

with
B(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\not=0
, the following conditions are
satisfied:

1.

The variables
\boldsymbol{Y}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
are in Noether position with
respect to
V_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq r
;

2.

The lifting fiber of
V_{s}
defined by
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})

satisfies conditions
({\sf L}_{1})
–
({\sf L}_{2})
for
1\leq i\leq s
;

3.

The hypersurface
\{F_{s+1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},Y_{n-s},\ldots,Y_{n})=0\}

intersects properly the discriminant locus
\{\rho_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},Y_{n-s})=0\}

within
C_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq r-1
.

Similar results are proved in
(
Heintz et al. 2001
, Theorem 3)
, for a
variety
V
defined over the rationals
\mathbb{Q}
, and in

(
Cafure and Matera 2006
, Theorem 3.3)
, for a variety
V
defined over a
finite field
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
. The new result contained in Theorem
23

improves both results in two important aspects. The first
one is its generality, as Theorem
23
holds for varieties
defined over a general perfect field
k
. The second aspect
is that we obtain a significant improvement of the degree
estimates of
(
Heintz et al. 2001
, Theorem 3)
and
(
Cafure and Matera 2006
, Theorem 3.3)
.
This is critical in the case when
k
is a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
,
because it determines the smallest size
q
such that the
algorithm does not require working on a finite field extension
of
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
(see Algorithm
61
). When one is interested in computing
a point
with coordinates in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
on the variety under
consideration, it is essential that the resulting Kronecker representation
of the algorithm is defined over
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
(see
Cafure and Matera 2006
and

Giménez et al. 2023
).

In view of this result, given a parameter
\varepsilon>0
, the first step of the algorithm
is to choose the entries
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
\boldsymbol{p}
randomly from a set
\mathcal{S}\subset k
of size
at least
\varepsilon^{-1}2n^{2}rd\delta
, or from a finite field extension
of sufficiently large cardinality, if the base field
k
does not contain
enough points. The well-known
Zippel-Schwartz lemma (see, e.g.,
(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Lemma 6.44)
) guarantees
that the probability of success of this random choice is at least
1-\varepsilon
.

Section
4
studies the
lifting curves

C_{s}

(1\leq s\leq r-1)
, which appear as intermediate objects in our algorithm,
and the procedure used to compute a Kronecker representation of a
lifting curve from a lifting fiber—namely, the Newton-Hensel lifting (the
arrow “NH lifting”
in Diagram
1.5
). This version of the
Newton-Hensel process is based on the approach introduced in
Giusti et al. 2001
. In
Proposition
33
, we provide
a detailed analysis of the correctness of the Newton-Hensel lifting,
and Theorem
35
establishes the
validity of the procedure
for computing a Kronecker representation of a lifting curve
C_{s}
.

Section
5
is devoted to the other
critical component of the algorithm:
the “intersection step”. This procedure takes as input a Kronecker representation
of a lifting curve
C_{s}
and a straight-line program for
F_{s+1}
,
and outputs a Kronecker representation of the
(s+1)
-th lifting fiber

\Pi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{s+1})
(as shown by the “Intersect” arrow
in Diagram
1.5
). A suitable choice
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
\boldsymbol{p}
, in accordance with Theorem
10
,
greatly simplifies this
step by ensuring that “spurious” points can be easily detected and
removed (see Proposition
39

and Algorithm
41
).

By combining the Newton-Hensel lifting and the intersection step,
Section
6
presents a full procedure for computing a
Kronecker representation of the final lifting fiber
\Pi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{r})
of the
input variety
\Pi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{F}_{r})
. More precisely, we obtain
the following result (see Theorem
60
for the precise statement).

Theorem 11
.

Let
k
be a perfect field, and let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
be polynomials satisfying
({\sf H}_{1})
–
({\sf H}_{2})
, given by a straight-line program of length
L
. For
1\leq s\leq r
, let
V_{s}:=\overline{V(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s})\setminus V(G)}
and
define
\delta_{s}:=\deg V_{s}
. Let
\delta:=\max\{\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}\}
and
d:=\max\{\deg F_{1},\ldots,\deg F_{r},\deg G\}
. Assume
that
\delta>d
. Then a Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber
of
V_{r}
can be computed by a probabilistic algorithm using

\mathcal{O}\,\widetilde{\ }\big(n^{4}+r(L+n^{2}+r^{4})\,d\,\delta^{2}\big)

arithmetic operations in
k
,

\mathcal{O}\,\widetilde{\ }\big((rL+rn^{2}+r^{2})\,d\,\delta^{2}\big)

identity tests in
k
, and
\mathcal{O}(r\delta)

extractions of
p
-th roots in
k
(if
\mathrm{char}(k)=p>0
),
where the soft-Oh notation
\mathcal{O}\,\widetilde{\ }
ignores
logarithmic factors.

This algorithm generalizes those of
Heintz et al. 2001
(for
k=\mathbb{Q}
),

Cafure and Matera 2006
(for
k=\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
, a finite field) and
Giusti et al. 2001
,
Durvye and Lecerf 2008

(for characteristic 0). This comes at the cost of relying
on operations such as identity tests and extractions of
p
-th roots in
k
,
whose cost must be specified for the field
k
under consideration.

Given
a real number
\varepsilon
with
0<\varepsilon<1/(16r)
, the algorithm succeeds
with probability at least

1-8r\varepsilon
(see Theorem
59
). Moreover,
if
k
has cardinality at least
N:=\varepsilon^{-1}2n^{2}rd\delta^{2}
, the
output is guaranteed to lie on
k
; otherwise, it lies in
a finite field extension of
k
of cardinality at least
N
.

We then examine two classical settings in
symbolic computation: when
k
is a finite field and when

k=\mathbb{Q}
. In both, we analyze the bit
complexity of the algorithm. For a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
with

q>\varepsilon^{-1}2n^{2}rd\delta^{2}
, Theorem
10

ensures that a Kronecker representation of a lifting
fiber of
V_{r}
exists over
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
. We provide a probabilistic
algorithm that computes such a Kronecker representation (see Theorem
62
for a precise statement).

Theorem 12
.

Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
be polynomials of degree at
most
d>0
, satisfying hypotheses
({\sf H}_{1})
–
({\sf H}_{2})
,
given by a straight–line program in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
of length

L
. For
1\leq s\leq r
, let

V_{s}:=\overline{V(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s})\setminus V(G)}
and

\delta_{s}:=\deg V_{s}
. Let

\delta:=\max\{\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}\}
, and assume that

\delta>d
. Given
0<\varepsilon<1/4
, if

q>2\varepsilon^{-1}n^{2}rd\delta^{3}
, then a Kronecker representation
of a lifting fiber of
V_{r}
defined over
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
can be computed with
bit complexity in

\mathcal{O}\,\widetilde{\ }\big((n^{4}+r(L+n^{2}+r^{4}))\,d\,\delta^{2}\log_{2}q\big)

and probability of success at least
1-2\varepsilon
.

We observe that this is not a straight-forward application of the algorithm in
Theorem
11
to the case
k=\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
. To minimize requirements
on
q
, the algorithm incorporates steps over algebraic extensions of
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
,
ensuring high probability of success while keeping
the final output in the base field. This significantly
improves the bounds in
(
Cafure and Matera 2006
, Theorem 4.8)
,
which requires
q>5\,\varepsilon^{-1}n^{4}d\,\delta^{4}
, and also offers better
bit complexity.
We finally mention that the Kronecker method can also be adapted to the case
of dense polynomial systems over fields that allow fast multipoint evaluation
of multivariate polynomials. This situation has been analyzed in detail by
van der Hoeven and Lecerf
van der Hoeven and Lecerf 2021
in the setting of finite fields.
Nevertheless, their results concern a computational model and problem setting
different from ours, and thus do not overlap with the present contribution.

In Section
7
we address the case
k=\mathbb{Q}
. A direct application of
Theorem
11
may result in exponential growth
of the bit size of intermediate results. To avoid
this, the modular strategy of
Giménez and Matera 2019
is adopted: the algorithm
is executed modulo a randomly chosen prime number
p
, followed
by
p
-adic lifting (originally introduced in
Giusti et al. 2001
).
If
p
is “lucky”, the
p
-adic lifting yields a Kronecker
representation of a lifting fiber over
\mathbb{Q}
of the input variety.
The selection of
p
relies on a combination of the techniques in

Giménez and Matera 2019
and the refined
bounds of Theorem
10
. The result is the following
(see Theorem
80
for a precise statement).

Theorem 13
.

Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
be polynomials of degree at
most
d>0
and coefficients of bit length at most
h
satisfying
hypotheses
({\sf H}_{1})
-
({\sf H}_{2})
, which are given by a
straight–line program in
\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
of length
L
and
parameters of bit length at most
h
. For
1\leq s\leq r
, let

V_{s}:=\overline{V(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s})\setminus V(G)}
and

\delta_{s}:=\deg V_{s}
. Let

\delta:=\max\{\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{s}\}
, and assume that

\delta>d
. Let
0<\varepsilon<1/18r
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
be
given. Then a Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber of
V_{r}

can be computed with bit complexity

\mathcal{O}\,\widetilde{\ }\Big(n(L+n^{4})\,d\,\delta^{2}\log_{2}(\varepsilon^{-1})+n^{2}d^{r}L\,\delta_{r}\big(h+\log_{2}(\varepsilon^{-1})\big)+\log_{2}^{3}(\varepsilon^{-1})\Big),

and probability of success at least
1-9r\varepsilon
.

The sharpness of the probability estimates established for the
algorithms in Theorems
11
,
12
, and
13
remains unclear.
The random parameters chosen in these algorithms are required to
avoid certain algebraic hypersurfaces whose existence is proved and
whose degrees are explicitly bounded. These hypersurfaces are,
however, sufficiently intricate to make it difficult to identify
families of polynomial systems for which the bounds would be tight,
or to derive meaningful lower bounds for the success probabilities.
Regarding complexity, our algorithms have cost quadratic in the
maximum degree
\delta
of the intermediate varieties. Within the
equation-by-equation framework of Kronecker-type methods,
subquadratic dependence on
\delta
appears unlikely in the general
case, given the inherently dense nature of the computations
involving lifting curves and the structure of the lifting process.
Achieving algorithms whose complexity depends instead of the degree
of the output variety, rather than on
\delta
, would represent a
substantially more difficult challenge. Finally, we note that,
although checking whether the structural hypotheses
({\sf H}_{1})
-
({\sf H}_{2})
hold for a given input system is in general
computationally prohibitive, the algorithms may still be applied to
arbitrary inputs and are expected to fail gracefully when these
assumptions are not satisfied. Moreover, it remains computationally
feasible to verify a posteriori that the obtained output indeed
corresponds to solutions of the input system, thus ensuring
practical reliability even when the hypotheses are not explicitly
checked.

2
Notions, notations and preliminary results

We use standard notions and notations of commutative algebra and
algebraic geometry, as found, for example, in
Harris 1992
,

Kunz 1985
, and
Shafarevich 1994
.

Let
k
be a perfect field and
\overline{k}
its algebraic closure. We
denote by
\mathbb{A}^{n}
the
n
–dimensional affine space
\overline{k}{}^{n}
. An
affine variety of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
defined over

k
(or an affine

k
–variety) is the set of common zeros in
\mathbb{A}^{n}
of polynomials

F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
. We will often write
V(F_{1},\ldots,F_{m})
or
\{F_{1}=0,\ldots,F_{m}=0\}
to denote this affine
k
–variety. For a polynomial

G\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, we denote by
\{G\not=0\}
the complement in
\mathbb{A}^{n}
of the
hypersurface
\{G=0\}
.

A
k
–variety
V
is said to be
k
–
irreducible
if it cannot be
expressed as a finite union of proper
k
–subvarieties of
V
. Any

k
–variety
V
admits a unique (up to reordering) irredundant
decomposition
V=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{C}_{s}
, where each

\mathcal{C}_{i}
is
k
–irreducible. These are
called the
irreducible

k
–
components
of
V
.

For a
k
–variety
V
of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
, we denote by
I(V)
its
defining ideal
, namely, the set of all polynomials in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
that vanish on
V
. The
coordinate ring

k[V]
of
V
is
defined as
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]/I(V)
. When
V
is irreducible, then

k[V]
is a domain, and its field of fractions

k(V)
is called the
field of rational functions
of
V
. The

dimension

\dim V
of
V
is the maximal length
r
of
chains
V_{0}\varsubsetneq V_{1}\varsubsetneq\cdots\varsubsetneq V_{r}

of nonempty irreducible
k
–varieties contained in
V
. We say that

V
has
pure dimension

r
if all its irreducible

k
–components are of dimension
r
.

The
degree

\deg V
of an irreducible
k
–variety
V
is the
maximum number of points in the intersection
V\cap L
, where

L
is a linear space of codimension
\dim V
such that
V\cap L
is a
finite set. More generally, following
Heintz 1983
(see also

Fulton 1984
), if
V=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{C}_{s}

is the decomposition of
V
into irreducible
k
–components, we
define the degree of
V
as

\deg V:=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\deg\mathcal{C}_{i}.

We will frequently use the following
Bézout inequality
(see

Heintz 1983
,
Fulton 1984
,
Vogel 1984
): if
V
and
W

are
k
–varieties of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
, then

\deg(V\cap W)\leq\deg V\cdot\deg W.

(2.1)

Let
V\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
be a
k
–variety with defining ideal
I(V)\subset k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
. For a point
\boldsymbol{x}\in V
, the
dimension

\dim_{\boldsymbol{x}}V

of

V

at

\boldsymbol{x}
is the maximum
dimension of the irreducible
k
–components of
V
containing

\boldsymbol{x}
. If
I(V)=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{m})
, the
tangent space

T_{\boldsymbol{x}}V

to
V
at
\boldsymbol{x}
is the kernel of the Jacobian matrix
(\partial F_{i}/\partial X_{j})_{1\leq i\leq m,1\leq j\leq n}(\boldsymbol{x})
of

F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}
with respect to
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
evaluated
at
\boldsymbol{x}
. The point
\boldsymbol{x}
is
regular
if
\dim T_{\boldsymbol{x}}V=\dim_{\boldsymbol{x}}V
; otherwise,
it is called
singular
. The set of singular points
of
V
is the
singular locus

\mathrm{Sing}(V)
of
V
; a
variety is called
nonsingular
if its singular locus is empty.

Let
V
and
W
be irreducible affine
k
–varieties of the same
dimension, and let
f:V\to W
be a regular map for which

\overline{f(V)}=W
, where
\overline{f(V)}
is the closure of

f(V)
with respect to the Zariski topology of
W
. Such a map is
called
dominant
. Then
f
induces a ring extension

k[W]\hookrightarrow k[V]
by composition with
f
. We say that the
dominant map
f
is a
finite morphism
if this extension is
integral, i.e., each element
\eta\in k[V]
satisfies a monic
equation with coefficients in
k[W]
. For
V
pure–dimensional and

W
irreducible, a regular map
f:V\to W
as above is called
finite
if its restriction to each irreducible component of
V
is a
finite morphism. A basic fact is that any finite morphism is
necessarily closed. We recall the following well–known
fact.

Fact 14

(
(
Danilov 1994
, §4.2, Proposition)
)
.

Let
f:V\to W
be a finite
morphism. Then for every irreducible closed subset
S\subset W
,
the preimage
f^{-1}(S)
is of pure dimension
\dim S
.

Let
V
and
W
be irreducible affine
k
–varieties of the same
dimension and
f:V\to W
a dominant map. Then
f
induces a finite
field extension
k(W)\hookrightarrow k(V)
. The degree of this field
extension is the
degree of the morphism

f
, denoted
\deg f
.

If the field extension
k(W)\hookrightarrow k(V)
is
separable, then by
(
Heintz 1983
, Proposition 1)
, the number of
points in the fiber
f^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y})
is at most
\deg f
for any
\boldsymbol{y}\in W
with a finite
fiber, with equality holding in a Zariski open subset of
W
. For
\boldsymbol{x}\in f^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y})
,
f
is
unramified
at
\boldsymbol{x}
if the
differential mapping
d_{\boldsymbol{x}}\pi\colon T_{\boldsymbol{x}}V\to T_{\boldsymbol{y}}W
is injective. The fiber
f^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y})
is unramified
if this holds for all
\boldsymbol{x}\in f^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y})
. The map
f

is
generically unramified
if there exists a dense Zariski
open subset
U
of
W
such that the fiber
f^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y})
is
unramified for any
\boldsymbol{y}\in U
.

An affine
k
–variety
V:=V(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{n}

defined by
r\leq n
polynomials is a
set-theoretic complete
intersection
if it has pure
dimension
n-r
. If, in addition, the ideal
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})
is
radical, then
V
is an

ideal-theoretic complete intersection
. The polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
form a
regular sequence
if the ideal

(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})
they generate in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
is proper,

F_{1}
is nonzero and, for
2\leq i\leq r
,
F_{i}
is neither zero nor
a zero divisor in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]/(F_{1},\ldots,F_{i-1})
.

2.1
Kronecker representations

Let
V\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
be a
k
–variety of pure dimension
n-s
, and
let
I\subset k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
be its vanishing ideal. For a change
of variables
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\to(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})
, denote

R:=k[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n-s}]
,
A:=k[V]
and
K:=k(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})
.
Consider
B:=K[Y_{n-s+1},\ldots,Y_{n}]/I^{e}
as a
K
–vector space, where

I^{e}
is the extended ideal
IK[Y_{n-s+1},\ldots,Y_{n}]
, and let

\delta:=\dim_{K}B
.

Definition 15
.

A
Kronecker representation
of
I
(or
V
) consists of the
following data:

•

a linear change
of variables
(X_{1},\dots,\!X_{n})

\to

(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})
with
the following properties:

–

The linear map
\pi:V\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
, defined by

Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}
, is a finite morphism. In this case,
the change of variables is called a
Noether normalization
of

V
, and we say that the variables
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
are in
Noether position
with respect to
V
, with

Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}
being
free variables
.

–

The linear form
Y_{n-s+1}
induces a
primitive element
of
I
, i.e., an
element
y_{n-s+1}\in A
whose monic minimal polynomial
M\in R[T]
over
K
satisfies the condition
\deg_{T}M=\delta
. Note
that
\deg M=\deg_{T}M\leq\deg V
;

•

The monic minimal polynomial
M\in R[T]
of
Y_{n-s+1}
modulo

I
;

•

Polynomials
W_{n-s+2},\ldots,W_{n}\in K[T]
, of degree at most

\delta-1
, such that the following identity holds in

K[Y_{n-s+1},\dots,Y_{n}]
:

I^{e}=\bigl(M(Y_{n-s+1}),M^{\prime}(Y_{n-s+1})Y_{n-s+2}-W_{n-s+2}(Y_{n-s+1}),\ldots,M^{\prime}(Y_{n-s+1})Y_{n}-W_{n}(Y_{n-s+1})\bigr),

(2.2)

where
M^{\prime}
denotes the derivative of
M
with respect to
T
.

Considering instead polynomials
V_{n-s+2},\ldots,V_{n}
of degree at
most
\delta-1
, such that

I^{e}=\bigl(M(Y_{n-s+1}),Y_{n-s+2}-V_{n-s+2}(Y_{n-s+1}),\dots,Y_{n}-V_{n}(Y_{n-s+1})\bigr),

we have a
univariate representation
of
I
(or
V
).

The identity (
2.2
) allows us a
geometric interpretation of Kronecker representations, as described
