in Section
1.1.3
. Let
\ell:\mathbb{A}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}
be the linear map defined by
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
, and let

W:=\ell(V)
. We interpret
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
as new indeterminates,
and consider the projection
\Pi:W\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n-s+1}

onto the first
n-s+1
coordinates.
Considering
M
as a polynomial of
k[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n-s+1}]
, we
observe that
\Pi
defines a birational map between
W
and the
hypersurface
\{M=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-s+1}
. Its inverse is the
rational map
\Phi:\{M=0\}\rightarrow W
, given by

\Phi(\boldsymbol{y}):=\left(\boldsymbol{y},\frac{W_{n-s+2}(\boldsymbol{y})}{M^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})},\dots,\frac{W_{n}(\boldsymbol{y})}{M^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})}\right).

This map is defined outside
the zero locus of
M^{\prime}
, namely, on the nonempty Zariski dense open
subset
\{M=0\}\cap\{M^{\prime}\not=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-s+1}
. If
\rho\in k[Y_{1},\ldots,X_{n-s}]

denotes the
discriminant
of
M
with respect to
Y_{n-s+1}
,
it is easy to see that the set where
\Phi
is undefined is
precisely
\{\rho=0\}\cap\{M=0\}
.

2.2
Computational model and basic operations

Throughout this work,
\log_{2}
denotes the base-2 logarithm. In addition
to the standard Big–Oh notation
\mathcal{O}
, we will also use the Soft–Oh
notation
\mathcal{O}^{\sim}
, which does not take into
account logarithmic terms. More precisely, for functions
f=f(n,d,h)
and
g=g(n,d,h)
depending on
integer parameters
n
,
d
,
h
, we write

f\in\mathcal{O}^{\sim}(g)
if there exists
s\geq 0
such that

f\in\mathcal{O}(g\log_{2}^{s}g)
.

In computer algebra, algorithms often assume the standard dense
(or sparse) representation model, where multivariate polynomials are
represented via their full list of coefficients (or only the nonzero
ones). However, a general
n
–variate polynomial of
degree
d
has
\binom{n+d}{n}=\mathcal{O}(d^{n})
nonzero
coefficients, and thus its dense or sparse representation requires an
exponential size in both
d
and
n
. Consequently, algorithms
manipulating such representations may require an exponential number of
arithmetic operations with respect to
d
and
n
.

To overcome this, we adopt an alternative representation based on
straight–line programs (cf.
Bürgisser et al. 1997
). A
(division–free) straight–line program

\beta
in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
,
which
represents
or
evaluates
polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{s}\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, is a sequence
(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{r})
of elements of

k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
satisfying the following conditions:

•

\{F_{1},\dots,F_{s}\}\subseteq\{Q_{1},\dots,Q_{r}\}
;

•

there exists a finite subset
\mathcal{T}\subset k
, called the set of
parameters
of
\beta
,
such that for every
1\leq\rho\leq r
, the polynomial
Q_{\rho}
either is an
element of
\mathcal{T}\cup\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
, or there exist

1\leq\rho_{1},\rho_{2}<\rho
such that
Q_{\rho}={\sf op}_{\rho}(Q_{\rho_{1}},Q_{\rho_{2}})
, where
{\sf op}_{\rho}
is one
of the arithmetic operations
+,-,\times
.

The
length
of
\beta
is the total number of
arithmetic operations performed during the evaluation process
defined by
\beta
.

Further, we allow decisions and selections (subject to previous
decisions). For this reason, we consider
computation trees
,
which are straight–line programs with
branchings
. The length of a
computation tree is defined analogously to that for
straight–line programs (see, e.g.,
Bürgisser et al. 1997
for more details
on the notion of computation trees).

When
k
is a finite field

\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}
, or
k=\mathbb{Q}
and an upper bound for the bit size is known, each arithmetic operation
{\tt op}\in\{+,-,\times\}
can be implemented by a Boolean circuit that
receives the bit representation of
{\tt a}\,,{\tt b}\in k
and returns the bit representation of
{\tt op}\,({\tt a}\,,{\tt b})
. The runtime is measured by the size of the
resulting Boolean circuit, i.e., the total number of bit operations
performed.

2.2.1
Basic operations

Given
\delta>0
, we will frequently use the notation

{\sf M}(\delta):=\delta\,\log_{2}\delta\,\log_{2}\log_{2}\delta.

Using the Schönage–Strassen multiplication algorithm, the product of two
integers of bit length
m
can be computed with
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(m))
bit operations (see, e.g.,

(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Section 8.3)
). Similarly, division with remainder
of integers of bit length
m
can be performed
with
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(m))
bit operations
(see, e.g.,
(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Section 9.1)
). It is worth mentioning that
there exist algorithms for integer multiplication and
division with remainder of integers of bit length
m

in
\mathcal{O}(m\log_{2}m)
bit operations
Harvey and van der Hoeven 2021
;
for simplicity, we will not use these algorithms in this paper.

Let
{\sf R}
be a commutative ring with unity,
T
an indeterminate
over
{\sf R}
, and
{\sf R}[T]
the ring of univariate polynomials
in
T
with coefficients in
{\sf R}
. Using the
Schönhage–Strassen multiplication algorithm, the product of two
polynomials of degree at most
\delta
can be computed with

\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta))
arithmetic operations in
{\sf R}

(see, e.g.,
(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Section 8.3)
). Moreover, given a monic
polynomial of
{\sf R}[T]
of degree at most
\delta
, polynomial
division with remainder in
{\sf R}[T]
can be performed with

\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta))
arithmetic operations in
{\sf R}

using Newton iteration
(see, e.g.,
(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Section 9.1)
). It follows that
multiplication in the quotient ring
{\sf R}[T]/(f)

can also be done with
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta))
arithmetic
operations in
{\sf R}
.

For polynomials
f,g\in k[T]
of degree at most
\delta
, their gcd
(along with a Bézout identity) can be computed with
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta)\log_{2}\delta)

arithmetic operations in
k
and identity tests between elements of

k
, using a fast Extended
Euclidean algorithm (see, e.g.,
(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Section 11.1)
). In the same
complexity, the resultant
\mathrm{res}(f,g)\in k
can be computed.
In particular, modular inversion in the residue class ring

k[T]/(f)
, where
f\in k[T]
has degree at most
\delta
, can be
done with
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta)\log_{2}\delta)
arithmetic
operations and identity tests in
k
.

Let
f\in k[T]
be a monic polynomial of degree at most

\delta
. It admits a unique square–free factorization

f=g_{1}g_{2}^{2}\cdots g_{m}^{m}
,
where
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
are monic polynomials which are pairwise–coprime. The
square–free part of
f
is
g_{1}\cdots g_{m}
. According to

(
Lecerf 2008
, Corollary 2)
, a variant of Yun’s square–free
factorization algorithm (see, e.g.,
(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Algorithm 14.21)
)
computes the square–free part of
f
with

\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta)\log_{2}\delta)
arithmetic operations in

k
, and
\mathcal{O}(\delta)
extractions of
p
–th roots in
k

(if
\mathrm{char}(k)=p>0
). In particular, if

k=\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
with
\mathrm{char}(k)=p
, then a
p
–th root extraction requires
\mathcal{O}(\log(q/p))
arithmetic
operations in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
.

For a commutative ring
{\sf R}
with unity, and a
polynomial
f\in{\sf R}[T]
of degree at most
\delta
, its
(multipoint) evaluation at
\delta
points of
{\sf R}
can be done
with
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta)\log_{2}\delta)
arithmetic
operations in
{\sf R}
(see, e.g.,
(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Corollary 10.8)
).
Likewise, given interpolation points

\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{\delta}\in{\sf R}
with pairwise invertible
differences
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}
for
i\not=j
, and values

\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{\delta}\in{\sf R}
, the unique
interpolating polynomial
f\in{\sf R}[T]
of degree at most
\delta
can
be computed with
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta)\log_{2}\delta)

arithmetic operations in
{\sf R}
(see, e.g.,
(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Corollary
10.12)
).

The determinant and the adjoint matrix of a matrix in

{\sf R}^{n\times n}
can be computed (without
divisions) using the Samuelson–Berkowitz algorithm with

\mathcal{O}(n^{\omega+1})
arithmetic operations (see
Berkowitz 1984
),
where
\omega\geq 2
denotes the exponent of matrix multiplication over

{\sf R}
; that is, the infimum over all real numbers
\beta
such that two
matrices of
{\sf R}^{n\times n}
can be multiplied using

\mathcal{O}(n^{\beta})
arithmetic operations in
{\sf R}
. The best
currently known bound is
\omega<2.371552
(see
Williams et al. 2024
).

For the sake of simplicity and clarity in our asymptotic
estimates, we will take
\omega=3
throughout the paper. This assumption
is justified by the fact that the parameter
n
(the dimension of the
Jacobian matrices involved) remains relatively small compared with other
quantities such as the degrees
\delta_{s}
and the height bounds. Adopting

\omega=3
therefore makes the complexity expressions more transparent, without
affecting their theoretical validity or the practical efficiency of
the algorithms under consideration.

2.2.2
Probabilistic aspects

Our algorithms are of the
Monte Carlo
or
BPP
type
(see, e.g.,
Pardo 1995
,
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
), meaning that they return the
correct output with probability at least a fixed constant
strictly greater than
1/2
. Consequently, the error probability
can be made arbitrarily small by independently repeating the algorithm. The
probabilistic nature of our methods arises from certain random
choices of points that are required not annihilating specific polynomials.
This is based on
the following result (see, e.g.,
(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Lemma 6.44)
):

Lemma 16
.

Let
k
be a field, let

\mathcal{S}\subset k
be a finite set, and let
F\in k[{X}_{1},\ldots,{X}_{n}]
be a
nonzero polynomial of degree at most
d
. Then the number of zeros
of
F
in
\mathcal{S}^{n}
is at most
d(\#\mathcal{S})^{n-1}
.

Lemma
16
was independently discovered by
DeMillo & Lipton
Millo and Lipton 1978
, Zippel
Zippel 1979
, and
Schwartz
Schwartz 1980
. For the analysis of our algorithms, we
interpret the statement of Lemma
16
in
probabilistic terms. Specifically, given a fixed nonzero polynomial

F\in k[{X}_{1},\ldots,{X}_{n}]
of degree at most
d
, and a finite set

\mathcal{S}\subset k
, the lemma implies that the probability of
randomly selecting a point
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
such
that
{F(\boldsymbol{a})=0}
is at most
d/\#\mathcal{S}
, assuming a
uniform distribution on
\mathcal{S}^{n}
.

It is worth mentioning that there is a more refined approach, based
on the concept of
correct-test sequences

Heintz and Schnorr 1982
, which
generalizes the probabilistic bound given by Lemma
16
. A correct-test sequence for a class

\mathcal{P}\subset k[{X}_{1},\ldots,{X}_{n}]
is a finite sequence of points in
k^{n}

such that, for every
F\in\mathcal{P}
, the vanishing of
F
at
these points implies
F=0
. The main result in
Heintz and Schnorr 1982
asserts
that, for every
L>0
, there exist short correct-test sequences for
the class
\mathcal{P}_{L}
of polynomials of
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]

computable by straight-line programs of length at most
L
(see also

Pardo and Sebastián 2022
for extensions and generalizations). Applying this
framework to the class of polynomials involved in our algorithms,
and taking into account their straight-line program length, could
lead to algorithms with higher reliability. Nevertheless, pursuing
this alternative would considerably increase the complexity of the
implementation, and for this reason, we have opted not to explore it
any further.

Finally, we observe that our algorithms do not appear to be of the
Las
Vegas
or
ZPP
type, since we do not have a mechanism to verify the
correctness of the output with a reasonable complexity.

3
Noether normalization, primitive elements and lifting fibers

Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G
be polynomials in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, with
r\leq n
, of
degree at most
d
. We denote by
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G}
the localization
of
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
at the multiplicative set generated by
G
.
For
1\leq s\leq r
, let
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s})_{G}
be
the ideal generated by
F_{1},\ldots,F_{s}
in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G}
.

We assume that the polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G
satisfy the
following conditions:

(
{\sf H}_{1}
)

F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
form a regular sequence in the
localization
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G}
,

(
{\sf H}_{2}
)

For
1\leq s\leq r
, the ideal
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s})_{G}
is radical in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G}
.

When these conditions hold, we say that
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
form
a
reduced regular sequence on

\{G\not=0\}
.

Let
V_{s}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
denote the Zariski closure of
\{F_{1}=0,\ldots,F_{s}=0,G\not=0\}
for
1\leq s\leq r
. Denote
V:=V_{r}
. Under
assumptions (
{\sf H}_{1}
) and (
{\sf H}_{2}
), each
V_{s}
has pure
dimension
n-s
. We denote by
\delta_{s}:=\deg V_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq r
, and define
\delta:=\max\{\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}\}
. Without
loss of generality, we shall assume that
\delta>d
, since the
most relevant asymptotic regime for our complexity analysis is when
the degrees of the intermediate varieties grow significantly faster
than the degrees of the defining polynomials.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}:=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
.
In this section, our goal is to prove that there exists a linear
change of variables
\boldsymbol{Y}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
, where

\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is an
n\times n
-matrix with coefficients in
\overline{k}
,
and a point
\boldsymbol{p}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1})\in\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
,
such that the variables
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
are

simultaneously
in Noether position with respect to all
the varieties
V_{1},\ldots,V_{r}
, and
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})

is a
lifting point
of
V_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq r
(a notion to
be defined precisely in Section
3.2
).
In addition, we will require the points
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}
to satisfy a subtle
but essential condition: since each
V_{s}
will be given
by a Kronecker representation—which only faithfully encodes

V_{s}

outside a certain discriminant locus
(see the remark
after Definition
15
)— the choice
of
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}
must ensure that the points involved in the computations
lie outside such a discriminant locus.

For this purpose, we will prove the existence of a polynomial

B\in\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}]
, where

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is an
(n\times n)
-matrix of indeterminates, and

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
is a vector of
n-1
indeterminates over

\overline{k}
, with the following property: for any

(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathbb{A}^{n\times n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
satisfying

B(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\not=0
, the change of variables
\boldsymbol{Y}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
and the point
\boldsymbol{p}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1})\in\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
satisfy all the conditions
above.

This allows the algorithm to begin by selecting a suitable pair

(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})
by
picking its coordinates at random

from a finite set
\mathcal{S}\subset\overline{k}
with

\#\mathcal{S}\geq\varepsilon^{-1}\deg B
, for a fixed

0<\varepsilon<1/2
. By Lemma
16
, the
probability of success of this choice is at least
1-\varepsilon
.
Moreover, if
k
has sufficiently large cardinality, we may choose

\mathcal{S}\subset k
, ensuring that the algorithm proceeds
entirely within
k
, without introducing field extensions. For these
reasons, it is crucial to obtain a polynomial
B
with minimal
possible degree, which will be the main focus of this section.

3.1
Simultaneous Noether normalization and a primitive element

Throughout this section, for
1\leq s\leq r
we interpret the elements
of
\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}
as
(n-s+1)\times n
–matrices with entries in

\overline{k}
. Given
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}
, we denote its
i
th
row by
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}:=(\lambda_{i,1},\ldots,\lambda_{i,n})\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
. We
frequently use the notation

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\cdot(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}):=\lambda_{i,1}X_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{i,n}X_{n}

for
1\leq i\leq n-s+1
. Accordingly, we write
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}:=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{X},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n-s+1}\cdot\boldsymbol{X})
. We also denote by
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s)n}

the submatrix consisting of the first
n-s
rows of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.

In this section, we show that a generic choice of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}
defines linear forms
\boldsymbol{Y}:=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n-s+1}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
such that
\boldsymbol{Y}^{*}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})
are in Noether position with respect to

V_{s}
, and
Y_{n-s+1}
is a primitive element of the
\overline{k}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})
-algebra extension

\overline{k}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})\to\overline{k}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})\otimes_{\overline{k}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}]}\overline{k}[V_{s}].

To that end, we let
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}:=(\Lambda_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq n-s+1,1\leq j\leq n}
be a matrix of indeterminates over

\overline{k}
, and denote its
i
th row by
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{i}:=(\Lambda_{i,1},\ldots,\Lambda_{i,n})
for
1\leq i\leq n-s+1
. Let
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}
be the submatrix of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}

consisting of its first
n-s
rows. We consider the vector of
generic linear forms
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}

and set
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}:=(\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s})
.

Our first result, probably well–known,
provides a condition ensuring that the ring homomorphism

\overline{k}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}]\to\overline{k}[V_{s}]
is
integral.

Lemma 17
.

For
1\leq s\leq r
, there
exists a nonzero polynomial
A_{s}^{1}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}]
of degree at
most
(n-s)\delta_{s}
with the following property: for any

\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s)n}
with
A_{s}^{1}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})\not=0
, if

\boldsymbol{Y}^{*}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})
, then the ring homomorphism

R_{s}:=\overline{k}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}]\to\overline{k}[V_{s}]

is integral.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
V_{s}
is irreducible.
Consider the morphism

\displaystyle\Phi:\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s+1}

(3.1)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\mapsto(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}).

For
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{y})\in Im(\Phi)
, the fiber

\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{y})
is isomorphic to
the set

V_{s}\cap\{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=y_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n-s+1}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=y_{n-s+1}\}.

For
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n-s}
generic,
the intersection
V_{s}\cap\{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=y_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n-s}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=y_{n-s}\}
has dimension zero. It follows
that a generic fiber
\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{y})
has dimension
zero. Therefore, by the Theorem on the Dimension of Fibers (see, e.g.,

(
Shafarevich 1994
, Chapter I, §6.3, Theorem 7)
) we conclude
that the Zariski closure
\overline{Im(\Phi)}
has dimension

(n-s+1)(n+1)-1
, namely it is a hypersurface of
\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s+1}
.

An equation for this hypersurface can be easily obtained
from a suitable specialization of the Chow form of
V_{s}
(see

(
Cafure and Matera 2006
, Proposition 3.1)
or
(
Giménez and Matera 2019
, Section 3.1)
for details).
More precisely,
\overline{Im(\Phi)}
is
defined by an irreducible polynomial
P_{V_{s}}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}]

satisfying the following conditions:

•

\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}}P_{V_{s}}=\deg_{\widetilde{Y}_{i}}P_{V_{s}}=\delta_{s}
for
1\leq i\leq n-s+1
,

•

P_{V_{s}}
is homogeneous of degree
\delta_{s}
in each group
of variables
(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{i},\widetilde{Y}_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq n-s+1
.

Let
A_{s}^{1}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}]
be the (nonzero) polynomial which
arises as coefficient of the monomial

\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}^{\delta_{s}}
in the polynomial
P_{V_{s}}
,
considering
P_{V_{s}}
as an element of
k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}][\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}]
. The above conditions imply
\deg A_{s}^{1}=(n-s)\delta_{s}
. Furthermore, the equality
\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1},\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}}P_{V_{s}}=\delta_{s}
implies
that
A_{s}^{1}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}]
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s)n}
be a point such that

A_{s}^{1}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})\not=0
, and let
\boldsymbol{Y}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
. We claim that the condition of
the lemma holds. Indeed, let
\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
be

\overline{k}
–linearly independent elements and let
\ell_{1}:=\boldsymbol{w}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{X},\ldots,\ell_{n}:=\boldsymbol{w}_{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
. Since

A_{1,s}^{*}:=A_{s}^{1}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})
is a nonzero element of
\overline{k}
,
the polynomial
P_{V_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},\boldsymbol{w}_{j},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s},\ell_{j})
is an integral dependence equation for the
coordinate function induced by
\ell_{j}
in the ring homomorphism

R_{s}\to\overline{k}[V_{s}]
for
1\leq j\leq s
. Since

\overline{k}[\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}]=\overline{k}[{X}_{1},\ldots,{X}_{n}]
,
the ring homomorphism
R_{s}\to\overline{k}[V_{s}]
is integral. ∎

Given
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}
as in the statement of Lemma

17
and
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
, by localization at
R_{s}\setminus\{0\}
we have the

\overline{k}
-algebra homomorphism

K_{s}:=\overline{k}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})\to\overline{k}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V_{s}].

In particular, Lemma
17
implies
that
\overline{k}[V_{s}]
is a finite
R_{s}
–module and
hence
K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V_{s}]
is a
finite–dimensional
K_{s}
–vector space.
Furthermore, the dimension of
K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V_{s}]
as

K_{s}
–vector space equals the rank of
\overline{k}[V_{s}]
as
R_{s}
–module.
Recall that a primitive element of this
\overline{k}
-algebra extension
is an element
y\in K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V_{s}]
such that its minimal
polynomial has degree
\dim_{K_{s}}K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V_{s}]
.

Suppose that
y\in\overline{k}[V_{s}]
is a primitive element of the

\overline{k}
-algebra extension
K_{s}\hookrightarrow K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V_{s}]
.
Since
R_{s}
is integrally closed, the minimal dependence equation of

y\in\overline{k}[V_{s}]
over
K_{s}
equals the minimal integral dependence equation
of
y
over
R_{s}
(see, e.g.,
(
Kunz 1985
, Lemma II.2.15)
).

Now we obtain a condition which implies that the
projection
\pi_{s}:V_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
defined by

Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}
is a finite morphism and the
linear
form
Y_{n-s+1}
is a primitive element of the
corresponding
K_{s}
–algebra extension

Proposition 18
.

For
1\leq s\leq r
,
there exists a nonzero polynomial
A_{s}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}]
of degree
at most
2(n-s+1)\delta_{s}^{2}
with the following property: for any

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}
with
A_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\not=0
, if

\boldsymbol{Y}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1})
, then

1.

the map
\pi_{s}:V_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
defined by
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}

is a finite morphism,

2.

the linear form
Y_{n-s+1}
induces a primitive element
of the
\overline{k}
-algebra extension
K_{s}\hookrightarrow K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V_{s}]
.

Proof.

Let
P_{V_{s}}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}]
be the polynomial
of the proof of Lemma
17
. Let

(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{y}^{*})\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
, let
\boldsymbol{Y}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
and
\boldsymbol{p}^{*}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})
. By the definition of
P_{V_{s}}
, the
zeros of
P_{V_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{*},\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1})
are the values
that
Y_{n-s+1}
takes at the points of the set

V_{s}\cap\{Y_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}=p_{n-s}\}.

(3.2)

According to
Heintz 1983
, for a generic
(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n-s},\boldsymbol{p}^{*})\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
, the
variety of (
3.2
) consists of
\delta_{s}

points. Furthermore, for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n-s+1}
generic, the linear
form
Y_{n-s+1}
separates the points of this variety. It follows
that the polynomial
P_{V_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{*},\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1})
has
\delta_{s}
distinct roots in

\overline{k}
, and therefore it is separable. In particular,

P_{V_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{*},\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1})
is coprime to
its derivative
\partial P_{V_{s}}/\partial\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{*},\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1})
,
and the discriminant
\rho_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{*})
of

P_{V_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{*},\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1})
is nonzero.
Furthermore, we have

\delta_{s}\leq\deg_{\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}}P_{V_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{*},\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1})\leq\deg_{\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}}P_{V_{s}}=\delta_{s}.

We conclude that
P_{V_{s}}
and
\partial P_{V_{s}}/\partial\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}
are relatively prime in

k(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*})[\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}]
,
and the discriminant

\rho_{s}:=\mbox{Res}_{\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}}(P_{V_{s}},\partial P_{V_{s}}/\partial\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1})\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}]

(3.3)

of
P_{V_{s}}
with respect to
\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}
is nonzero. It
satisfies the degree estimates

•

\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}}\rho_{s}\leq(2\delta_{s}-1)\delta_{s}
,

•

\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{i},\widetilde{Y}_{i}}\rho_{s}=(2\delta_{s}-1)\delta_{s}
for
1\leq i\leq n-s
,

•

\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1}}\rho_{s}\leq(2\delta_{s}-1)\delta_{s}
.

Let
A_{s}^{2}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}]
be a nonzero coefficient of a monomial
of
\rho_{s}
, considering
\rho_{s}
as an element of

k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}][\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
, and let

A_{s}:=A_{s}^{1}A_{s}^{2}
, where
A_{s}^{1}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}]
is the
polynomial of the statement of Lemma
17
. Observe that
\deg A_{s}\leq 2(n-s+1)\delta_{s}^{2}
. Let

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}
satisfy the condition

A_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\not=0
, and let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s)n}
be
the matrix consisting on the first
n-s
rows of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.
Denote
\boldsymbol{Y}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}^{*}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
. By Lemma
17
it is clear that the ring homomorphism

R_{s}\to\overline{k}[V_{s}]
is integral.

First we show that condition (
2
) holds. If
P_{V_{s}}^{*}
and
\rho_{s}^{*}
are the polynomials obtained
from
P_{V_{s}}
and
\rho_{s}
by evaluating
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}
at

\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}
, then the definition of
A_{s}^{1}
implies that

\rho_{s}^{*}
is a nonzero element of
k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1},\boldsymbol{Y}^{*}]

which equals the discriminant of
P_{V_{s}}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1},\boldsymbol{Y}^{*},\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1})
with respect to
\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\xi}:=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})
be the vector of coordinate
functions of
V_{s}
defined by
\boldsymbol{X}
, let
\zeta_{i}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}
for
1\leq i\leq n-s
and

\widehat{Y}_{n-s+1}:=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}
. From the
definition of
P_{V_{s}}
we conclude that the identity

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle P_{V_{s}}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\!\zeta_{n-s},\widehat{Y}_{n-s+1})=P_{V_{s}}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n-s},\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Lambda_{n-s+1,k}\,\xi_{k})

(3.4)

holds in
\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1}]\otimes_{\overline{k}}\overline{k}[V_{s}]
.
Following, e.g.,
Alonso et al. 1996
or
Rouillier 1997
, taking the
partial derivative with respect to the variable
\Lambda_{n-s+1,k}

at the first and the last sides of (
3.4
) we deduce the
following identity in
\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1}]\otimes_{\overline{k}}\overline{k}[V_{s}]
for
1\leq k\leq n
:

\frac{\partial P_{V_{s}}^{*}}{\partial\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n-s},\widehat{Y}_{n-s+1})\xi_{k}+\frac{\partial P_{V_{s}}^{*}}{\partial\Lambda_{n-s+1,k}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n-s},\widehat{Y}_{n-s+1})=0.

(3.5)

Since
\rho_{s}^{*}
is the discriminant of
P_{V_{s}}^{*}
with respect to

\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}
, it can be written as a linear combination
of
P_{V_{s}}^{*}
and
\partial P_{V_{s}}^{*}/\partial\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}
. Therefore, (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) imply

\rho_{s}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n-s})\,\xi_{k}+P_{k}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n-s},\widehat{Y}_{n-s+1})=0,

(3.6)

where
P_{k}
is an element of
k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n-s+1},{Z}_{1},\ldots,{Z}_{n-s+1}]
for
1\leq k\leq n
. Substituting
\lambda_{n-s+1,k}
for

\Lambda_{n-s+1,k}
for
1\leq k\leq n
in (
3.6
), we conclude that the powers of the coordinate
function of
\overline{k}[V_{s}]
defined by
Y_{n-s+1}
generate

K_{s}\otimes_{\overline{k}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}]}\overline{k}[V_{s}]
as a

K_{s}
–vector space. In particular, its minimal polynomial
over
K_{s}
has degree equal to
\dim_{K_{s}}(K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V_{s}])
.
In other words,
Y_{n-s+1}

induces a primitive element of the

K_{s}
–algebra extension
K_{s}\hookrightarrow K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V_{s}]
.
This proves condition (
2
).

Now we show that condition (
1
) holds. For this purpose, it remains to prove that the
mapping
\pi_{s}:V_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
is dominant. As

\rho_{s}^{*}(\lambda_{n-s+1},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})
is nonzero, there
exists
\boldsymbol{y}^{*}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
with
\rho_{s}^{*}(\lambda_{n-s+1},\boldsymbol{y})\not=0
. Let
y_{n-s+1}
be any root in
\overline{k}
of

P_{V_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{y}^{*},T)
and
\boldsymbol{y}:=(\boldsymbol{y}^{*},y_{n-s+1})
. Finally, in view of (
3.6
), let
x_{k}\in\overline{k}
be defined as

x_{k}:=\frac{P_{k}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n-s+1},\boldsymbol{y})}{\rho_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{y})}

for
1\leq k\leq n
and
\boldsymbol{x}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. It is easy to see
that
\boldsymbol{x}\in V_{s}
and
\pi_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{y}^{*}
(see, e.g.,

(
Cafure and Matera 2006
, Proposition 6.3)
for details).
This finishes the proof of the proposition.
∎

From the proof of Proposition
18
, one deduces that if
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}
are linear forms
as in the statement, then

\dim_{K_{s}}(K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}\overline{k}[V_{s}])=\delta_{s}.

Furthermore, it follows readily
that the polynomial
A_{s}
has degree at
most
2\delta_{s}^{2}
in
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n-s+1
.

Now let
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}
be linear forms as in the statement
of the proposition. From identity (
3.6
), we conclude that there exist polynomials
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{n}\in k[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}]
, each of degree at most
\delta_{s}-1
, such that

\rho_{s}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})X_{k}\equiv Q_{k}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1})

(3.7)

in
V_{s}
for
1\leq k\leq n
, where
\rho_{s}:=\mbox{Res}_{Y_{n-s+1}}(P_{V_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}),\frac{\partial P_{V_{s}}}{\partial Y_{n-s+1}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}))
, and

P_{V_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1})\in k[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}]
is
the minimal polynomial of
Y_{n-s+1}
in the ring extension

R_{s}\hookrightarrow\overline{k}[V_{s}]
.

Similarly, from identity (
3.5
), we deduce the existence of polynomials

\widehat{Q}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{Q}_{n}\in k[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}]
, each of degree
at most
\delta_{s}-1
, such that

\frac{\partial P_{V_{s}}}{\partial Y_{n-s+1}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1})X_{k}\equiv\widehat{Q}_{k}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1})

(3.8)

in
V_{s}
for
1\leq k\leq n
.

From now on, we work with linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}
that satisfy the conditions of Proposition

18
. To this end,
we consider the localization defined by the condition

A_{s}\not=0
, that is, instead of considering the
morphism
\Phi
of the proof of Lemma
17
,
we consider the following morphism:

\displaystyle\Phi_{s}:(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}

\displaystyle\to(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s})\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}

(3.9)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda},Y_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}),\ldots,Y_{n-s}(\boldsymbol{x})\big).

We have the following result.

Lemma 19
.

\Phi_{s}
is a finite morphism.

Proof.

First we show that
\Phi_{s}
is surjective. Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{y})\in(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s})\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}
. Since

A_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\not=0
, Proposition
18
shows that the map
\pi_{s}:V_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}

defined by
\boldsymbol{Y}^{*}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
is a finite
morphism. In particular, there exists
\boldsymbol{x}\in V_{s}
with

\pi_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{y}
. This shows that

\Phi_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{x})=(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{x})=(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{y})
.

Now let
\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
be
\overline{k}
–linearly
independent elements and let
\ell_{1}:=\boldsymbol{w}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{X},\ldots,\ell_{n}:=\boldsymbol{w}_{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
. By definition, the polynomial

P_{V_{s}}\in\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}]
of the proof
of Lemma
17
vanishes on

\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s}
. Considering
P_{V_{s}}
as an element of

\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}][\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}]=\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}][\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}]
, it is of degree
\delta_{s}
. Further, by construction, the
(nonzero) coefficient
A_{s}^{1}\in\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}]
of the monomial

\widetilde{Y}_{n-s+1}^{\delta_{s}}
in
P_{V_{s}}
is a divisor of

A_{s}
.

As
A_{s}
is a unit of the localization

\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}]_{A_{s}}
, the equality

P_{V_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*},\boldsymbol{w}_{j},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X},\ell_{j})=0

is an integral dependence equation for the coordinate function
induced by
\ell_{j}
in the ring extension

\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}]_{A_{s}}\hookrightarrow\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}][V_{s}]_{A_{s}}
for
1\leq j\leq s
. Since
\overline{k}[\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}]=\overline{k}[{X}_{1},\ldots,{X}_{n}]
, we deduce the assertion
of the lemma.
∎

3.2
Lifting fibers not meeting a discriminant

Assume we are given linearly independent linear forms
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\in k[\boldsymbol{X}]
defining variables in Noether position with
respect to
V_{s}
, as in Proposition
18
. Let
\pi_{s}:V_{s}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
be the
finite morphism defined by
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n-s}
, and let
J_{s}\subset k[\boldsymbol{X}]
denote the ideal

J_{s}:=((F_{1},\ldots,F_{s}):G^{\infty},Y_{1}-p_{1},\dots,Y_{n-s}-p_{n-s})
.
We say that a point
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})\in k^{n-s}
is a

lifting point
of
\pi_{s}
with respect to the system

F_{1}=0,\dots,F_{s}=0,G\neq 0
if
J_{s}
is radical. We call the
zero–dimensional variety
V_{\boldsymbol{p}^{s}}:=\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
the

lifting fiber
of
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}

2
2

2

Let
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\subset\{G\not=0\}
. A straightforward computation
shows that
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
is unramified if and only if

\det(\partial F_{i}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{x})/\partial Y_{n-s+j})_{1\leq i\leq s,1\leq j\leq s}\not=0

for all
\boldsymbol{x}\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
. By
(
Eisenbud 1995
, Theorem 18.15)
,
this condition is equivalent to the ideal
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s},Y_{1}-p_{1},\dots,Y_{n-s}-p_{n-s})
being
a radical ideal.
.

The notion of lifting fiber in this framework was first introduced
in
Giusti et al. 1997a
. It was formalized in
Heintz et al. 2000
,
where it was shown how a Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber
of an equidimensional variety can be used to tackle certain
fundamental algorithmic problems (see also
Giusti et al. 2001
,

Schost 2003
,
Bompadre et al. 2004
,
Pardo and San Martín 2004
, and

Jeronimo et al. 2009
for extensions, refinements and algorithmic
developments related to lifting fibers). The notion is also
prominent in numerical algebraic geometry, where it is known as a

witness set
(see, e.g.,
Sommese and Wampler 2005
; see also

Sommese et al. 2008
for a dictionary between lifting fibers and witness
sets).

In this section, we show that through a generic choice of
coordinates, one obtains a lifting point
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}\in k^{n-s}
such
that the corresponding lifting fiber
V_{\boldsymbol{p}^{s}}
does not
intersect the hypersurface
\{G=0\}
for
1\leq s\leq r
.
Additionally, we ensure that the lifting fiber
V_{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}}:=\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
, for
0\leq s\leq r-1
, has
the following property: for any point
\boldsymbol{q}\in V_{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}}
,
the morphism
\pi_{s}
is unramified at
\pi_{s}(\boldsymbol{q})
. This ensures
that no point of
V_{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}}
lies in the locus of
V_{s}
which
cannot be parametrized by the Kronecker representation of
V_{s}

under consideration, thereby leading to significant algorithmic
simplifications. To this end, we must control those points
\boldsymbol{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
whose fibers
V_{\boldsymbol{p}}
, under any admissible change
of coordinates, either intersect the hypersurface
\{G=0\}
or fail
to be lifting points. Both conditions can be captured by the
vanishing of a certain hypersurface
\{H=0\}\subset(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}
.

The approach developed in this section builds upon and extends the
methods introduced in
Heintz et al. 2001
for the case
k=\mathbb{Q}
and in

Cafure and Matera 2006
for finite fields. In contrast with these earlier
works, which deal with closed varieties, we consider here the more
general setting of locally closed sets over a perfect field
k
.
This broader framework requires a refined analysis of the geometric
conditions under consideration, leading to improved degree bounds
for the hypersurfaces defining the exceptional parameter choices.
The organization of this subsection follows the structure of

Heintz et al. 2001
;
Cafure and Matera 2006
, but the arguments are adapted and
strengthened to accommodate these generalizations.

In the following technical result, we show that the “defective”
points
\boldsymbol{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
lie in a hypersurface of “low” degree.
This improvement is critical for tightening the estimates in

(
Heintz et al. 2001
, Lemma 1
(iii)
)
and
(
Cafure and Matera 2006
, Lemma 3.2)
.

Lemma 20
.

With notations
and assumptions as above, fix
s
with
1\leq s\leq r
. Let
A_{s}
be
the polynomial from Proposition
18
and let
H\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{X}]\setminus k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}]
be a polynomial with
\deg_{\boldsymbol{X}}H\leq D_{\boldsymbol{X}}
and
\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}H\leq D_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
. Suppose that the
Zariski closure
{V}_{s}^{H}
of
(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{H=0,A_{s}\not=0\}
is empty or of pure dimension

(n-s+1)(n+1)-2
. Then the Zariski closure of the image of
{V}_{s}^{H}

under the morphism
\Phi_{s}
of (
3.9
) is
contained in a hypersurface of
\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
defined
by a polynomial
B_{s}^{H}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
with

\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}}B_{s}^{H}\leq D_{\boldsymbol{X}}\delta_{s},\quad\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}B_{s}^{H}\leq(D_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}+(n-s)D_{\boldsymbol{X}})\delta_{s}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we may assume that
V_{s}
is irreducible
and
{V}_{s}^{H}
has pure
dimension
(n-s+1)(n+1)-2
.

By Lemma
19
, the morphism
\Phi_{s}
is
finite. It follows that
\Phi_{s}(V_{s}^{H})
is a hypersurface of

(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s})\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}
,
and hence its Zariski closure is a hypersurface of

\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
. Since both
V_{s}^{H}

and
\Phi_{s}
are defined over
k
, there exists a polynomial
P\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
of minimal
degree whose vanishing defines the Zariski closure of

\Phi_{s}(V_{s}^{H})
, where
\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}
are
new indeterminates. In particular,

P(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X})
vanishes on
V_{s}^{H}
, and
therefore, it belongs to the radical of the ideal

(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s},H)
in
k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{X}]_{A_{s}}
, and
a
fortiori
, to the radical of
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s},H):A_{s}^{\infty}
in

k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{X}]
(see, e.g.,
(
Zariski and Samuel 1958
, Chapter IV, §8)
). The minimality of
P
implies that
A_{s}
and

P
are coprime in
k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{X}]
.

Next, let
\mathcal{K}
be an algebraic closure of
k(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})
,
and let
W_{s}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathcal{K}}
be the affine variety obtained from

V_{s}
by extension of coefficients, namely,
W_{s}
is defined by the
ideal
I(V_{s})\otimes_{k}\mathcal{K}
. Let

\Phi_{s,\mathcal{K}}:W_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}_{\mathcal{K}}
be the corresponding
morphism, which remains finite.

Under our assumptions,

{W_{s}}\cap\{H=0\}
has pure dimension
n-s-1
. Hence,
the image
\Phi_{s,\mathcal{K}}({W_{s}}\cap\{H=0\})

is a hypersurface of
\mathbb{A}^{n-s}(\mathcal{K})
.
Observe that
W_{s}
,
H
and
\Phi_{s,\mathcal{K}}
are
defined over
k(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})
, which implies that

\Phi_{s,\mathcal{K}}({W_{s}}\cap\{H=0\})
is also defined over

k(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})
, therefore by a polynomial

B_{s}^{H}\in k(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})[\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]

of minimal degree. We may assume that

B_{s}^{H}
belongs to
k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}][\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
and it is primitive as a polynomial of

k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}][\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
.

By the Hilbert Nullstellensatz,
B_{s}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X})
lies in the radical of
(F_{1},\dots,F_{s},H)
in
k(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})[\boldsymbol{X}]
, and
B_{s}^{H}
is of minimal degree
in
k(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})[\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]

with this property. Observe that the ideal defined by
(F_{1},\dots,F_{s},H)
in

k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}]_{A_{s}}[\boldsymbol{X}]
is the contraction of the ideal
defined by
(F_{1},\dots,F_{s},H)
in
k(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})[\boldsymbol{X}]
. By

(
Zariski and Samuel 1958
, Chapter IV, §8)
, the radical of the former is the
contraction of the radical of the latter. We conclude that

B_{s}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}\cdot{\boldsymbol{X}})
lies in the radical
of the ideal
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{s},H)
in
k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{X}]_{A_{s}}
,
and thus in the radical of the ideal
(F_{1},\dots,F_{s},H):A_{s}^{\infty}
in
k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{X}]
.

On the other hand, considering the polynomial
P
above as a
polynomial in
k(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})[\boldsymbol{X}]
, we have that

P(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X})
vanishes on
{W_{s}}\cap\{H=0\}
, and thus
P(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s})
vanishes on
\Phi_{s,\mathcal{K}}({W_{s}}\cap\{H=0\})
. As a consequence, the definition of
B_{s}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s})
implies that

B_{s}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s})

divides
P(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s})
in

k(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})[\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
. In
addition, the fact that
B_{s}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X})
lies in the radical ideal of

(F_{1},\dots,F_{s},H):A_{s}^{\infty}
in
k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{X}]
,
together with the definition of
P
, imply that

P(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s})
divides

A_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})B_{s}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s})
, and thus
B_{s}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s})
, in

k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}][\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
.
Furthermore, since
B_{s}^{H}
is primitive as a polynomial of

k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}][\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
, we
conclude that
P
is primitive as well. It follows that both
P
and

B_{s}^{H}
have the same degree in
\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}
, and being both primitive as polynomials in

k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}][\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
, they must
differ by a nonzero constant in
k
.

To estimate
the degrees of this polynomial, we apply
(
D’Andrea et al. 2013
, Theorem
3.1)
. According to this result, there
exist a polynomial
B_{s}^{H}
with the bounds:

\displaystyle\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}}B_{s}^{H}

\displaystyle\leq\deg(W_{s}\cap\{H=0\})\leq D_{\boldsymbol{X}}\delta_{s},

\displaystyle\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}B_{s}^{H}

\displaystyle\leq h(W_{s}\cap\{H=0\})+\deg(W_{s}\cap\{H=0\})(n-s),

where
h
is the height as defined in
(
D’Andrea et al. 2013
, Definition
2.2)
. Since
h(W_{s})=0
by definition,
by
(
D’Andrea et al. 2013
, Corollary 2.20)
we have

h(W_{s}\cap\{H=0\})\leq D_{\boldsymbol{X}}\left(h(W_{s})+\deg W_{s}\frac{D_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}}{D_{\boldsymbol{X}}}\right)=D_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\deg W_{s}.

It follows that

\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}B_{s}^{H}\leq(D_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}+(n-s)D_{\boldsymbol{X}})\delta_{s},

which yields the desired estimate.
∎

Now we apply Lemma
20

to control the set of points which fail to satisfy the desired conditions.
To ensure that
\boldsymbol{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-s}

is a lifting point of
V_{s}
and that
\boldsymbol{p}^{*}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-s-1}

is a lifting point of
V_{s+1}
, we introduce the following
polynomials
D_{s},D_{s+1}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{X}]
:

D_{s}:=\det\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda_{1,1}&\dots&\Lambda_{1,n}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\Lambda_{n-s,1}&\dots&\Lambda_{n-s,n}\\
\frac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial X_{1}}&\dots&\frac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial X_{n}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial F_{s}}{\partial X_{1}}&\dots&\frac{\partial F_{s}}{\partial X_{n}}\end{pmatrix},\quad D_{s+1}:=\det\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda_{1,1}&\dots&\Lambda_{1,n}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\Lambda_{n-s-1,1}&\dots&\Lambda_{n-s-1,n}\\
\frac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial X_{1}}&\dots&\frac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial X_{n}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial F_{s+1}}{\partial X_{1}}&\dots&\frac{\partial F_{s+1}}{\partial X_{n}}\end{pmatrix}.

We show that the polynomials
G
,
D_{s}
and
D_{s+1}
meet the requirements
of Lemma
20
.

Lemma 21
.

The following assertions hold:

1.

The Zariski closure of
(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{G=0,A_{s}\not=0\}
is empty or of pure dimension
(n-s+1)(n+1)-2
.

2.

The Zariski closure of
(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{D_{s}=0,A_{s}\not=0\}
is empty or of pure dimension

(n-s+1)(n+1)-2
.

3.

The Zariski closure of

(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s)n}\times V_{s+1})\cap\{D_{s+1}=0,A_{s+1}\not=0\}
is
empty or of pure dimension
(n-s)(n+1)-2
.

Proof.

We begin with the first assertion. Let

V_{s}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{C}_{N}
be the decomposition
of
V_{s}
into irreducible components. By definition, each component

\mathcal{C}_{j}
has dimension
n-s
and it is not contained in

\{G=0\}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{C}_{j}\cap\{G=0\}
is either empty or has pure
dimension
n-s-1
. It follows that

(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{G=0\}=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{\,\,N}\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times(\mathcal{C}_{i}\cap\{G=0\})

is either empty or has pure dimension
(n-s+1)n+n-s-1=(n-s+1)(n+1)-2
.
Since this variety has a cylindrical structure, the subset
\{A_{s}\not=0\}

is Zariski dense open within it, which proves the first assertion.

For the second assertion, observe that

\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s}=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{\,\,N}\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathcal{C}_{i}

is a decomposition into irreducible components. Take any component

\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathcal{C}
, and let

\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathcal{C}
be a nonsingular point of
V_{s}
with
G(\boldsymbol{x})\not=0
.
Since
F_{1},\ldots,F_{s}
define a radical ideal in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G}
,
the gradients
\nabla F_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}),\ldots,\nabla F_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})
are
linearly independent. Thus,

D_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{x})\not=0
, and there exists

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}
with
D_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{x})\not=0
.
This shows that there exists a point
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)(n+1)}\times\mathcal{C}
not belonging to the
hypersurface
\{D_{s}=0\}
. On the other hand,
D_{s}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{x})=0
,
where
\boldsymbol{0}
represents the zero matrix of
\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}
. This
implies that
(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{D_{s}=0\}
is of pure
dimension
(n-s+1)(n+1)-2
, and hence the Zariski closure of

(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{D_{s}=0,A_{s}\not=0\}
is either empty
or has pure dimension
(n-s+1)(n+1)-2
.

The third assertion follows similarly by applying the same
reasoning to
V_{s+1}
,
A_{s+1}
and
D_{s+1}
.
∎

Now consider the following morphisms:

\displaystyle\Phi_{s}:(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}

\displaystyle\to(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s})\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda},Y_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}),\ldots,Y_{n-s}(\boldsymbol{x})\big),

\displaystyle\Phi_{s+1}:(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s)n}\times V_{s+1})\cap\{A_{s+1}\not=0\}

\displaystyle\to(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s-1})\cap\{A_{s+1}\not=0\}

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},Y_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}),\dots,Y_{n-s-1}(\boldsymbol{x})\big).

As shown in Lemma
19
, the morphism
\Phi_{s}

is finite. The same argument applies to show that

\Phi_{s+1}
is also finite.

In view of Lemma
21
,
we may assume without loss of generality that

(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{D_{s}=0,A_{s}\not=0\}
has pure
dimension
(n-s+1)(n+1)-2
(or pure codimension 1
in
(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}
).
It follows that
\Phi_{s}(\{D_{s}=0\})
is a
hypersurface of
(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s})\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}
,
defined by the polynomial
B_{s}^{D}
, that is,

\Phi_{s}(\{D_{s}=0,A_{s}\not=0\})=\{B_{s}^{D}=0,A_{s}\not=0\}.

This hypersurface consists of all the pairs

(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s})\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}

such that
\boldsymbol{p}
fails to be a lifting point of
V_{s}
under the
change of coordinates
\boldsymbol{Y}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
.

Let
\rho_{s}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
be the
(nonzero) discriminant of
V_{s}
, as
defined in (
3.3
). Now we address the more delicate
condition: for any
point
\boldsymbol{p}\in V_{\boldsymbol{p}^{*}}
, the morphism
\pi_{s}
must be
unramified at
\pi_{s}(\boldsymbol{p})
. Considering both
{B}_{s}^{D}
and

\rho_{s}
as elements of
k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}]
,
we have the following result.

Lemma 22
.

The Zariski closure of
(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s+1})\cap\{(\rho_{s}{B}_{s}^{D})(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X})=0,A_{s+1}\not=0\}
is empty or has pure dimension

(n-s+1)(n+1)-3
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s+1}
has a cylindrical structure,
no irreducible component is contained
in
\{A_{s}=0\}
, so the open set
\{A_{s}\not=0\}
intersects
each component densely. Suppose by contradiction that there
exists an irreducible component
\mathcal{D}
of

\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s+1}
such that

\mathcal{D}\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}\subset\Phi_{s}^{-1}(\{\rho_{s}B_{s}^{D}=0\}).

Then

\Phi_{s}(\mathcal{D}\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\})\subset\Phi_{s}\circ\Phi_{s}^{-1}(\{\rho_{s}B_{s}^{D}=0\})\subset\{\rho_{s}B_{s}^{D}=0\}\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}.

Observe that

\mathcal{D}
can be expressed as
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathcal{D}_{0}
, where
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is an irreducible component of

V_{s+1}
. Let
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
be a nonsingular point of

V_{s+1}
with
G(\boldsymbol{x})\not=0
, which is also a nonsingular point of
V_{s}
. Hence, for a
generic choice of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}
, we have
A_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\not=0
, the fiber

W_{s}:=V_{s}\cap\{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}\}
is unramified (see, e.g.,
(
Mumford 1995
, §5A)
), and the
linear form
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n-s+1}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
separates the points
of
W_{s}
. This shows that any point
\boldsymbol{y}\in V_{s}\cap\{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}\}
satisfies the
conditions
D_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{y})\not=0
and
\rho_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{y})\not=0
. We conclude that the point
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{x})
belongs to the set

\Phi_{s}(\mathcal{D}\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\})\setminus(\{\rho_{s}B_{s}^{D}=0\}\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}),

contradicting the inclusion

\Phi_{s}(\mathcal{D}\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\})\subset\{\rho_{s}B_{s}^{D}=0\}\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}
.

Hence,
\mathcal{D}\cap\{\rho_{s}B_{s}^{D}=0\}
is empty or has pure
dimension
(n-s+1)(n+1)-3
for any irreducible component

\mathcal{D}
of
\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s+1}
, which readily proves
the lemma.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the main result of this section, namely,
we show that there exists a polynomial condition of
“low” degree which guarantees that, for any choice of the coefficients
defining the linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}
, and the points

\boldsymbol{p}^{s}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})
and
\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s-1})
,
satisfying this condition, all the required properties involving

Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}
,
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}
and
\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}
are fulfilled.

Theorem 23
.

With notations as in Proposition

18
, fix
s
with
1\leq s<r
. There exists a nonzero polynomial
B_{s}\in\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
, of degree at most

2(n-s+2)nd\delta_{s}\delta_{s+1}\max\{\delta_{s},\delta_{s+1}\}
, such
that for any
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s}

with
B_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\not=0
, the following conditions are
satisfied: if
\boldsymbol{Y}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
, then:

1.

The morphism
\pi_{s}:V_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
defined by

Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}
is finite, the point
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})\in\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
is a lifting point of
\pi_{s}
,
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\subset\{G\not=0\}
, and
Y_{n-s+1}
is a primitive element of

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
.

2.

Let
\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s-1})\in\mathbb{A}^{n-s-1}
. Then the morphism

\pi_{s+1}:V_{s+1}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s-1}
defined by
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s-1}
is
finite, the point
\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}
is a lifting point of
\pi_{s+1}
,

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\subset\{G\not=0\}
, and
Y_{n-s}
is a
primitive element of
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
.

3.

Every point
\boldsymbol{q}\in\pi_{s}\big(\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\big)
is a
lifting point of
\pi_{s}
and
Y_{n-s+1}
is a primitive element of

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{q})
for any
\boldsymbol{q}\in\pi_{s}\big(\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\big)
.

4.

No point
\boldsymbol{q}\in\pi_{s}\big(\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\cap\{G=0\}\big)

belongs to
\pi_{s}\big(\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\big)
.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
21
,
we may assume without loss of generality that
all the sets appearing in its statement have
pure codimension 1 in
(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s})\cap\{A_{s}\not=0\}
and

(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s)n}\times V_{s+1})\cap\{A_{s+1}\not=0\}
, respectively.
It follows that
there exist polynomials
B_{s}^{D},B_{s}^{G}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
and

B_{s+1}^{D},B_{s+1}^{G}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s-1}]
of minimal degree
such that the following identities hold:

\displaystyle\Phi_{s}(\{D_{s}=0\})

\displaystyle=\{B_{s}^{D}=0,A_{s}\not=0\},

(3.10)

\displaystyle\Phi_{s}(\{G=0\})

\displaystyle=\{G=0,A_{s}\not=0\},

(3.11)

\displaystyle\Phi_{s+1}(\{D_{s+1}=0\})

\displaystyle=\{B_{s+1}^{D}=0,A_{s+1}\not=0\},

\displaystyle\Phi_{s+1}(\{G=0\})

\displaystyle=\{G=0,A_{s+1}\not=0\}.

By Lemma
20
, the following degree bounds
are satisfied:

\displaystyle\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}}B_{s}^{D}

\displaystyle\leq s(d-1)\delta_{s},\hskip 56.9055pt\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}B_{s}^{D}\leq(n-s)(1+s(d-1))\delta_{s},

\displaystyle\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}}B_{s}^{G}

\displaystyle\leq d\delta_{s},\hskip 89.626pt\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}B_{s}^{G}\leq(n-s)d\delta_{s},

\displaystyle\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}}B_{s+1}^{D}

\displaystyle\leq(s+1)(d-1)\delta_{s+1},\quad\ \ \deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}B_{s+1}^{D}\leq(n-s-1)(1+(s+1)(d-1))\delta_{s+1},

\displaystyle\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}}B_{s+1}^{G}

\displaystyle\leq d\delta_{s+1},\hskip 78.24507pt\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}B_{s+1}^{G}\leq(n-s-1)d\delta_{s+1}.

We have also the following degree bounds for the discriminants:

\displaystyle\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}}\rho_{s}\leq\delta_{s}(2\delta_{s}-1),\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\rho_{s}\leq(n-s+1)\delta_{s}(2\delta_{s}-1),

\displaystyle\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}}\deg\rho_{s+1}\leq\delta_{s+1}(2\delta_{s+1}-1),

\displaystyle\quad\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\rho_{s+1}\leq(n-s)\delta_{s+1}(2\delta_{s+1}-1).

We will show that the nonvanishing of the polynomials
A_{s}
and

A_{s+1}
of Proposition
18
and
B_{s}^{D}
,
B_{s+1}^{D}
,
B_{s}^{G}
,
B_{s+1}^{G}
,
\rho_{s}
,

\rho_{s+1}
guarantees that conditions (
1
) and (
2
) are
fulfilled.

To address condition (
3
), in view of Lemma
22
,
we may assume without loss of generality
that the set

(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s+1})\cap\{(\rho_{s}B_{s}^{D})(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X})=0,A_{s+1}\not=0\}

has pure codimension 1 in
(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s+1})\cap\{A_{s+1}\not=0\}
. Define the map

\displaystyle\Psi_{s}:(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times V_{s+1})\cap\{A_{s+1}\not=0\}

\displaystyle\to(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s-1})\cap\{A_{s+1}\not=0\},

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda},Y_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}),\ldots,Y_{n-s-1}(\boldsymbol{x})\big).

Arguing as in Lemma
19
, one sees that

\Psi_{s}
is a finite morphism. Hence, the image

\Psi_{s}(\{(\rho_{s}B_{s}^{D})(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X})=0\})
is
a hypersurface of
(\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s-1})\cap\{A_{s+1}\not=0\}
,
defined by a minimal-degree polynomial

\widetilde{B}_{s}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s-1}]
, namely:

\Psi_{s}(\{(\rho_{s}B_{s}^{D})(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X})=0\})=\{\widetilde{B}_{s}=0,A_{s+1}=0\}.

(3.12)

By Lemma
20
, we have

\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{*}}\widetilde{B}_{s}\leq(s(d-1)+2\delta_{s})\delta_{s}\delta_{s+1},\quad\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\widetilde{B}_{s}\leq 2(n-s+1)(s(d-1)+2\delta_{s})\delta_{s}\delta_{s+1}.

The nonvanishing of
\widetilde{B}_{s}
will account for the
fulfillment of condition (
3
).

Now consider condition (
4
). Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
be such that conditions (
1
) and (
2
) are satisfied. Since the morphism
\pi_{s}:V_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
is finite, by Fact

14
it follows that

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
is of pure dimension 1.
Furthermore, it has degree at most
\delta_{s}
, because

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})=V_{s}\cap\{Y_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s-1}=p_{n-s-1}\}

is a linear
section of
V_{s}
. Observe that

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})=\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\cap\{Y_{n-s}=p_{n-s}\}

is a
zero–dimensional linear section of
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
. The fact that
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}\in k^{n-s}
is a lifting point of

\pi_{s}
implies
\#\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})=\delta_{s}
. In particular,

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
intersects each irreducible component of

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
. Indeed, let

\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{t}
be the irreducible components
of
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
. We have

\displaystyle\delta_{s}=\#\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{t}\#(\mathcal{C}_{i}\cap\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\#(\mathcal{C}_{i}\cap\{Y_{n-s}=p_{n-s}\})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{t}\deg\mathcal{C}_{i}=\deg\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\leq\delta_{s}.

This proves that all the inequalities above are equalities, which shows our claim.

As
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\subset\{G\not=0\}
, we see that
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\cap\{G=0\}
is of dimension at most 0. In particular, a
generic linear form
Y_{n-s}
separates the points of

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\cap\{G=0\}
from those of

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\subset\{G\not=0\}
. It follows that the polynomials

B_{s}^{G}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]

and
P_{V_{s+1}}\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
(of the proof of Lemma
17
)
share no common factors in

k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s-1}][\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}]
, and the
resultant

\widehat{B}_{s}:=\mathrm{Res}_{\widetilde{Y}_{n-s}}(B_{s}^{G},P_{V_{s+1}})\in k[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}][\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-s-1}]

is nonzero, with the following degree bounds:

\deg_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}}\widehat{B}_{s}\leq 2d\delta_{s}\delta_{s+1},\qquad\deg_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\widehat{B}_{s}\leq 2(n-s)d\delta_{s}\delta_{s+1}.

Let

B_{s}:=A_{s}A_{s+1}\rho_{s}\rho_{s+1}B_{s}^{D}B_{s+1}^{D}B_{s}^{G}B_{s+1}^{G}\widetilde{B}_{s}\widehat{B}_{s}.

From the bounds above we deduce that

\deg B_{s}\leq 2(n-s+2)nd\delta_{s}\delta_{s+1}\max\{\delta_{s},\delta_{s+1}\}.

Now suppose
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\in\mathbb{A}^{(n-s+1)(n+1)}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
is
such that
B_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\not=0
. We claim that

(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
satisfies all the conditions of the theorem.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}
be the submatrix of the first
n-s
rows of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
,
and let
\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}
denote the first
n-s-1

coordinates of
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}
. Since
A_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})A_{s+1}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})\not=0
, from Proposition
18
we conclude that the morphisms

\pi_{s}:V_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
and
\pi_{s+1}:V_{s+1}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s-1}
, defined
by the linear forms
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
and

(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s-1}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
, are
finite. Since
A_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\not=0
, from
(
3.10
) it follows that the condition

B_{s}^{D}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\not=0
implies
D_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{x})\not=0
for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}

is a lifting point of
\pi_{s}
. Similarly, the point

\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}
is a lifting point of
\pi_{s+1}
. The conditions

\rho_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\not=0
and
\rho_{s+1}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\not=0
ensure that
Y_{n-s+1}
and
Y_{n-s}
are
primitive elements of
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
and
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
, respectively. Finally, as
B_{s}^{G}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\not=0
, by (
3.11
) it follows that

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\subset\{G\not=0\}
. Similarly, the
condition
B_{s+1}^{G}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\not=0
implies
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\subset\{G\not=0\}
.
As a consequence,
conditions (
1
) and (
2
) of the statement of the theorem are
satisfied.

On the other hand, from (
3.12
) it follows that the conditions

\widetilde{B}_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\not=0
and

A_{s+1}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})\not=0
imply

(\rho_{s}B_{s}^{D})\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},Y_{n-s}(\boldsymbol{x})\big)\not=0

for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
. Therefore, as

A_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\not=0
, from (
3.10
)
we deduce that
D_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{q})\not=0
,
and
\rho_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\pi_{s}(\boldsymbol{q}))\not=0
, for any
\boldsymbol{q}\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},Y_{n-s}(\boldsymbol{x}))
with
\boldsymbol{x}\in\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
. This shows that condition
(
3
) holds.

Finally, as
conditions (
1
) and (
2
) hold, the remarks above show that

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\cap\{G=0\}
has dimension at
most 0. Furthermore, we have
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{q})\in\Phi_{s}(\{G=0\})
for any
\boldsymbol{q}\in\pi_{s}\big(\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\cap\{G=0\}\big)
, which implies

B_{s}^{G}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{q})=0
by (
3.11
). Each such point
\boldsymbol{q}
can be
expressed as
\boldsymbol{q}=(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\xi)
with
\xi\in\overline{k}
and

B_{s}^{G}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\xi)=0
. On the other hand,

P_{V_{s+1}}\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\pi_{s}(\boldsymbol{q})\big)=P_{V_{s+1}}\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},Y_{n-s}(\boldsymbol{q})\big)=0

for each point
\boldsymbol{q}\in\pi_{s}(\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}))
. Since

\widehat{B}_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\not=0
, the polynomials

B_{s}^{G}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},Y_{n-s})
and

P_{V_{s+1}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},Y_{n-s})
have no common roots in

\overline{k}
, which shows that condition (
4
) is satisfied.
∎

We conclude this section with a result that summarizes the conclusions
of Theorem
23
for

1\leq s<r
, namely, it asserts the existence
of a low-degree condition that must be satisfied by the
coefficients of linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
, and the
coordinates of a point
\boldsymbol{p}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1})
, in order
for all the requirements of Theorem
23

to be satisfied for
1\leq s<r
.

Corollary 24
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}:=(\Lambda_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
be a matrix of indeterminates over

\overline{k}
, and let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
. There
exists a nonzero polynomial
B\in\overline{k}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{n-1}]
of degree at most

2n^{2}rd\delta^{3}
such
that for any
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathbb{A}^{n^{2}}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}

with
B(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\not=0
, if
\boldsymbol{Y}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s+1}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
, then conditions
(
1
), (
2
),
(
3
),
(
4
) of Theorem
23
are satisfied
for
1\leq s<r
.

Proof.

Let

B:=\prod_{s=1}^{r-1}B_{s},

where
B_{s}
is the polynomial from
Theorem
23
for
1\leq s<r
. We have

\deg B=\sum_{s=1}^{r-1}\deg B_{s}\leq\sum_{s=1}^{r-1}2(n-s+2)nd\delta_{s}\delta_{s+1}\max\{\delta_{s},\delta_{s+1}\}\leq 2n^{2}rd\delta^{3}.

By Theorem
23
we immediately
deduce the corollary.
∎

4
Lifting curves

Let the notations and assumptions be as in Section
3
. The algorithm we describe proceeds in
r

steps. For each
1\leq s<r
, the
s
th step takes as input a Kronecker
representation of a lifting fiber of the variety
V_{s}
, and outputs
a Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber of
V_{s+1}
.

Fix
s
with
1\leq s<r
. From this point on, we assume that the variables

X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
satisfy all the conditions stated in Theorem
23
. In particular, this implies that the
morphisms

\begin{array}[]{rclrcl}\pi_{s}:V_{s}&\to&\mathbb{A}^{n-s},&\quad\pi_{s+1}:V_{s+1}&\to&\mathbb{A}^{n-s-1},\\
\boldsymbol{x}&\mapsto&(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-s})&\boldsymbol{x}&\mapsto&(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-s-1})\end{array}

are finite and generically unramified, and satisfy

\deg\pi_{s}=\delta_{s}:=\deg V_{s},\qquad\deg\pi_{s+1}=\delta_{s+1}:=\deg V_{s+1}.

Denote

\displaystyle R_{s}:=k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n-s}],\qquad

\displaystyle A_{s}:=k[V_{s}],\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle K_{s}:=k(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n-s}),\qquad

\displaystyle B_{s}:=K_{s}\otimes A_{s}.

According to Proposition
18
and its proof, we have:

•

A_{s}
is an integral ring extension of
R_{s}
,

•

B_{s}
is a

K_{s}
–vector space of dimension
\deg\pi_{s}=\delta_{s}
.

Moreover:

•

k[V_{s}]
is a Cohen-Macaulay ring, and
a free
R_{s}
-module of finite rank (see
Giusti et al. 1993
).

For any
F\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, let
\eta_{F}:A_{s}\to A_{s}
denote the

R_{s}
-linear endomorphism of multiplication by
F
. Denote by
m_{F}\in K_{s}[T]
the
minimal polynomial of
\eta_{F}
. Our assumptions imply that
m_{F}
is a
monic polynomial in
R_{s}[T]
.

Let
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})\in k^{n-s}
be a point satisfying the
hypotheses of Theorem
23
. In
particular, the following conditions hold:

({\sf A}_{1})

The fiber
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
is unramified,

({\sf A}_{2})

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\subset\{G\not=0\}
,

({\sf A}_{3})

X_{n-s+1}
separates the points of
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
.

Suppose we are given univariate polynomials
m_{s},v_{n-s+2}^{s},\ldots,v_{n}^{s}\in k[T]
, with
\deg m_{s}=\delta_{s}
and
\deg v_{i}^{s}<\delta_{s}

for
n-s+2\leq i\leq n
, defining a Kronecker representation of

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
with
X_{n-s+1}
as primitive element, namely,

\displaystyle\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})=\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s}\}\times\{(x_{n-s+1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{A}^{s}:\

\displaystyle m_{s}(x_{n-s+1})=0,

\displaystyle x_{n-s+i}=v_{n-s+i}^{s}(x_{n-s+1})\ \,(2\leq i\leq s)\}.

By Theorem
23
, the point

\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s-1})
satisfies the following properties:

({\sf B}_{1})

The fiber
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
is unramified,

({\sf B}_{2})

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\subset\{G\not=0\}
,

({\sf B}_{3})

X_{n-s}
separates the points of
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{*})
,

({\sf B}_{4})

For every
\boldsymbol{q}\in\pi_{s}\big(\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\big)
,
the fiber
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{q})
is unramified and
X_{n-s+1}

separates its points.

The
s
th recursive step of the algorithm consists of
two main phases: lifting and elimination. In this section, we focus
on the lifting phase, which produces a Kronecker representation of the
variety

C_{s}:=\pi_{s}^{-1}\left(\{X_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,X_{n-s-1}=p_{n-s-1}\}\right).

(4.1)

As will become clear,
C_{s}
is of pure dimension 1
—that is, it is a curve. We refer to
C_{s}
as the

lifting curve
associated
with
\pi_{s}
and the point
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}
.

4.1
Properties of the
s
th lifting curve

In this section, we establish several properties of the variety

C_{s}
defined in (
4.1
). We begin by showing
that
C_{s}
is a curve of degree
\delta_{s}
, and that the projection
onto its
(n-s)
th coordinate defines a suitable direction of
projection.

Proposition 25
.

The variety
C_{s}
from (
4.1
) has pure
dimension 1 and degree
\delta_{s}
, and the morphism

\pi_{s}^{*}:C_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
, given by
\pi_{s}^{*}(\boldsymbol{x}):=x_{n-s}
, is finite
of degree
\delta_{s}
. Furthermore, let
M_{s}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
denote
the minimal polynomial of
X_{n-s+1}
in the
k(X_{n-s})
-algebra
extension
k(X_{n-s})\hookrightarrow k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]
. Then
M_{s}
is a monic
polynomial in
k[X_{n-s}][T]
of degree
\deg_{T}M_{s}=\deg M_{s}=\delta_{s}
. Moreover,
M_{s}
is a separable element of

k(X_{n-s})[T]
, and
M_{s}
and its derivative
\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}
are relatively prime in
k(X_{n-s})[T]
. In
particular,
\rho_{s}:=\mathrm{disc}_{T}M_{s}\in k[X_{n-s}]\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

Since
\pi_{s}
is a finite morphism and
\{X_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,X_{n-s-1}=p_{n-s-1}\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
is an irreducible variety of
dimension 1, the first claim about
C_{s}
follows from Fact
14
. As
C_{s}=V_{s}\cap\{X_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,X_{n-s-1}=p_{n-s-1}\}
, the Bézout inequality (
2.1
) implies
\deg C_{s}\leq\deg V_{s}=\delta_{s}
. Moreover, since
the fiber
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
is unramified and
\deg\pi_{s}=\delta_{s}
, we have

\#\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})=\delta_{s}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\delta_{s}=\deg\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})

\displaystyle=\deg C_{s}\cap\{X_{n-s}=p_{n-s}\}\leq\deg C_{s},

where the last inequality also follows from the Bézout
inequality (
2.1
). This proves the second assertion on
C_{s}
.

Since
\pi_{s}:V_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
is a finite morphism, it follows that

\pi_{s}^{*}
is finite as well. In particular, we obtain an
integral ring extension
k[X_{n-s}]\hookrightarrow k[C_{s}]
and
a finite-dimensional
k(X_{n-s})
–algebra extension
k(X_{n-s})\hookrightarrow k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]
. This extension arises from
specializing the
K_{s}
–algebra extension
K_{s}\hookrightarrow K_{s}\otimes_{R_{s}}k[V_{s}]
, and since the latter is a

K_{s}
–vector space of dimension
\delta_{s}
, it follows that

k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]
must be a
k(X_{n-s})
-vector space of
dimension at most
\delta_{s}
.

Furthermore, as the fiber

(\pi_{s}^{*})^{-1}(p_{n-s})=\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
has cardinality

\delta_{s}
, and
X_{n-s+1}
separates the
points in
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, the coordinate
X_{n-s+1}
takes
\delta_{s}

distinct values on this fiber.
Since
M_{s}
vanishes on
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\subset C_{s}
, it follows that
the polynomial
M_{s}(p_{n-s},T)
has
\delta_{s}
distinct
roots in
\overline{k}
. Hence, the inequalities

\delta_{s}\leq\deg M_{s}(p_{n-s},T)\leq\deg_{T}M_{s}\leq\delta_{s}

must all be equalities. This shows that

k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]
is a

k(X_{n-s})
–vector space of dimension
\delta_{s}
.

The equality
\deg M_{s}=\delta_{s}
is a consequence of, e.g.,
(
Sabia and Solernó 1996
, Proposition
1)
. In particular, we have
\deg M_{s}(p_{n-s},T)=\deg_{T}M_{s}=\deg M_{s}
.
Since
M_{s}(p_{n-s},T)
is square-free, and thus separable,
we conclude that
M_{s}
is square-free as well, and separable as a polynomial
in
k(X_{n-s})[T]
. Moreover, since
M_{s}(p_{n-s},T)

and
\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(p_{n-s},T)
are
coprime in
k[T]
, it follows that
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
and

\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},T)
are coprime in

k(X_{n-s})[T]
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Next, we discuss the remaining polynomials forming the
Kronecker representation of
C_{s}
that we aim to compute.
By specializing (
3.8
) we deduce
the existence of polynomials
W_{n-s+2}^{s},\ldots,W_{n}^{s}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
, of
degree at most
\delta_{s}-1
, such that

\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})\cdot X_{n-s+i}\equiv W_{n-s+i}^{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})

(4.2)

in the coordinate ring of
C_{s}
, for
2\leq i\leq s
. The polynomials
M_{s},W_{n-s+2}^{s},\ldots,W_{n}^{s}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
thus provide a Kronecker representation of
C_{s}
, with

X_{n-s+1}
as primitive element. In the following, we describe
an algorithm for computing such a representation.

It is also useful to note that, by specializing (
3.7
), we obtain polynomials
V_{n-s+2}^{s},\ldots,V_{n}^{s}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
, of degree at most
\delta_{s}-1
, such that

\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})\cdot X_{n-s+i}\equiv V_{n-s+i}^{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})

(4.3)

in
C_{s}
for
2\leq i\leq s
, where

\rho_{s}:=\mathrm{Res}_{T}(M_{s},\partial M_{s}/\partial T)
with a slight
abuse of notation.

We now explicitly describe the birational morphism between the
lifting curve
C_{s}
and the hypersurface
\{M_{s}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}

associated to the Kronecker representation above (compare with

(
Cafure and Matera 2006
, Lemma 3.5)
for closed sets over finite fields).

Lemma 26
.

Let

\pi_{s,s+1}:C_{s}\to\{M_{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2},\quad\pi_{s,s+1}(\boldsymbol{x}):=(x_{n-s},x_{n-s+1}).

Then
\pi_{s,s+1}
is a birational
mapping, which restricts to an isomorphism between the Zariski open dense
subsets
C_{s}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})=0\}
of
C_{s}
and

\{M_{s}=0\}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})=0\}
of
\{M_{s}=0\}
. Moreover,
the inverse mapping

\psi:\{M_{s}=0\}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})=0\}\to C_{s}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})=0\}

is
given by:

\psi(x_{n-s},x_{n-s+1}):=\left(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},x_{n-s},x_{n-s+1},\frac{W_{n-s+2}^{s}(x_{n-s},x_{n-s+1})}{\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(x_{n-s},x_{n-s+1})},\ldots,\frac{W_{n}^{s}(x_{n-s},x_{n-s+1})}{\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(x_{n-s},x_{n-s+1})}\right).

Proof.

The morphism
\pi_{s,s+1}
is well-defined since

M_{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})
vanishes identically on
C_{s}
, and it maps

C_{s}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})=0\}
to
\{M_{s}=0\}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})=0\}
. The identity (
4.3
) ensures that
\pi_{s,s+1}
is injective on

C_{s}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})=0\}
.

To show surjectivity, it suffices to verify that
\psi
is
well-defined.
Let
(x_{n-s},x_{n-s+1})\in\mathbb{A}^{2}
be such that

M_{s}(x_{n-s},x_{n-s+1})=0
and
\rho_{s}(x_{n-s})\not=0
.
Then
\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(x_{n-s},x_{n-s+1})\not=0
. Let
H\in I(C_{s})
,
and set

H_{1}:=(\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T})^{\deg H}H
. Then we may
write

H_{1}=H_{2}\Big(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n-s+1},\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}X_{n-s+2},\ldots,\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}X_{n},\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}\Big)

for some

H_{2}\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},X_{n+1}]
. Since
H_{1}\in I(C_{s})
,
identity (
4.2
)
implies that

H_{2}\Big(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1},W_{n-s+2}^{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1}),\ldots,W_{n}^{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1}),\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})\Big)

vanishes on
C_{s}
, and hence is divisible
by
M_{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})
. It follows that
H_{1}
vanishes
on the image of
\psi
, and therefore so does
H
. Thus,
\psi

maps
\{M_{s}=0\}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})=0\}
into

C_{s}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})=0\}
, and from (
4.2
) we see that
\psi

is the inverse map of
\pi_{s,s+1}
restricted to
C_{s}\setminus\{\rho(X_{n-s}=0\}
. This completes the proof.
∎

We now include a simple observation that will be used
later.

Remark 27
.

The fiber
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})=C_{s}\cap\{X_{n-s}=p_{n-s}\}
intersects
every irreducible component of
C_{s}
.

Proof.

Let
C_{s}=\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{C}_{i}
be the decomposition
of
C_{s}
into irreducible components. Since
