C_{s}\cap\{X_{n-s}=p_{n-s}\}=\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, the intersection

\mathcal{C}_{i}\cap\{X_{n-s}=p_{n-s}\}
has dimension at most zero.
Moreover,

\delta_{s}=\#\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})\leq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\#(\mathcal{C}_{i}\cap\{X_{n-s}=p_{n-s}\})\leq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\deg\mathcal{C}_{i}=\deg C_{s}=\delta_{s}.

Hence, all inequalities must be equalities, which implies that

\mathcal{C}_{i}\cap\{X_{n-s}=p_{n-s}\}
has cardinality equal to

\deg\mathcal{C}_{i}
, which is positive for each
i\in\mathcal{I}
.
∎

We recall that, in addition to the conditions
({\sf B}_{1})
–
({\sf B}_{4})

listed above,
the point
\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}
is assumed to satisfy the following condition related to
the lifting curve
C_{s}
:

({\sf B}_{5})

No point
\boldsymbol{q}\in\pi_{s}\big(C_{s}\cap\{G=0\}\big)
belongs to

\pi_{s}\big(\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\big)
.

The following result, which adapts and extends
(
Cafure and Matera 2006
, Lemma
4.1)
to our setting, will be essential for proving the
correctness of the procedure for computing a Kronecker
representation of the lifting curve
C_{s}
. Observe that the image of
the map
C_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{s+1}
,
\boldsymbol{x}\mapsto(x_{n-s},\ldots,x_{n})
, is
isomorphic to
C_{s}
. For simplicity of notations, we will henceforth
use
C_{s}
to denote either the curve of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
as defined in
(
4.1
), or its image under this projection.
The next result shows that we are in a position to apply the
Newton-Hensel process described in Section
4.2
.

Proposition 28
.

With the above notations and assumptions, the following
statements hold:

1.

The polynomials
F_{j}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})

(1\leq j\leq s)
form a regular sequence in the localized ring
k[X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})}
, and they generate the localization of the ideal of

C_{s}
in this ring.

2.

The morphism
\pi_{s}^{*}:C_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
, defined by
\pi_{s}^{*}(x_{n-s},\ldots,x_{n}):=x_{n-s}
,
is finite and generically unramified, and its fiber over
p_{n-s}
,
namely
(\pi_{s}^{*})^{-1}(p_{n-s})
, is unramified and has cardinality

\delta_{s}=\deg C_{s}
.

Proof.

We first prove that the polynomials
F_{j}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s}\ldots X_{n})

(1\leq j\leq s)
form a regular sequence in
k[X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})}
. To this end, by

(
Matsumura 1986
, Theorem 17.6)
, it suffices to show that, for each

1\leq i\leq s
,

Z_{i}:=\overline{\{F_{j}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s}\ldots X_{n})=0:1\leq j\leq i\}\setminus\{G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})=0\}}

has pure
dimension
s+1-i
.

Let
L_{s+1}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
be the linear subvariety defined by the
equations
X_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,X_{n-s-1}=p_{n-s-1}
. Then

Z_{i}=V_{i}\cap L_{s+1}=\pi_{i}^{-1}(\pi_{i}(L_{s+1}))
for
1\leq i\leq s
.
Since
\pi_{i}:V_{i}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-i}
is a finite morphism and

\pi_{i}(L_{s+1})\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-i}
is an irreducible variety of
dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{A}^{n-i}}\pi_{i}(L_{s+1})=n-i-(n-s-1)=s+1-i
, it
follows from Fact

14
that
Z_{i}
has
pure dimension
s+1-i
, as desired.

Now we show that the polynomials
F_{j}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\dots,X_{n})

(1\leq j\leq s)
generate the ideal of
C_{s}
in the localized ring
k[X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G(\boldsymbol{p}^{*},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})}
. By definition, we have

C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\}=(V_{s}\setminus\{G=0\})\cap\{X_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,X_{n-s-1}=p_{n-s-1}\}.

Hence, the common set of zeros in
\mathbb{A}^{n}\setminus\{G=0\}
of the polynomials

F_{j}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\dots,X_{n})

(1\leq j\leq s)
is precisely

C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\}
. Since
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}
is a lifting point of
\pi_{s}
,
the Jacobian determinant

J_{F}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}):=\det\big(\partial F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})/\partial X_{n-s+j}\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq s}

does not vanish at any point of

C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\}\cap\{X_{n-s}=p_{n-s}\}=\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
.
By Remark
27
, this implies that the coordinate function of

C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\}
defined by
J_{F}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})

does not vanish identically on any irreducible component of

C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\}
, and is therefore not a zero divisor of

k[C_{s}]_{G}
. By
(
Eisenbud 1995
, Theorem 18.15)
,
the ideal generated by the polynomials
F_{j}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})

(1\leq j\leq s)
is radical in
k[X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})}
, and thus it coincides with the ideal of
C_{s}
in that ring.

We finally prove the claims about the morphism
\pi^{*}_{s}
. Since
\pi_{s}

is a finite morphism of degree
\delta_{s}
, its restriction to
C_{s}

induces a finite morphism
\pi^{*}_{s}:C_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
of degree at most
\delta_{s}
.
Moreover, because the fiber
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
is unramified and has
cardinality
\delta_{s}
, the fiber
(\pi_{s}^{*})^{-1}(p_{n-s})

is also unramified of cardinality
\delta_{s}
, and it follows that

\pi_{s}^{*}
is generically unramified and has degree exactly
\delta_{s}
.
∎

4.2
A Kronecker representation of the
s
th lifting curve

According to Lemma
26
,
there is an isomorphism between the Zariski open dense subsets

C_{s}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})=0\}
of
C_{s}
and

\{M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)=0\}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})=0\}
of

\{M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}
. This allows us to
represent
C_{s}
in terms of the zeros of
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
.

The zeros of
M_{s}
can be represented using the well–known Hensel
lemma (see, e.g.,
(
Abhyankar 1990
, Lecture 12)
). More precisely,
the facts that
M_{s}
is a monic element of
k[X_{n-s}][T]
of degree

\delta_{s}
, and that

\rho_{s}(X_{n-s}):=\mathrm{Res}_{T}\left(M_{s}(X_{n-s},T),\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},T)\right)\not=0,

imply that
M_{s}
admits a factorization of the form

M_{s}=\prod_{j=1}^{\delta_{s}}(T-\sigma_{j})

in
\overline{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T],

where
\overline{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
denotes the ring of
formal power series in
X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}
. We will see that
approximations of order
\delta_{s}
of

\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\delta_{s}}
are sufficient for our purposes, and
discuss how such approximations can be computed.

Let
\boldsymbol{F}_{s}:=(F_{1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}),\ldots,F_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}))
, and let

J_{s}(X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}):=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\tfrac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial X_{n-s+1}}&\ldots&\tfrac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial X_{n}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\tfrac{\partial F_{s}}{\partial X_{n-s+1}}&\ldots&\tfrac{\partial F_{s}}{\partial X_{n}}\end{array}\right)(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})

be the Jacobian matrix of
\boldsymbol{F}_{s}
with respect to
X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n}
. Considering
\boldsymbol{F}_{s}
and
J_{s}
as elements of

k[X_{n-s}][X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, the Newton–Hensel operator
associated to
\boldsymbol{F}_{s}
is the
s
–tuple
N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}
of
rational functions of
k(X_{n-s})(X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n})
defined
by

N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}(X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n})^{t}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X_{n-s+1}\\
\vdots\\
X_{n}\end{array}\right)-\big(J_{s}^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}_{s}^{t}\big)(X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n}),

(4.4)

where
t
denotes transposition.

As a consequence of Proposition

28
, we have the following
result, showing that, starting from an arbitrary point of

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, the
j
–th iteration of
N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}
is
well-defined. In the following,
(X_{n-s}-p_{n-s})
denotes the maximal ideal of
\overline{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
generated by
X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}
. The proof of this
result follows
mutatis mutandis
the proof of
Assertions (I) and (II) in
Heintz et al. 2000
.

Proposition 29
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\xi}:=(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},\xi_{n-s+1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
. For each
j\geq 0
, consider the

s
–tuple
\boldsymbol{R}^{(j,\boldsymbol{\xi})}:=(R_{n-s+1}^{(j,\boldsymbol{\xi})},\ldots,R_{n}^{(j,\boldsymbol{\xi})})\in\overline{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]^{s}

defined recursively by

\boldsymbol{R}^{(0,\boldsymbol{\xi})}:=(\xi_{n-s+1},\ldots,\xi_{n}),\qquad\boldsymbol{R}^{(j+1,\boldsymbol{\xi})}:=N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}(\boldsymbol{R}^{(j,\boldsymbol{\xi})}).

Then the sequence
(\boldsymbol{R}^{(j,\boldsymbol{\xi})})_{j\geq 0}
is
well–defined, and the following properties hold for each
j\geq 0
:

1.

F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{R}^{(j,\boldsymbol{\xi})})\in(X_{n-s}-p_{n-s})^{2^{j}}
for
1\leq i\leq s
;

2.

\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{R}^{(j,\boldsymbol{\xi})})\notin(X_{n-s}-p_{n-s})
;

3.

R_{i}^{(j+1,\boldsymbol{\xi})}\equiv R_{i}^{(j,\boldsymbol{\xi})}\mod(X_{n-s}-p_{n-s})^{2^{j}}
for
n-s+2\leq i\leq n
.

From Proposition
29
, the
Newton–Hensel theorem follows, ensuring the existence of the
parametrizations by power series for the branches of
C_{s}
near
each point of a lifting fiber
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
.
More precisely, we have the following result, whose proof
follows
mutatis mutandis
that of
(
Heintz et al. 2000
, Lemma
3)
.

Theorem 30
.

For any point
\boldsymbol{\xi}:=(\boldsymbol{p},\xi_{n-s+1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, there exists a
unique
s
–tuple of formal power series
\boldsymbol{R}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}}:=(R_{n-s+1}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\ldots,R_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\in\overline{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]^{s}
satisfying:

•

F_{1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{R}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}})=0,\ldots,F_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{R}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}})=0
;

•

\boldsymbol{R}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(p_{n-s})=(\xi_{n-s+1},\ldots,\xi_{n})
.

Condition
({\sf A}_{2})
asserts that
G(\boldsymbol{\xi})\not=0
for any

\boldsymbol{\xi}:=(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},\xi_{n-s+1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
.
Thus, by the second claim of Theorem
30
, it
follows that, for all
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
,

G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{R}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\not=0.

Collecting the
\delta_{s}
vectors of power series
\boldsymbol{R}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
yields a description of
C_{s}
to compute a Kronecker
representation as in (
4.2
). More precisely:

Lemma 31
.

With the notations of Theorem

30
, we have the factorization

M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)=\prod_{\xi\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p})}(T-R_{n-s+1}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}})

(4.5)

in
\overline{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
.

Proof.

Since
M_{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})
vanishes on
C_{s}
, by
Proposition
28
we conclude
that it belongs to the (radical) ideal generated by the
polynomials
F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})

(1\leq i\leq s)
in

k[X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}]_{G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
. Since
F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{R}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}})=0
for
1\leq i\leq s
and
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{R}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\not=0
, we obtain

M_{s}(X_{n-s},R_{n-s+1}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}})=0.

Moreover, as
\boldsymbol{R}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(p_{n-s})=(\xi_{n-s+1},\ldots,\xi_{n})
for
all
\boldsymbol{\xi}:=(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},\xi_{n-s+1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
,
and
X_{n-s+1}
separates the points of
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, it
follows that
R_{n-s+1}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\neq R_{n-s+1}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}
for

\boldsymbol{\xi}\neq\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}
. Thus, the terms
T-R_{n-s+1}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
are coprime,
implying that their product
divides
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
. Since both sides are monic polynomials of
the same degree, the lemma follows.
∎

Proposition
29
shows that, starting at

\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, the
j
–th iteration of the
Newton-Hensel operator
N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}
yields a vector of power
series
\boldsymbol{R}^{(j,\boldsymbol{\xi})}\in\overline{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]^{s}
satisfying
\boldsymbol{R}^{(j,\boldsymbol{\xi})}\equiv\boldsymbol{R}^{\boldsymbol{\xi}}

modulo
(X_{n-s}-p_{n-s})^{2^{j}}
for any
j\geq 0
. It follows that

M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)\equiv\prod_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})}(T-R_{n-s+1}^{(j,\boldsymbol{\xi})})\mod(X_{n-s}-p_{n-s})^{2^{j}}.

Now, as
\deg_{X_{n-s}}M_{s}\leq\delta_{s}
, for
j_{0}:=\left\lceil\log(\delta_{s}+1)\right\rceil
, the Taylor expansion of order
2^{j_{0}}
of

M_{s}
in powers of
X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}
yields
M_{s}
, which proves that

M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)=\left(\prod_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})}(T-R_{n-s+1}^{(j_{0},\boldsymbol{\xi})})\mod(X_{n-s}-p_{n-s})^{2^{j_{0}}}\right).

This suggests a procedure for computing
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
. The
coordinates of the elements of the fiber
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
can
be expressed in the splitting field of the minimal polynomial

m_{s}\in k[T]
of
X_{n-s+1}
in
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
. To avoid
working in such a field extension, we start the iterations of the
Newton–Hensel operator
N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}
with the class of
T
in the
quotient ring
k[T]/(m_{s}(T))
, which represents all the roots of

m_{s}
. Following an idea introduced in
(
Giusti et al. 2001
, Algorithm 1)
,
the critical point of the algorithm below is that it allows one to
incrementally compute the polynomials
M_{s},W_{n-s+1},\ldots,W_{n}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
defining the Kronecker representation of
C_{s}
under
consideration.

In the description and analysis of the algorithm, we use the
following terminology: for
t\geq 0
and
\sigma,\tau\in\overline{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
, we say that
\tau=\sigma

with precision

t
if
\tau\equiv\sigma\mod(X_{n-s}-p_{n-s})^{t}
. Moreover, if
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\overline{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
are polynomials in a single variable
T
(of the
same degree), we say that
Q_{2}=Q_{1}
with precision
t
if each
coefficient of
Q_{2}
equals the corresponding coefficient of
Q_{1}

with precision
t
.

Algorithm 32

(Newton lifting)
.

Input:
The dense representation of polynomials

m_{s},v^{s}_{n-s+2},\ldots,v^{s}_{n}\in k[T]
with
\deg m_{s}=\delta_{s}
and

\deg v_{n-s+i}^{s}<\delta_{s}
for
2\leq i\leq s
, such that
m_{s}(X_{n-s+1})\equiv 0

and
X_{n-s+i}\equiv v_{n-s+i}^{s}(X_{n-s+1})
over
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})

for
2\leq i\leq s
.

Output:
The dense representation of polynomials
M_{s},W^{s}_{n-s+2},\ldots,W^{s}_{n}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
forming the Kronecker
representation of
C_{s}
, with
X_{n-s+1}
as primitive element, that

lifts
the input Kronecker representation, that is, with the
following properties:

1.

\deg M_{s}=\deg_{T}M_{s}=\delta_{s}
and
\deg W^{s}_{n-s+i}<\delta_{s}

for
2\leq i\leq s
;

2.

M_{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})\equiv 0
on
C_{s}
;

3.

\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})\cdot X_{n-s+i}\equiv W^{s}_{n-s+i}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})
on
C_{s}
for
2\leq i\leq s
;

4.

M_{s}(p_{n-s},T)=m_{s}
and

\big((\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T})^{-1}\cdot W^{s}_{n-s+i}\big)(p_{n-s},T)=v^{s}_{n-s+i}\mod m_{s}
for
2\leq i\leq s
.

1
Set
M^{0}:=m_{s}(T)
,
V_{n-s+1}^{0}:=T
.

2
Set
V_{n-s+i}^{0}:=v^{s}_{n-s+i}(T)
for
2\leq i\leq s

as elements of
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
.

3
Set
j:=0
.

4
While
2^{j}<\delta_{s}+1
, do

4.1
Compute
v_{n-s+1}^{j+1},\ldots,v_{n}^{j+1}
modulo

M^{j}
with precision
2^{j+1}
as follows:

(v_{n-s+1}^{j+1},\ldots,v_{n}^{j+1}):=N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}(V_{n-s+1}^{j},\ldots,V_{n}^{j}).

4.2
Compute
\Delta^{j+1}:=v_{n-s+1}^{j+1}-T
.

4.3
For
i=n-s+1,\ldots,n
do

4.3.1
Compute
\Delta_{i}^{j+1}:=\Delta^{j+1}\frac{\partial v_{i}^{j+1}}{\partial T}
mod
M^{j}
with precision
2^{j+1}
.

4.3.2
Set
V_{i}^{j+1}:=v_{i}^{j+1}-\Delta_{i}^{j+1}.

4.4
Compute
\Delta_{M}^{j+1}:=\Delta^{j+1}\frac{\partial M^{j}}{\partial T}
mod
M^{j}
with precision
2^{j+1}
.

4.5
Set
M^{j+1}:=M^{j}-\Delta_{M}^{j+1}
.

4.6
Set
j:=j+1

5
Return
M^{j}
with precision
\delta_{s}+1
.

6
For
i=n-s+1,\ldots,n
, return
\frac{\partial M^{j}}{\partial T}V_{i}^{j}\mod M^{j}
with precision
\delta_{s}+1
.

The algorithm requires performing arithmetic operations in the ring
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]

with a prescribed precision
t
. Concretely, this means that we are given the dense representations
of two polynomials in
k[X_{n-s}]
, and the desired arithmetic
operation is carried out modulo
(X_{n-s}-p_{n-s})^{t}
. In particular,
under the assumption that the input
polynomials have degree at most
t
—as is the case in Algorithm

32
—each such operation can be performed using

\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(t))
arithmetic operations over
k
.

We first analyze the behavior of the main loop of Algorithm

32
.

Proposition 33
.

Fix
j\geq 0
, and assume that we are given polynomials
M^{j},V_{n-s+1}^{j},\ldots V_{n}^{j}\in k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
, with
\deg_{T}M^{j}=\delta_{s}
and
\deg_{T}V_{n-s+i}^{j}<\delta_{s}
for
1\leq i\leq s
, satisfying the following
congruences with precision
2^{j}
:

\displaystyle F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})

\displaystyle\equiv 0\mod M^{j}\quad(1\leq i\leq s),

(4.6)

\displaystyle V_{n-s+1}^{j}

\displaystyle=T,

(4.7)

where
\boldsymbol{V}^{j}:=(V_{n-s+1}^{j},\ldots,V_{n}^{j})
. Assume further that

\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})
is invertible modulo
M^{j}
. Then the

(j+1)
th iteration of the loop of step
4
can be correctly
executed and outputs polynomials
M^{j+1},V_{n-s+1}^{j+1},\ldots V_{n}^{j+1}\in k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
, with

\deg_{T}M^{j+1}=\delta_{s}
and
\deg_{T}V_{n-s+i}^{j+1}<\delta_{s}
for

1\leq i\leq s
, satisfying the following congruences with precision

2^{j+1}
:

\displaystyle F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1})

\displaystyle\equiv 0\mod M^{j+1}\quad(1\leq i\leq s),

\displaystyle V_{n-s+1}^{j+1}

\displaystyle=T,

where
\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1}\!:=(V_{n-s+1}^{j+1},\ldots,V_{n}^{j+1})
.
Moreover,
\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1})
is invertible modulo

M^{j+1}
. In addition, if
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})\in k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
is invertible modulo
M^{j}
, then

G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1})
is invertible modulo

M^{j+1}
.

Proof.

Regarding the execution of the
(j+1)
th iteration, the only point
to check is whether the Newton-Hensel operator
N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})
is well-defined. By hypothesis,
\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})\mod M^{j}
is invertible, and thus the
matrix
J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})
admits an inverse modulo

M^{j}
. Therefore,
N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})

is well–defined modulo
M^{j}
.

Now we analyze the output produced by the
(j+1)
th iteration. Set
\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}:=(v_{n-s+1}^{j+1},\ldots,v_{n}^{j+1})
. We first claim that

\boldsymbol{F}_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1})\mod M^{j}=0

(4.8)

with precision
2^{j+1}
. Indeed, from (
4.6
)
we have
\boldsymbol{F}_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})=0\mod M^{j}
with precision
2^{j}
.
By the definition of
\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}
, it follows that

\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}-\boldsymbol{V}^{j}=-(J_{s}^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}_{s})(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})=0\mod M^{j},

with precision

2^{j}
. Since

\deg_{T}(v_{n-s+i}^{j+1}-V_{n-s+i}^{j})<\delta_{s}
for
1\leq i\leq s
, we
conclude that

\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}-\boldsymbol{V}^{j}=0

(4.9)

with precision
2^{j}
, and thus,
(\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}-\boldsymbol{V}^{j})^{2}=0
with
precision
2^{j+1}
. Applying the second-order Taylor expansion of

\boldsymbol{F}_{s}
in powers of
X_{n-s+1}-V_{n-s+1}^{j},\ldots,X_{n}-V_{n}^{j}
, we
obtain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1})

\displaystyle\equiv\boldsymbol{F}_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})+J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})(\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}-\boldsymbol{V}^{j})\mod M^{j}

\displaystyle\equiv\boldsymbol{F}_{s}(X_{n-s,}\boldsymbol{V}^{j})-J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})(J_{s}^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}_{s})(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})\mod M^{j}=\boldsymbol{0},

with precision
2^{j+1}
. This proves the claim. Note that, as

\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}
agrees with
\boldsymbol{V}^{j}
with precision
2^{j}
, step

4.1
in the main loop only involves modifications of the
coefficients of
\boldsymbol{V}^{j}
of order
2^{j}
or higher.

It follows that
\boldsymbol{F}_{s}
vanishes at

\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}
with precision
2^{j+1}
, but modulo
M^{j}
,
which represents the output with
precision only
2^{j}
. The next steps in the main loop
correct this lack of precision. To analyze these steps, recall that
we define

\Delta^{j+1}:=v_{n-s+1}^{j+1}-T.

We may consider
\Delta^{j+1}
as the “error” or “failure” of

\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}
to correctly parametrize the coordinates of

X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n}
with precision
2^{j+1}
. Observe that

\Delta^{j+1}=0,\quad\Delta^{j+1}_{M}=0,\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\Delta_{i}^{j+1}=0\quad(n-s+1\leq i\leq n),

all with precision
{2^{j}}
. In fact,
\Delta^{j+1}=0
with precision

{2^{j}}
because
v_{n-s+i}^{j+1}=V_{n-s+i}^{j}
with precision
2^{j}

and (
4.7
) holds, while the remaining
assertions follow immediately from this one. The fact that

\Delta^{j+1}=0
with precision
2^{j}
indicates that
\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}

correctly parametrizes
X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n}
with precision
2^{j}
.

Claim
.

Fix
j\geq 0
. The following congruences hold with precision
2^{j+1}
:

\displaystyle M^{j}(T-\Delta^{j+1})

\displaystyle\equiv 0\mod M^{j+1},

(4.10)

\displaystyle v^{j+1}_{i}(T-\Delta^{j+1})

\displaystyle\equiv V^{j+1}_{i}\mod M^{j+1}\textrm{ for }n-s+1\leq i\leq n.

(4.11)

Proof of Claim.

We start by expanding
M^{j}(T-\Delta^{j+1})
using Taylor expansion
around
T
, in
powers of
\Delta^{j+1}
:

M^{j}(T-\Delta^{j+1})=M^{j}(T)-\frac{\partial M^{j}}{\partial T}\Delta^{j+1}+\mathcal{O}\big((\Delta^{j+1})^{2}\big).

Since
\Delta^{j+1}=0
with precision
2^{j}
, it follows that

(\Delta^{j+1})^{2}=0
with precision
2^{j+1}
. Hence,

M^{j}(T-\Delta^{j+1})=M^{j}(T)-\frac{\partial M^{j}}{\partial T}\Delta^{j+1}+\mathcal{O}\big((\Delta^{j+1})^{2}\big),

(4.12)

with precision
2^{j+1}
. By the definition of
M^{j+1}
,
we have
M^{j}=M^{j+1}+\Delta^{j+1}_{M}
, where
\Delta^{j+1}_{M}
is the
remainder of the division of
\Delta^{j+1}\frac{\partial M^{j}}{\partial T}
by
M^{j}
, taken with precision
2^{j+1}
. Let

h\in k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
denote the quotient of this
division. By (
4.12
), and the definition
of
M^{j+1}
and
\Delta^{j+1}_{M}
, we have

\displaystyle M^{j}(T-\Delta^{j+1})=M^{j}-(h(T)M^{j}+\Delta^{j+1}_{M})

\displaystyle=(M^{j+1}+\Delta^{j+1}_{M})\big(1-h(T)\big)-\Delta^{j+1}_{M}

\displaystyle=M^{j+1}\big(1-h(T)\big)-h(T)\Delta^{j+1}_{M}(T),

with precision
2^{j+1}
. Since
\Delta^{j+1}\frac{\partial M^{j}}{\partial T}=0
with precision
2^{j}
, and
M^{j}
is monic,
the quotient
h(T)
vanishes with precision
2^{j}
, and thus

\Delta^{j+1}_{M}=0
with precision
2^{j}
. We conclude that

h(T)\Delta^{j+1}_{M}(T)=0
with precision
2^{j+1}
, which implies
(
4.10
).

Now we turn to (
4.11
). For
n-s+1\leq i\leq n
, consider the Taylor expansion of

v^{j+1}_{i}(T-\Delta^{j+1})
around
T
, in
powers of
\Delta^{j+1}
. We have

v^{j+1}_{i}(T-\Delta^{j+1})=v^{j+1}_{i}(T)-\Delta^{j+1}\frac{\partial v^{j+1}_{i}}{\partial T}(T),

with precision
2^{j+1}
. By construction,

v^{j+1}_{i}=V^{j+1}_{i}+\Delta^{j+1}_{i}
with precision
2^{j+1}
, where

\Delta^{j+1}_{i}
is the remainder of the division of

\Delta^{j+1}\frac{\partial v^{j+1}_{i}}{\partial T}
by
M^{j}
, taken
with precision
2^{j+1}
. Let
h_{i}\in k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]

denote the quotient of this division. Then

v^{j+1}_{i}(T-\Delta^{j+1})=V_{i}^{j+1}+\Delta^{j+1}_{i}-(h_{i}(T)M^{j}(T)+\Delta_{i}^{j+1})=V_{i}^{j+1}-h_{i}(T)M^{j}(T),

(4.13)

with precision
2^{j+1}
. We observe that
h_{i}(T)M^{j}(T)\equiv 0\mod M^{j+1}
with precision
2^{j+1}
. Indeed, similarly as before we
see that
h_{i}=0
, and then
\frac{\partial h_{i}}{\partial T}=0
, with
precision
2^{j}
. It follows that
h_{i}(T-\Delta^{j+1})=h_{i}(T)-\frac{\partial h_{i}}{\partial T}\Delta^{j+1}+\mathcal{O}\big((\Delta^{j+1})^{2}\big)=h_{i}(T)
with
precision
2^{j+1}
. Then, by (
4.10
),

\displaystyle h_{i}(T)M^{j}(T)=h_{i}(T)\Big(M^{j}(T)-\frac{\partial M^{j}}{\partial T}\Delta^{j+1}\Big)

\displaystyle=h_{i}(T-\Delta^{j+1})M^{j}(T-\Delta^{j+1})

\displaystyle\equiv 0\mod M^{j+1},

with precision
2^{j+1}
. This, combined with (
4.13
), yields (
4.11
), which completes the proof of the claim.
∎

Substituting
T-\Delta^{j+1}
for
T
in
(
4.8
), we obtain

\boldsymbol{F}_{s}\big(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}(T-\Delta^{j+1})\big)\equiv 0\mod M^{j}(T-\Delta^{j+1}),

(4.14)

with precision
2^{j+1}
. Taking into account (
4.10
) and (
4.11
), we conclude that

\boldsymbol{F}_{s}\big(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1}\big)\equiv 0\mod M^{j+1}

with precision
2^{j+1}
, as desired.
Furthermore, since by definition
v^{j+1}_{n-s+1}=T+\Delta^{j+1}
,
substituting
T-\Delta^{j+1}
for
T
in this expression we obtain

V^{j+1}_{n-s+1}\equiv T\mod M^{j+1}
, with precision
2^{j+1}
.

It remains to prove the invertibility of
\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1})
and
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1})
, both modulo

M^{j+1}
. Observe that the invertibility of the former holds if and
only if the resultant
\mathrm{Res}_{T}\big(\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1}),M^{j+1}\big)
is an invertible element of

\overline{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
. By (
4.9
), we have
\boldsymbol{v}^{j+1}=\boldsymbol{V}^{j}
with precision
2^{j}
.
Furthermore, as
V_{i}^{j+1}:=v_{i}^{j+1}-\Delta_{i}^{j+1}
, and

\Delta_{i}^{j+1}=0
with precision
2^{j}
, for
1\leq i\leq s
, it
follows that
\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1}=\boldsymbol{V}^{j}
with precision
2^{j}
, which in
turn implies
\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1})=\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})
with precision
2^{j}
. As
M^{j+1}=M^{j}
with precision

2^{j}
, we conclude that

\mathrm{Res}_{T}\big(\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1}),M^{j+1}\big)=\mathrm{Res}_{T}\big(\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j}),M^{j}\big)

with precision
2^{j}
. Since the right-hand side is invertible by
hypothesis, the assertion follows. The same argument proves that the
invertibility of
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})
modulo
M^{j}

implies the invertibility of
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1})

modulo
M^{j+1}
.
∎

Let
M_{s},W_{n-s+i}^{s}\ldots,W_{n}^{s}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
be the
polynomials that form the Kronecker representation of
C_{s}
, with
primitive element
X_{n-s+1}
, whose existence is established in Section

4.1
. Define

V^{(s)}_{n-s+1}:=T\ \textrm{ and }\ V^{(s)}_{n-s+i}:=\Big(\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}\Big)^{-1}W^{s}_{n-s+i}\mod M_{s}\ \textrm{ for }\ 2\leq i\leq s.

Remark 34
.

The Kronecker representation
M_{s},W_{n-s+i}^{s}\ldots,W_{n}^{s}
of
C_{s}

“lifts” the Kronecker representation

m_{s},w_{n-s+1}^{s},\ldots,w_{n}^{s}
of the lifting fiber
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, in the sense that
M_{s}(p_{n-s},T)=m_{s}
and

W^{s}_{n-s+i}(p_{n-s},T)=w^{s}_{n-s+i}
for
2\leq i\leq s
. Moreover,
since the discriminant

\rho_{s}:=\mathrm{Disc}_{T}\big(M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)\big)
satisfies

\rho_{s}(p_{n-s})\neq 0
by the choice of the lifting point
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}
,
it follows that each
V^{(s)}_{n-s+i}
is a well–defined element of

k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
for
2\leq i\leq s
, with

V^{(s)}_{n-s+i}(p_{n-s},T)=v^{(s)}_{n-s+i}
.

Now we are in position to establish the correctness of
Algorithm
32
.

Theorem 35
.

Algorithm
32
correctly computes the
coefficients in
k[X_{n-s}]
of the polynomials
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
,

W_{n-s+i}^{s}(X_{n-s},T)

(2\leq i\leq s)
defining a
Kronecker representation of
C_{s}
with
X_{n-s+1}
as primitive element.

Proof.

In view of Proposition
33
, we
claim that

\displaystyle F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{0})

\displaystyle\equiv 0\mod M^{0}\quad(1\leq i\leq s),

\displaystyle V_{n-s+1}^{0}

\displaystyle=T,

with precision 1, and that
\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{0})
is invertible
modulo
M^{0}
, where
\boldsymbol{V}^{0}:=(V_{n-s+1}^{0},\ldots,V_{n}^{0})
.

Since
M^{0}:=m_{s}
and
V_{n-s+i}^{0}:=v_{n-s+i}^{s}
for

2\leq i\leq s
, it suffices to prove that
F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},\boldsymbol{V}^{0}(p_{n-s},T))

(1\leq i\leq s)
and
V_{n-s+1}^{0}(p_{n-s},T)-T

vanish at all roots of
m_{s}
, and
\det J_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},\boldsymbol{V}^{0}(p_{n-s},T))
does not vanish at any root of
m_{s}
.

By assumption,
X_{n-s+i}\equiv v^{s}_{n-s+i}(X_{n-s+1})
over
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
for
2\leq i\leq s
.
Therefore, for each point
(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},x_{n-s+1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, we have

x_{n-s+i}=V_{n-s+i}^{0}(p_{n-s},x_{n-s+1}).

(4.15)

Since
F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},x_{n-s+1},\ldots,x_{n})=0
for
1\leq i\leq s
, and

x_{n-s+i}=V_{n-s+i}^{0}(p_{n-s},x_{n-s+1})
for
2\leq i\leq s
, it
follows that
F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},\boldsymbol{V}^{0}(p_{n-s},T))
vanishes at all
roots
x_{n-s+1}
of
M^{0}(p_{n-s},T)
, for
1\leq i\leq s
. The
second assertion is clear by definition.

Finally, as
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
is a lifting fiber, Proposition

28
proves that it is
unramified. Thus,
\det J_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},x_{n-s+1},\ldots,x_{n})\not=0
for
each point
(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},x_{n-s+1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, and
it follows that
\det J_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},\boldsymbol{V}^{0}(p_{n-s},T))
does not
vanish at any root
x_{n-s+1}
of
m_{s}
. This completes the proof of
the claim.

By Proposition
33
, we deduce that
Algorithm
32
executes correctly. It remains
to prove that its output consists of the polynomials
M_{s},W^{s}_{n-s+1},\ldots,W^{s}_{n}\in{k}[X_{n-s},T]
which define a Kronecker
representation of the lifting curve
C_{s}
, with
X_{n-s+1}
as
primitive element.

By construction,
M_{s}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
is monic in

T
and satisfies
\deg_{T}M_{s}=\deg M_{s}=\delta_{s}
. Moreover,

m_{s}=M_{s}(p_{n-s},T)
. Since
m_{s}
is square-free of degree
\delta_{s}
,
Hensel’s lemma implies that
M_{s}
factors in

\overline{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
as

M_{s}=\prod_{x\in\{m_{s}=0\}}(T-R_{n-s+1}^{x}),

where
R_{n-s+1}^{x}(p_{n-s})=x
for each root
x
of
m_{s}
. Denote

R_{n-s+i}^{x}:=V_{n-s+i}^{(s)}(R_{n-s+1}^{x})
for
2\leq i\leq s
and
each
x\in\{m_{s}=0\}
, and let
\boldsymbol{R}^{x}:=(R_{n-s+1}^{x},\ldots,R_{n}^{x})
. We claim that, for each
x\in\{m_{s}=0\}
,

•

F_{1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{R}^{x})=0,\ldots,F_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{R}^{x})=0
;

•

(p_{n-s},\boldsymbol{R}^{x}(p_{n-s}))\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
.

Indeed, fix such an
x
and set
\boldsymbol{V}^{(s)}:=(V^{(s)}_{n-s+1},\ldots,V^{(s)}_{n})
. Since
M_{s}(R_{n-s+1}^{x})=0

and
\boldsymbol{R}^{x}=\boldsymbol{V}^{(s)}(R_{n-s+1}^{x})
, together with the relation

F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{(s)})\mod M_{s}=0\quad(1\leq i\leq s),

it follows immediately that
F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{R}^{x})=0

for
1\leq i\leq s
. For the second assertion, since
\boldsymbol{R}^{x}=\boldsymbol{V}^{(s)}(R_{n-s+1}^{x})

and the identities
V_{n-s+i}(p_{n-s},T)=v_{n-s+1}
and
R^{x}_{n-s+1}(p_{n-s})=x
hold,
by substituting
p_{n-s}
for
X_{n-s}
it follows that

(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},\boldsymbol{R}^{x}(p_{n-s}))=(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},x,v_{n-s+2}(x),\ldots,v_{n}(x))\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s}).

Finally, we verify that the output of Algorithm
32
is the desired one. Suppose the main loop is executed for
all
j\geq 0
. The fact that
M^{j+1}=M^{j}
and

V_{n-s+i}^{j+1}=V_{n-s+i}^{j}

(1\leq i\leq s)
with precision
2^{j}

for all
j\geq 0
shows that there exist unique polynomials

M^{\infty},V_{n-s+1}^{\infty},\ldots,V_{n}^{\infty}\in{k}[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
satisfying
M^{\infty}=M^{j}
and
V_{n-s+i}^{\infty}=V^{j}_{n-s+i}

(1\leq i\leq s)
with precision
2^{j}
for all

j\geq 0
.

In particular, we have
\deg_{T}M^{\infty}=\delta_{s}
and

\deg_{T}V_{n-s+i}^{\infty}<\delta_{s}
for
1\leq i\leq s
. Furthermore,
since the congruences (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) hold for all
j\geq 0
, it follows that

\displaystyle F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})\mod M^{\infty}

\displaystyle=0\quad(1\leq i\leq s),

\displaystyle V_{n-s+1}^{\infty}

\displaystyle=T.

As
m_{s}=M^{\infty}(p_{n-s},T)
, applying Hensel’s lemma once more,

M^{\infty}
admits a factorization

M^{\infty}=\prod_{x\in\{m_{s}=0\}}(T-S_{n-s+1}^{x}),

where
S_{n-s+1}^{x}(p_{n-s})=x
for each
x\in\{m_{s}=0\}
. Set

S_{n-s+i}^{x}:=V_{n-s+i}^{\infty}(S_{n-s+1}^{x})
for
2\leq i\leq s
, and

\boldsymbol{S}^{x}:=(S_{n-s+1}^{x},\ldots,S_{n}^{x})
. Then

F_{1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{S}^{x})=0,\ldots,F_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{S}^{x})=0.

(4.16)

We claim that

(p_{n-s},\boldsymbol{S}^{x}(p_{n-s}))\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s}).

(4.17)

First we show that
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})

is invertible modulo
M^{\infty}(X_{n-s},T)
in
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
.
Indeed, let

D^{\infty}:=\mathrm{Res}_{T}(G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty}),M^{\infty}(X_{n-s},T)).

Since
\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty}(p_{n-s},T)=\boldsymbol{v}^{s}(T):=(v^{s}_{n-s+1}(T),\ldots,v^{s}_{n}(T))
and

M^{\infty}(p_{n-s},T)=m_{s}(T)
, we obtain

D^{\infty}(p_{n-s})=\mathrm{Res}_{T}(G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},\boldsymbol{v}^{s}),m_{s})
, which is nonzero
because
G
does not vanish on any point of
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
. Hence

D^{\infty}
is a unit of
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
, which implies the
claimed invertibility. Since
M^{\infty}(S_{n-s+1}^{x})=0
, the fact that

D^{\infty}
is a unit of
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
shows that

G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty}(S_{n-s+1}^{x}))=G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{S}^{x})
is also a unit of
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
.
Consequently,
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},\boldsymbol{S}^{x}(p_{n-s}))\not=0
. Together
with (
4.16
), this implies (
4.17
).

Finally, we claim that

\boldsymbol{R}^{x}(p_{n-s})=\boldsymbol{S}^{x}(p_{n-s}).

(4.18)

By definition,

S_{n-s+1}^{x}(p_{n-s})=x=R_{n-s+1}^{x}(p_{n-s})
. Furthermore,

S_{n-s+i}^{x}(p_{n-s})=V_{n-s+i}^{\infty}(p_{n-s},S_{n-s+1}^{x}(p_{n-s}))=V_{n-s+i}^{\infty}(p_{n-s},x)=v_{n-s+i}^{s}(x)

for
2\leq i\leq s
. On the other hand,

R_{n-s+i}^{x}(p_{n-s})=V_{n-s+i}^{s}(p_{n-s},R_{n-s+1}^{x}(p_{n-s}))=V_{n-s+i}^{s}(p_{n-s},x)=v_{n-s+i}^{s}(x).

Thus
R_{n-s+i}^{x}(p_{n-s})=v_{n-s+i}^{s}(x)=S_{n-s+i}^{x}(p_{n-s})
for

2\leq i\leq s
.

Combining (
4.16
), (
4.17
) and
(
4.18
) with Theorem
30
yields
\boldsymbol{R}^{x}=\boldsymbol{S}^{x}

for each
x\in\{m_{s}=0\}
. In particular,

M^{\infty}=\prod_{x\in\{m_{s}=0\}}(T-S_{n-s+1}^{x})=\prod_{x\in\{m_{s}=0\}}(T-R_{n-s+1}^{x})=M_{s}.

Moreover, for each
i
with
1\leq i\leq s
, the equalities

V^{(s)}_{n-s+i}(R_{n-s+1}^{x})=R_{n-s+i}^{x}=S_{n-s+i}^{x}=V_{n-s+i}^{\infty}(R_{n-s+1}^{x})

for each of the
\delta_{s}
distinct roots
x\in\overline{k}
of

m_{s}
, together with

\deg_{T}V_{n-s+i}^{\infty},\deg_{T}V_{n-s+i}^{(s)}<\delta_{s}
,
imply that
V_{n-s+i}^{\infty}=V^{(s)}_{n-s+i}
.

Thus
M_{s}=M^{j}

and
V^{(s)}_{n-s+i}=V^{j}_{n-s+i}(1\leq i\leq s)
with precision

2^{j}
for all
j\geq 0
. Setting
j_{0}:=\lfloor\log_{2}(\delta_{s}+1)\rfloor
, we obtain with precision
2^{j_{0}}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial M^{j_{0}}}{\partial T}V^{j_{0}}_{n-s+i}\mod M^{j_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}V^{(s)}_{n-s+i}\mod M_{s}=W^{s}_{n-s+i}\quad(1\leq i\leq s).

Since
\frac{\partial M^{j_{0}}}{\partial T}V^{j_{0}}_{n-s+i}\mod M^{j_{0}}
and
W^{s}_{n-s+i}
have degree at most
\delta_{s}<2^{j_{0}}

in
X_{n-s}
, it follows that they are equal. Similarly,

M^{j_{0}}=M_{s}
. Hence the output of Algorithm
32
consists of the desired polynomials
M_{s},W^{s}_{n-s+1},\ldots,W^{s}_{n}
.
∎

4.3
The cost of Newton lifting

We now proceed to analyze the complexity of Algorithm
32
. Its output consists of polynomials of

k[X_{n-s},T]
, which are given by their dense representation.
Algorithm
32
involves the evaluation of the
Newton-Hensel operator

N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}(X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n})^{t}:=(X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n})^{t}-(J_{s}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}^{t})(X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n}),

where
\boldsymbol{F}_{s}:=(F_{1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}),\ldots,F_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}))
, and
J_{s}
denotes the Jacobian matrix of

{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}
with respect to
X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n}
. Viewing
N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}
as a vector of elements of
k[X_{n-s}][X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, we
are interested in the cost of its evaluation over
k[X_{n-s}]
. We
begin with the following result.

Lemma 36
.

If
F_{1},\ldots,F_{s}\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
are given by a straight-line
program
\beta
in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
of length
L
, then the
determinant
\det J_{s}
and the adjoint matrix
\mathrm{Adj}(J_{s})

can be evaluated by a straight-line program
\beta^{\prime}
in
k[X_{n-s}]

of length
\mathcal{O}(Ls+s^{4})
.

Proof.

From the straight-line program
\beta
, we
immediately obtain a straight-line program to evaluate the
specialized polynomials
F_{1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}),\ldots,F_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})
, using at most

L
arithmetic operations in
k[X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}]
. We retain
the notation
\beta
for this new straight-line program.

Applying the Baur–Strassen theorem to
\beta
yields a
straight-line program which evaluates the partial derivatives of

F_{1},\ldots,F_{s}
with respect to
X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n}
, using

\mathcal{O}(Ls)
arithmetic operations in
k[X_{n-s}]
(see

Baur and Strassen 1983
).

To this straight-line program we append, for instance, the
Samuelson–Berkowitz algorithm (see
Berkowitz 1984
) to
compute both the determinant and the adjoint matrix of
J_{s}
, with
an additional cost of
\mathcal{O}(s^{4})
operations.
∎

Let
\boldsymbol{V}^{j}:=(V_{n-s+1}^{j},\ldots,V_{n}^{j})
for each
j\geq 0
. The

j
th iteration of step
4
of Algorithm
32
requires computing
N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}(\boldsymbol{V}^{j})
modulo
M^{j}

with precision
2^{j+1}
. For this purpose, we establish the following result.

Lemma 37
.

The evaluation of
N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})
modulo
M^{j}
can
be performed using
\mathcal{O}((Ls+s^{4}+\log_{2}\delta_{s}){\sf M}(\delta_{s}))

arithmetic operations in
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
.

Proof.

By Lemma
36
, we can compute

\det J_{s}
and
\mathrm{Adj}(J_{s})
via a straight–line program
using
\mathcal{O}(Ls+s^{4})
arithmetic operations in
k[X_{n-s}]
. We
substitute the values
\boldsymbol{V}^{j}
for the variables
X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n}
, and perform all these arithmetic operations in

k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
modulo
M^{j}
. Since
M^{j}
is a monic
element of
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
, each such modular
operation involves
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta_{s}))
arithmetic
operations in
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
. Hence, computing both

\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})
and
\mathrm{Adj}(J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j}))
modulo
M^{j}
requires
\mathcal{O}((Ls+s^{4}){\sf M}(\delta_{s}))

arithmetic operations in
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
.

According to Proposition
33
,
\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})
is invertible modulo
M^{j}
. Therefore, we
can compute its inverse
\det J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})^{-1}
modulo

M^{j}
with
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta_{s})\log_{2}\delta_{s})
arithmetic
operations in
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
.

Finally, we compute modulo
M^{j}
the multiplication

(J_{s}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}^{t})(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})=\big(\det J_{s}^{-1}\mathrm{Adj}(J_{s}){\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}^{t}\big)(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})

and the subtraction

N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})^{t}=(\boldsymbol{V}^{j})^{t}-(J_{s}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}^{t})(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})

with
\mathcal{O}(s^{2}{\sf M}(\delta_{s}))
arithmetic operations in

k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
.

In total, the procedure requires

\mathcal{O}((Ls+s^{4}+\log_{2}\delta_{s}){\sf M}(\delta_{s}))
arithmetic
operations in
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
, as claimed. ∎

The matrix inversion of the Jacobian matrix
J_{s}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})

required for evaluating the Newton–Hensel operator
N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}(X_{n-s},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})
in step
4.1
of Algorithm
32
can be performed incrementally, as described in

(
Giusti et al. 2001
, Section 4.3)
. Following this approach, the inversion
can be implemented using Newton iteration, thereby avoiding the
additional
\log_{2}(\delta_{s})
factor in the first term of the
complexity estimate of Lemma
37
.
Nevertheless, since this improvement does not affect the overall
asymptotic cost of the main algorithm—which is dominated by other
routines of higher complexity—we have chosen to retain the present
formulation for the sake of clarity and uniformity of exposition.

Now we analyze the overall cost of Algorithm
32
.

Theorem 38
.

Assume that
F_{1},\ldots,F_{s}\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
are given by a
straight–line program
\beta
in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
of length
L
.
Then Algorithm
32
can be implemented
with
\mathcal{O}((Ls+s^{4}+\log_{2}\delta_{s}){\sf M}(\delta_{s})^{2})
arithmetic
operations in
k
.

Proof.

We analyze the loop of step
4
. Let
j
be fixed such that

2^{j}<\delta_{s}
. The
j
th iteration operates with elements of

k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
, and all the arithmetic operations
are performed at precision
2^{j+1}
. Therefore, we analyze the
number of arithmetic operations in
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]

performed in this iteration. Step
4.1
requires the evaluation

N_{\boldsymbol{F}_{s}}(\boldsymbol{V}_{j})
, which, by Lemma
37
, requires
\mathcal{O}((Ls+s^{4}+\log_{2}\delta_{s}){\sf M}(\delta_{s}))
arithmetic operations in
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
.
Steps
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
and
4.5
consist of

\mathcal{O}(s)
arithmetic operations, modulo
M^{j}
, of polynomials
of
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!][T]
of degree at most
\delta_{s}
. As a
consequence, these steps require
\mathcal{O}(s\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s}))

arithmetic operations in
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
. It follows that
the complete
j
th iteration can be performed using

\mathcal{O}((Ls+s^{4}+\log_{2}\delta_{s}){\sf M}(\delta_{s}))
arithmetic
operations in
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
. Each such operation is
performed with precision
2^{j+1}
, and then consists of
{\sf M}(2^{j+1})
arithmetic operations in
k
, so the total cost of the

j
th iteration is

\mathcal{O}((Ls+s^{4}+\log_{2}\delta_{s}){\sf M}(\delta_{s}){\sf M}(2^{j+1}))

arithmetic operations in
k
. Summing over
j=0
to
\lfloor\log\delta_{s}\rfloor
,
we obtain the total cost of the main loop:

\sum^{\lfloor\log\delta_{s}\rfloor}_{j=0}\mathcal{O}((Ls+s^{4}+\log_{2}\delta_{s}){\sf M}(\delta_{s}){\sf M}(2^{j+1}))=\mathcal{O}((Ls+s^{4}+\log_{2}\delta_{s}){\sf M}(\delta_{s})^{2}).

The remaining steps have a negligible cost compared to this bound.
∎

5
From the
s
th lifting curve to the
(s+1)
th lifting fiber

In this section, we discuss the elimination phase of the
s
th
recursive step in the main algorithm for computing a Kronecker
representation of the Zariski closure
V:=V_{r}
of
V(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})\setminus V(G)
. Recall that, for
1\leq j\leq r
, the variety

V_{j}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
denotes the Zariski closure of

\{F_{1}=0,\ldots,F_{j}=0,G\not=0\}
, and that
\pi_{j}:V_{j}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-j}

denotes the projection onto the first
n-j
coordinates. Throughout
this section, we assume that the variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
and the
point
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
satisfy all the conditions of Theorem

23
. The goal of this step is to
compute a Kronecker representation of the lifting fiber

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
, given a Kronecker representation of the
lifting curve
C_{s}:=\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
.

5.1
Computing projections of the
(s+1)
th lifting fiber

The computation of a Kronecker representation of the lifting fiber

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
is based on the following result.

Proposition 39
.

The following assertions hold:

1.

C_{s}\cap V(F_{s+1})\setminus\{G=0\}=\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
.

2.

C_{s}\cap V(G)
has dimension at most zero.

3.

C_{s}\cap V(F_{s+1})
has dimension zero.

Proof.

We start with the first assertion. Let
(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\boldsymbol{x})\in\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
. Then
F_{j}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\boldsymbol{x})=0
for
1\leq j\leq s+1
, which implies that
(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\boldsymbol{x})\in V(F_{s+1})
.
Furthermore, since
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\subset\{G\not=0\}
,
Proposition
28
(1)

implies that
(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\boldsymbol{x})\in C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\}
. It follows
that
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\subset C_{s}\cap V(F_{s+1})\setminus\{G=0\}
.

Conversely, let
(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\boldsymbol{x})\in(C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\})\cap V(F_{s+1})
. Then
F_{j}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\boldsymbol{x})=0
for
1\leq j\leq s+1
and

G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\boldsymbol{x})\not=0
. Since
\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}
is a lifting point of

\pi_{s+1}
, the polynomials
F_{j}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})
for

1\leq j\leq s+1
, together with
X_{i}-p_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n-s-1
,
generate the radical ideal of
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
. Hence,

(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\boldsymbol{x})\in\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
, completing the
proof of the first assertion.

To prove the second assertion, observe from Proposition

28
(1)
that

C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\}
has pure dimension 1. Moreover, since

(C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\})\cap\{X_{n-s}=p_{n-s}\}=\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})

has cardinality
\delta_{s}
, we deduce that

\delta_{s}\leq\deg(C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\})\leq\deg(C_{s})=\delta_{s},

which implies that
\deg(C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\})=\deg(C_{s})
. Hence,

C_{s}\setminus\{G=0\}
is Zariski dense in
C_{s}
, and so

C_{s}\cap\{G=0\}
has dimension at most zero, proving the second
assertion.

For the third assertion, by the first assertion we have

\displaystyle C_{s}\cap V(F_{s+1})=

\displaystyle\big(C_{s}\cap V(F_{s+1})\cap\{G=0\}\big)\cup\big(C_{s}\cap V(F_{s+1})\setminus\{G=0\}\big)

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle C_{s}\cap\{G=0\}\cup\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}).

Since both
C_{s}\cap\{G=0\}
and

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
are finite, this proves that
C_{s}\cap V(F_{s+1})

is finite, hence of dimension zero.
∎

Proposition
39
suggests
a strategy to compute a Kronecker representation of

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
: determine the intersection
C_{s}\cap V(F_{s+1})
and discard the points lying in
V(G)
. To achieve this,
we compute the image of this intersection under the map

\pi_{s}^{*}:C_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
, given by
\pi_{s}^{*}(x_{n-s},\ldots,x_{n}):=x_{n-s}
.

Condition
({\sf B}_{3})
of Section
4
ensures
that
\pi_{s}^{*}
is injective on
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
, while
condition
({\sf B}_{5})
guarantees that the points in
C_{s}\cap V(G)

can be identified and removed via their images under
\pi_{s}^{*}
.
Additionally, the Kronecker representation of
C_{s}
under
consideration provides a “good” parametrization of the points

(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},x_{n-s},\ldots,x_{n})
of
C_{s}
for which
\rho_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},x_{n-s})\not=0
. Condition
({\sf B}_{4})
ensures that the points
of
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\subset C_{s}
admit such a good
representation.

This procedure requires computing the projection
\pi_{s}^{*}
of the
points in an intersection
C_{s}\cap V(F)
, where
F\in k[X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}]
satisfies
\#\big((C_{s}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s})=0\})\cap V(F)\big)<\infty
. Since
\pi_{s}^{*}:C_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
is a finite morphism, the
coordinate ring
k[C_{s}]
becomes a finite
k[X_{n-s}]
–module. Moreover,

k[C_{s}]
is a Cohen-Macaulay ring and a free
k[X_{n-s}]
-module
(see
Giusti et al. 1993
). It follows that

k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]
is a (finite-dimensional)

k(X_{n-s})
–vector space. We consider the homothety

\eta_{F}:k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]\to k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]
by
F
, and its
characteristic polynomial
\chi_{F}\in k(X_{n-s})[T]
.

The following lemma shows that
the square–free part of the constant term of
\chi_{F}

describes the image of the projection of the intersection.

Lemma 40
.

Let
F\in k[X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}]
be such that
\#(C_{s}\cap V(F))<\infty
. Let
\chi_{F}\in k[X_{n-s}][T]
be the characteristic
polynomial of the homothety
\eta_{F}:k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]\to k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]
by
F
, and let

\widetilde{a}\in k(X_{n-s})
be the constant term of
\chi_{F}
. Then

\widetilde{a}\in k[X_{n-s}]
. Let
a_{F}\in k[X_{n-s}]
be the monic
polynomial which consists of the product of the irreducible factors
on
k[X_{n-s}]
of
\widetilde{a}
. Then:

1.

a_{F}\in k[X_{n-s}]\setminus\{0\}
,

2.

a_{F}\in I(C_{s}\cap V(F))
.

Proof.

According to
(
Giusti et al. 2001
, Corollary 2)
,
\chi_{F}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
.
Thus, its constant term
\widetilde{a}
belongs to
k[X_{n-s}]
, and
so does its square-free part
a_{F}
.

By the Cayley-Hamilton theorem,
\chi_{F}(F)=0
in

k[C_{s}]
, hence the constant term
\widetilde{a}
of

\chi_{F}
lies in the ideal generated by
I(C_{s})
and
F
,
proving the second assertion.

To prove the first assertion, let
m_{F}\in k(X_{n-s})[T]
be the
minimal polynomial of
\eta_{F}
. As
\chi_{F}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
, we
have
m_{F}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
, and its constant term
b\in k[X_{n-s}]

is equal to zero if and only if
\widetilde{a}=0
. Suppose
b=0
.
Then
m_{F}=T\cdot\widetilde{m}
for some
\widetilde{m}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
, so
0=m_{F}(F)=F\cdot\widetilde{m}(F)
in
k[C_{s}]
.
Minimality of
m_{F}
implies
\widetilde{m}(F)\not=0
in
k[C_{s}]
, so

F
is a zero divisor in
k[C_{s}]
, and hence
F
vanishes on an
irreducible component of
C_{s}
, contradicting the
condition
\#(C_{s}\cap V(F))<\infty
. Therefore,
b\not=0
, so

\widetilde{a}\not=0
. This readily implies the first assertion.
∎

Now we describe the algorithm for computing the constant term of the
characteristic polynomial of the homothety
\eta_{F}
by a polynomial

F
as above. This algorithm isolates a routine that was only
implicitly contained in the algorithm underlying

(
Cafure and Matera 2006
, Proposition 4.4)
, and extends it by introducing an
interpolation stage that allows the method to work over arbitrary
perfect fields. This reformulation not only broadens the
applicability of the procedure but also clarifies the internal
structure of the computation, making explicit the intermediate
objects involved in the process.

Algorithm 41

(Computation of a projection)
.

Input:

1.

A Kronecker representation of
C_{s}
, as
in the output of Algorithm
32
;

2.

F\in k[X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n}]
such that

\#(C_{s}\setminus\{\rho_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s})=0\})\cap V(F)<\infty
;

3.

Access to uniformly random elements of a finite set

\mathcal{S}\subset k
of size at least

\varepsilon^{-1}(D_{s}+1)(\delta_{s}^{2}+D_{s})
, where
0<\varepsilon<1
and
D_{s}:=d\cdot\delta_{s}
.

Output:
The dense representation of the
square–free part of the constant term
a_{F}\in k[X_{n-s}]
of the
characteristic polynomial
\chi_{F}\in k[X_{n-s}][T]
of the homothety

\eta_{F}:k[C_{s}]\to k[C_{s}]
of multiplication by
F
.

1
For
j=0,\ldots,D_{s}
, do

1.1
Choose
\alpha_{j}\in\mathcal{S}
at random;

1.2
Compute
V_{n-s+i}^{s}(\alpha_{j},T)=\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(\alpha_{j},T)^{-1}W_{n-s+i}^{s}(\alpha_{j},T)\mod M_{s}(\alpha_{j},T)
for

2\leq i\leq s
;

1.3
Compute
F^{*}(\alpha_{j},T)=F(\alpha_{j},T,V_{n-s+2}^{s}(\alpha_{j},T),\ldots,V_{n}^{s}(\alpha_{j},T))\mod M_{s}(\alpha_{j},T)
;

1.4
Compute

\widetilde{a}(\alpha_{j}):=\mathrm{Res}_{T}\left(M_{s}(\alpha_{j},T),F^{*}(\alpha_{j},T)\right)
.

2
Interpolate the polynomial
\widetilde{a}
from its values.

***[Observe that
\widetilde{a}\in k[X_{n-s}]\setminus k
]***

3
Compute the square–free part
a_{F}

of
\widetilde{a}
.

Remark 42
.

We assume that the base field
k
has sufficiently large cardinality
to allow the choice of the evaluation points
\alpha_{j}
required in
step
1.1
inside
k
. If this is not the case, one may
perform the computations over a finite extension of
k
of adequate
size; this increases the cost of Algorithm
41
only by an additional logarithmic factor. To avoid
further complicating the complexity estimates we henceforth state
the costs under the convention that
k
is large enough, with the
understanding that a passage to a small extension is always possible
at the indicated (logarithmic) overhead.

We stress that the polynomial
a_{F}
produced in step
1.3
is
defined over
k
(i.e.
a_{F}\in k[X_{n-s}]
) regardless of whether
the intermediate evaluation points
\alpha_{j}
lie in
k
or in a
finite extension, since both the lifting curve
C_{s}
and the
polynomial
F
are defined over
k
.

The following result shows that Algorithm
41
functions correctly as specified.

Lemma 43
.

Assume that
k
has cardinality at least

\varepsilon^{-1}(D_{s}+1)(\delta_{s}^{2}+D_{s})
. For a lucky choice of

\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{D_{s}}
in step
1.1
, Algorithm

41
correctly computes
a_{F}
. Such a
choice is lucky with probability
1-\varepsilon
.

Proof.

We first prove the correctness of a simplified version of the
algorithm, where steps
1
and
2
are replaced by the
following steps:

1’

Compute
V_{n-s+i}^{s}(X_{n-s},T):=\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},T)^{-1}W_{n-s+i}^{s}(X_{n-s},T)\mod M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
in
k(X_{n-s})[T]
for

2\leq i\leq s
.

2’

Compute
F^{*}(X_{n-s},T):=F(X_{n-s},T,V_{n-s+2}^{s}(X_{n-s},T),\ldots,V_{n}^{s}(X_{n-s},T))
.

3’

Compute
\widetilde{a}:=\mathrm{Res}_{T}\left(M_{s}(X_{n-s},T),F^{*}(X_{n-s},T)\right)\in k[X_{n-s}]
.

For simplicity, we denote by
X_{i}
(
n-s\leq i\leq n
) both the
variables in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
and the corresponding coordinate
functions in
k[C_{s}]
. By Proposition
28
, the
k(X_{n-s})
–vector space

k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]
has dimension
\delta_{s}
.
Since
X_{n-s+1}
is a primitive element and its minimal polynomial

M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
has degree
\delta_{s}
, the set
\{1,X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n-s+1}^{\delta_{s}-1}\}
forms a basis of this vector space.

To perform step
1’
we require that

\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},T)
is
invertible modulo
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
, which holds because these
polynomials are coprime in

k(X_{n-s})[T]
by Proposition
25
.

Taking into account that

\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},T)^{-1}\cdot\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},T)\equiv 1\mod M_{s}(X_{n-s},T),

and
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
is the minimal polynomial of
X_{n-s+1}
in

k[C_{s}]
, we conclude that

\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})^{-1}\cdot\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})\equiv 1

in
C_{s}
. This implies that

X_{n-s+i}\equiv\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})^{-1}\cdot W_{n-s+i}^{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})

(5.1)

in
C_{s}
for
2\leq i\leq s
. The
right–hand side of (
5.1
), reduced by
M_{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})
, provides the representation of

X_{n-s+i}
in terms of the basis
\{1,X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n-s+1}^{\delta_{s}-1}\}
for
2\leq i\leq s
. Hence,

F\equiv F^{*}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})

in
k(X_{n-s})[C_{s}]
, where
F^{*}(X_{n-s},T)
is the polynomial computed in step

2’
. Let
M_{X_{n-s+1}}\in k(X_{n-s})^{\delta_{s}\times\delta_{s}}
be the
matrix of the homothety
\eta_{X_{n-s+1}}
by
X_{n-s+1}
in the
basis
\{1,X_{n-s+1},\ldots,X_{n-s+1}^{\delta_{s}-1}\}
. Then

M_{X_{n-s+1}}
is the companion matrix of
M_{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})
.
As
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
is square–free, the matrix
M_{X_{n-s+1}}
is
diagonalizable, its eigenvalues
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\delta_{s}}
being the
roots of
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
in an algebraic closure of
{k(X_{n-s})}
.
It follows that the matrix of
\eta_{F}=F^{*}(X_{n-s},\eta_{X_{n-s+1}})

in such a basis is
M_{F}=F^{*}(X_{n-s},M_{X_{n-s+1}})
, and its
characteristic polynomial is

\chi_{F}=\prod_{i=1}^{\delta_{s}}(T-F^{*}(X_{n-s},\xi_{i}))
. In
particular,

\chi_{F}(0)=(-1)^{\delta_{s}}\prod_{i=1}^{\delta_{s}}F^{*}(X_{n-s},\xi_{i})=\pm\mathrm{Res}_{T}\left(F^{*}(X_{n-s},T),M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)\right),

due to the expression for the resultant in roots.
Thus, the constant term
\widetilde{a}
of

\eta_{F}
is, up to a sign, as computed in step
3’
.

To justify Algorithm
41
, we note
that in steps
1
–
3
are performed substituting
\alpha_{j}

for
X_{n-s}

for
0\leq j\leq D_{s}
. The preceding arguments show that the polynomial
\widetilde{a}\in k[X_{n-s}]
computed in step
3’
is the constant term of the
characteristic polynomial of the homothety

\eta_{F}:k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]\to k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]
by
F
. According to

(
Giusti et al. 2001
, Corollary 2)
, we have
\deg\widetilde{a}\leq D_{s}:=\delta_{s}\cdot\deg F
. Consequently,
\widetilde{a}
can be
computed via interpolation once
D_{s}+1
pairwise-distinct
evaluations of
\widetilde{a}
are obtained.

Step
1.2
, however,
requires that
\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(\alpha_{j},T)
and

M_{s}(\alpha_{j},T)
be relatively prime for
0\leq j\leq D_{s}
; this
condition holds if and only if
\rho_{s}(\alpha_{j})\not=0
for
0\leq j\leq D_{s}
. Since
\deg\rho_{s}\leq\delta_{s}^{2}
, the probability that one
of
D_{s}
random, non-repeated choices
of
\alpha_{j}
fails is bounded above by

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{D_{s}}\frac{\delta_{s}^{2}+j}{\varepsilon^{-1}(D_{s}+1)(\delta_{s}^{2}+D_{s})-j}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{D_{s}}\frac{\delta_{s}^{2}+j}{\varepsilon^{-1}((D_{s}+1)(\delta_{s}^{2}+D_{s})-j)}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon(D_{s}+1)\frac{\delta_{s}^{2}+D_{s}}{(D_{s}+1)(\delta_{s}^{2}+D_{s})}=\varepsilon.

∎

Next, we analyze the cost of Algorithm
41
.

Lemma 44
.

Suppose that
F
is given by a straight–line program of length
L
.
Then Algorithm
41
requires

\mathcal{O}((L+s\,\log_{2}\delta_{s})\,d\,\delta_{s}\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s}))

arithmetic operations and identity tests in
k
, and

\mathcal{O}(d\,\delta_{s})
extractions of
p
–th roots in
k
(if

k
has characteristic
p>0
).

Proof.

First, assume that a lucky choice of
\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{D_{s}}
in
step
1.1
is made. We then perform steps
1.2
,
1.3
,
and
1.4
of Algorithm
41
for

0\leq j\leq D_{s}
.

For each
j
, step
\mathbf{1.2}
requires the multipoint
evaluations
M_{s}(\alpha_{j},T)
,
({\partial M_{s}}/{\partial T})(\alpha_{j},T)
, and
W_{n-s+i}^{s}(\alpha_{j},T)
for
2\leq i\leq s
.
More precisely, considering
M_{s}
,
{\partial M_{s}}/{\partial T}
,
and
W_{n-s+i}^{s}
as polynomials of
k[X_{n-s}][T]
, each
coefficient in
k[X_{n-s}]
of these polynomials must be evaluated
at
\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{D_{s}}
. Since there are

\mathcal{O}(s\,\delta_{s})
such polynomials, all of degree at most

\delta_{s}
, these evaluations require
\mathcal{O}(s\,\delta_{s}\,{\sf M}(D_{s})\log_{2}D_{s})
arithmetic operations in
k
. Since we have
assumed that
\delta>d
, we may, without loss of generality,
suppose that
\delta_{s}>d
for all
1<s\leq r
. As
D_{s}:=d\,\delta_{s}
, this assumption justifies the inclusion

\mathcal{O}(s\,\delta_{s}\,{\sf M}(D_{s})\log_{2}D_{s})\subset\mathcal{O}(s\,d\,\delta_{s}\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s})\log_{2}\delta_{s}).

This
simplification does not affect the overall asymptotic complexity of
the algorithm, since the case
\delta_{s}\leq d
would only lead to
smaller cost estimates.

Next, the inversion
({\partial M_{s}}/{\partial T})(\alpha_{j},T)^{-1}
, and the multiplication
({\partial M_{s}}/{\partial T})(\alpha_{j},T)^{-1}W_{n-s+i}^{s}(\alpha_{j},T)
for

2\leq i\leq s
, modulo the monic polynomial
M_{s}(\alpha_{j},T)
of
degree
\delta_{s}
, are carried out for
0\leq j\leq D_{s}
. This step
requires an additional
\mathcal{O}(s\,d\,\delta_{s}\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s})\log_{2}\delta_{s})
arithmetic operations and identity
tests in
k
.

In step
\mathbf{1.3}
, we replace
\alpha_{j}
for
X_{n-s}
, and

V_{n-s+i}(\alpha_{j},T)
for
X_{n-s+i}
for
2\leq i\leq s
, in the
straight–line program representing
F
, and perform all arithmetic
operations modulo
M_{s}(\alpha_{j},T)
. This procedure can be performed
with
\mathcal{O}(L\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s}))
arithmetic operations in

k
for each
j
, for a total of
\mathcal{O}(L\,d\,\delta_{s}\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s}))
arithmetic operations in
k
.

Step
\mathbf{1.4}
involves computing
D_{s}+1
resultants of
polynomials of degree at most
\delta_{s}
, requiring

\mathcal{O}(d\,\delta_{s}\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s})\log_{2}\delta_{s})

arithmetic operations and identity tests in
k
. The interpolation
step
2
recovers the dense representation of
\widetilde{a}
at
cost

\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(D_{s})\log_{2}D_{s})\subset\mathcal{O}(d\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s})\log_{2}\delta_{s}).

Finally, step
3
computes the square–free part
a_{F}
of

\widetilde{a}
, which requires
\mathcal{O}(d\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s})\log_{2}\delta_{s})
arithmetic operations and identity
tests in
k
, and
\mathcal{O}(d\,\delta_{s})
extractions of
p
–th
roots in
k
(if
k
has characteristic
p>0
).

On the other hand, if any choice
\alpha_{j}
is not lucky,
then the inversion
(\partial M_{s}/\partial T)(\alpha_{j},T)^{-1}

modulo
M(\alpha_{j},T)
fails, and the computation halts,
incurring similar costs.
∎

For later use, we state a few remarks
on the polynomials
F^{*}
and
a_{F}
.

Remark 45
.

Under the assumptions of Algorithm
41
, let
\rho_{s}:=\mathrm{disc}_{T}M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
, and let

(\xi,\varphi)\in\mathbb{A}^{2}
satisfy
M_{s}(\xi,\varphi)=0
and

\rho_{s}(\xi)\not=0
. Then:

1.

There exists a
unique point
\boldsymbol{x}\in C_{s}
with
F^{*}(\xi,\varphi)=F(\boldsymbol{x})
,
x_{n-s}=\xi

and
x_{n-s+1}=\varphi
.

2.

If
a_{F}(\xi)\not=0
, then
F(\boldsymbol{x})\not=0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
26
,
there is a unique point
\boldsymbol{x}\in C_{s}
with
x_{n-s}=\xi
and

x_{n-s+1}=\varphi
, explicitly given by

\boldsymbol{x}:=\psi(\xi,\varphi):=\left(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\xi,\varphi,V_{n-s+2}^{s}(\xi,\varphi),\ldots,V_{n}^{s}(\xi,\varphi)\right).

It follows that
F(\boldsymbol{x})=F(\psi(\xi,\varphi))=F^{*}(\xi,\varphi)
.
Moreover, if
a_{F}(\xi)\not=0
, then
M_{s}(\xi,T)
and
F^{*}(\xi,T)

have no common zeros in
\overline{k}
, implying

F^{*}(\xi,\varphi)=F(\boldsymbol{x})\not=0
.
∎

We will apply Algorithm
41
to

F_{s+1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})
and
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})
. Proposition
39
ensures that these polynomials satisfy
the input requirements of Algorithm
41
. Let
\chi_{F_{s+1}},\chi_{G}\in k[X_{n-s},T]
be the
characteristic polynomials of the homotheties

\eta_{F_{s+1}},\eta_{G}:k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]\to k(X_{n-s})\otimes_{k[X_{n-s}]}k[C_{s}]
by
F_{s+1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})
and
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})
,
respectively. Denote by
a_{F_{s+1}},a_{G}\in k[X_{n-s}]
the
square–free parts of the constant terms of

\chi_{F_{s+1}},\chi_{G}
, respectively. The following lemma
summarizes their key properties. This result extends the approach of

(
Heintz et al. 2001
, Lemma 8)
, which addressed the case of a closed set
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, to the more general setting of locally closed
sets over an arbitrary perfect field
k
.

Lemma 46
.

The following properties hold:

1.

For any
\xi\in\overline{k}
with
(\rho_{s}\cdot a_{G})(\xi)\not=0
, where

\rho_{s}:=\mathrm{disc}_{T}M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
, we have

a_{F_{s+1}}(\xi)=0
if and only if there exists
\boldsymbol{x}\in\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
with
\pi_{s}^{*}(\boldsymbol{x})=\xi
.

2.

\mathrm{gcd}(m_{s+1},\rho_{s}\cdot a_{G})=1
.

Proof.

For the first assertion, let
\xi\in\overline{k}
satisfy
(\rho_{s}\cdot a_{G})(\xi)\not=0
. If
a_{F_{s+1}}(\xi)=0
, then, since

a_{F_{s+1}}
is the square-free part of the resultant of

M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
and
F_{s+1}^{*}(X_{n-s},T)
with respect to
T
,
there exists
\varphi\in\overline{k}
with

M_{s}(\xi,\varphi)=F_{s+1}^{*}(\xi,\varphi)=0
. By Remark
45
, there is a unique
\boldsymbol{x}\in C_{s}
with

x_{n-s}=\xi
and
x_{n-s+1}=\varphi
, such that
F_{s+1}(\boldsymbol{x})=F_{s+1}^{*}(\xi,\varphi)=0
. That same remark also ensures that

G(\boldsymbol{x})\not=0
. It follows that
\boldsymbol{x}\in C_{s}\cap V(F_{s+1})\setminus V(G)
, that is,
\boldsymbol{x}\in\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
.

Conversely, if
\boldsymbol{x}\in\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
satisfies
\pi_{s}^{*}(\boldsymbol{x})=\xi
, then, since

a_{F_{s+1}}\in k[X_{n-s}]
vanishes on
C_{s}\cap V(F_{s+1})\supset\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
by Lemma
40
, we deduce that
a_{F_{s+1}}(\xi)=0
. This proves the first
assertion.

For the second assertion, suppose that
m_{s+1}
and

\rho_{s}
have a nontrivial common factor in
k[X_{n-s}]
. Then
there exists
\xi\in\overline{k}
such that
\boldsymbol{q}:=(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\xi)\in\pi_{s}\big(\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\big)

and
\rho_{s}(\xi)=0
. By condition
({\sf B}_{4})
,
\boldsymbol{q}
is a lifting point of
\pi_{s}
and

X_{n-s+1}
separates the points of
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{q})
. This
implies
\rho_{s}(\xi)=\mathrm{Disc}_{T}(M_{s}(\xi,T))\not=0
,
contradicting our assumption. We conclude that
m_{s+1}
and

\rho_{s}
cannot share nontrivial common factors in
k[X_{n-s}]
.

On the other hand, if there exists
\xi\in\overline{k}
with

m_{s+1}(\xi)=a_{G}(\xi)=0
, then
\boldsymbol{q}:=(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},\xi)\in\pi_{s}\big(\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\big)
,
and since
\gcd(m_{s+1},\rho_{s})=1
, we have
\rho_{s}(\xi)\not=0
, while

a_{G}(\xi)=0
. Denote
G^{*}(X_{n-s},T):=G(X_{n-s},T,V_{n-s+2}^{s}(X_{n-s},T),\ldots,V_{n}^{s}(X_{n-s},T))
. Since

a_{G}(\xi)=0
, there exists
\varphi\in\overline{k}
with

M_{s}(\xi,\varphi)=G^{*}(\xi,\varphi)=0
. By Remark
45
, there is
\boldsymbol{x}\in\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{q})

with
G^{*}(\xi,\varphi)=G(\boldsymbol{x})=0
. Hence,
\boldsymbol{x}\in C_{s}\cap\{G=0\}
, and thus
\boldsymbol{q}\in\pi_{s}\big(C_{s}\cap\{G=0\}\big)
lies in

\pi_{s}\big(\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})\big)
, which contradicts
condition
({\sf B}_{5})
.
∎

5.2
A Kronecker representation of the
(s+1)
th lifting fiber

Algorithm
41
serves as the foundation
for computing a Kronecker representation of the lifting fiber

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
.

This procedure relies on the following subroutine, which describes
how to compute a Kronecker representation of a zero–dimensional

k
–variety
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}
from the minimal polynomials

m,m_{1},m_{2}\in k[T]
of three separating linear forms

\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}\in k[X,Y]
for

\mathcal{V}
.

Algorithm 47

(Bidimensional shape lemma)
.

Input:

1.

Linear forms
\mathcal{L}:=X,\mathcal{L}_{1}:=X+\lambda_{1}Y,\mathcal{L}_{2}:=X+\lambda_{2}Y\in k[X,Y]
that separate the points of
a zero–dimensional
k
-variety
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}
, with

\mathcal{L}_{2}
separating the points of

\{m(X)=0,m_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{1})=0\}
,

2.

The minimal polynomials

m,m_{1},m_{2}\in k[T]
of
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}
in

\mathcal{V}
.

Output:
A polynomial
v\in k[T]
with
\deg v<\deg\mathcal{V}
and

Y-v(X)\equiv 0
in
\mathcal{V}
.

1
Compute the dense representation of

m_{i}(X+\lambda_{i}Y)\in k[X,Y]
for
i=1,2
.

2
Compute
\mathrm{gcd}\big(m_{1}(X+\lambda_{1}Y),m_{2}(X+\lambda_{2}Y)\big)=Y-v(X)
in
\big(k[X]/(m(X))\big)[Y]
3
3

3

In this context, by the
(monic) gcd
of two polynomials
f_{1},f_{2}
in
(k[X]/(m(X)))[Y]
,
with
m\in k[X]
monic, reduced, but not necessarily irreducible, we mean any monic generator of the ideal

(f_{1},f_{2})\subset(k[X]/(m(X)))[Y]
(see
Moreno Maza and Rioboo 1995
).

.

We remark that Step
2
will be implemented using
dynamic
evaluation
. This approach allows one to compute the greatest common
divisor in
\big(k[X]/(m(X))\big)[Y]
without performing explicit
factorizations, by exploiting the decomposition of
k[X]/(m(X))
as
a product of fields. Adopting dynamic evaluation notably simplifies
the analysis of the success probability of Algorithm 6
in
Heintz et al. 2001
, and avoids the factorization stage required in
the algorithm underlying Lemma 4.5 of
Cafure and Matera 2006
, which was
restricted to the case of finite base fields.

The following lemma justifies the correctness of Algorithm

47
(compare with
(
Heintz et al. 2001
, Lemma
6)
).

Lemma 48
.

With the notations of Algorithm
47
, we have the following assertions:

•

There exists a unique
v\in k[T]
with
\deg v<\#\mathcal{V}
such that
Y\equiv v(X)
in
\mathcal{V}
;

•

Let
\alpha\in\overline{k}
be a root of
m
,
r\in k[T]
the
irreducible factor of
m
vanishing on
\alpha
, and
{\sf K}:=k[T]/(r(T))\cong k(\alpha)
. Then

\mathrm{gcd}\big(m_{1}(\alpha+\lambda_{1}Y),m_{2}(\alpha+\lambda_{2}Y)\big)=Y-v(\alpha)\in{\sf K}[T].

Proof.

The first assertion follows from the classical shape lemma.

Let
\alpha\in\overline{k}
be a root of
m
. Then there exist a unique

\beta\in\overline{k}
such that
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{V}
. Observe that

m_{1}(\alpha+\lambda_{1}\beta)=m_{2}(\alpha+\lambda_{2}\beta)=0
, implying
that
Y-\beta=Y-v(\alpha)
divides the nonzero polynomial

m_{i}(\alpha+\lambda_{i}Y)
for
i=1,2
. We claim that

\mathcal{V}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{A}^{2}:m(x)=0,m_{1}(x+\lambda_{1}y)=0,m_{2}(x+\lambda_{2}y)=0\}.

Define
\mathcal{W}
as the set in right–hand side. The
inclusion
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{W}
is clear since

m(X)
,
m_{1}(X+\lambda_{1}Y)
and
m_{2}(X+\lambda_{2}Y)

vanish on
\mathcal{V}
. Conversely, since
\mathcal{L}_{2}

separates the points of

\mathcal{W}_{1}:=\{m(X)=0,m_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{1})=0\}
, the linear from

\mathcal{L}_{2}
maps injectively
\mathcal{W}_{1}
to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
Restricting
\mathcal{L}_{2}
to
\mathcal{V}
, the image is

\{\xi\in\overline{k}:m_{2}(\xi)=0\}
, showing that

\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{W}
.

From this, we deduce that
\beta
is the only common root in

\overline{k}
of
m_{1}(\alpha+\lambda_{1}Y)
and
m_{2}(\alpha+\lambda_{2}Y)
,
completing the proof.
∎

Next, we analyze the computational cost of Algorithm
47
.

Lemma 49
.

Algorithm
47
can be performed using

\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\#\mathcal{V})^{2}\log_{2}(\#\mathcal{V}))
arithmetic
operations and equality tests in
k
.

Proof.

The dense representation of
m_{i}(X+\lambda_{i}Y)\in k[X,Y]
for

i=1,2
can be obtained by interpolation. More precisely, consider
the ring homomorphism
\sigma:k[X,Y]\to k[X]
defined by the
Kronecker substitution
\sigma(X):=X
,

\sigma(Y):=X^{\#\mathcal{V}+1}
. Any
F\in k[X,Y]
of degree at
most
\#\mathcal{V}
in each variable
X
,
Y
is uniquely
determined by its image
\sigma(F)
(without computational cost). To
compute the dense representation of
\sigma(m_{i}(X+\lambda_{i}Y))=m_{i}(X+\lambda_{i}X^{\#\mathcal{V}+1})
, perform multipoint
evaluations
m_{i}(\alpha_{j}+\lambda_{i}\,\alpha_{j}^{\#\mathcal{V}+1})

at
N:=\#\mathcal{V}(\#\mathcal{V}+1)+1
distinct points

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N}\in k
(or in a finite field extension of
k

if
k
lacks enough elements). Then interpolation reconstructs

m_{i}(X+\lambda_{i}X^{\#\mathcal{V}+1})
, and thus
m_{i}(X+\lambda_{i}Y)
.

The multipoint evaluations can be performed with

\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(N)\log_{2}N)=\mathcal{O}({\sf M}((\#\mathcal{V})^{2})\log_{2}(\#\mathcal{V}))

arithmetic operations in

k
, and within a similar cost the interpolation step is
completed.

To compute

\mathrm{gcd}\big(m_{1}(X+\lambda_{1}Y),m_{2}(X+\lambda_{2}Y)\big)=Y-v(X)

in
\big(k[X]/(m(X))\big)[Y]
, we use dynamical
evaluation (see
Dora et al. 1985
): we perform the Euclidean algorithm
in
k[X][Y]
, computing each arithmetic
operation in
k[X]
modulo
m(X)
. Each test for equality to zero
eventually induces a splitting of the modulo, leading to a
splitting of the computation. Ultimately, we obtain polynomials

v_{1},\ldots,v_{t},m_{1},\ldots,m_{t}\in k[X]
such that
m=m_{1}\cdots m_{t}
,
\deg v_{i}<\deg m_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq t
and

\mathrm{gcd}\big(m_{1}(X+\lambda_{1}Y),m_{2}(X+\lambda_{2}Y)\big)=Y-v_{i}(X)
in
\big(k[X]/(m_{i}(X))\big)[Y]
for
1\leq i\leq t
. The final polynomial
v\in k[X]
is obtained
via Chinese remaindering. By
(
Dahan et al. 2006
, Proposition
4.1)
, this step requires
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\#\mathcal{V})^{2}\log_{2}(\#\mathcal{V}))
arithmetic operations and
equality tests in
k
.

Noting that
{\sf M}((\#\mathcal{V})^{2})\leq{\sf M}(\#\mathcal{V})^{2}
, the lemma follows.
∎

Now we are in position to describe the procedure for computing a
Kronecker representation of the fiber
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
.
According to Lemma
46
, the roots of

a_{F_{s+1}}
are the
(n-s)
th coordinates of the points of

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
, except for those corresponding to roots
of
\rho_{s}
or
a_{G}
. Therefore, by removing from
a_{F_{s+1}}
the
roots of these polynomials, we obtain the minimal polynomial

m_{s+1}
of
X_{n-s}
in
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
.

Through a deformation process, we compute the minimal polynomials of
two linear forms
\mathcal{L}_{1}:=X_{n-s}+\lambda_{1}X_{n-s+1},\mathcal{L}_{2}:=X_{n-s}+\lambda_{2}X_{n-s+1}\in k[X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1}]
.
Using these polynomials, Algorithm
47
computes the polynomial
v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}\in k[T]

parametrizing
X_{n-s+1}
in terms of
X_{n-s}
.

Algorithm 50

(Computation of
m_{s+1}
and
v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}
)
.

Input:

1.

A Kronecker representation of
C_{s}
, as in
the output of Algorithm
32
;

2.

Access to
uniformly random elements of a finite set
\mathcal{S}\subset k
of
size at least
2\varepsilon^{-1}\delta^{4}
, where
0<\varepsilon<1
.

Output:
Polynomials
m_{s+1},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}\in k[T]
with the
following properties:

1.

\deg m_{s+1}=\delta_{s+1}
and
\deg v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}<\delta_{s+1}
;

2.

m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})\equiv 0
and

X_{n-s+1}-v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(X_{n-s})\equiv 0
in
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
.

1
Apply Algorithm
41
to

F_{s+1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})
to compute
a_{F_{s+1}}\in k[X_{n-s}]
.

2
Apply Algorithm
41
to

G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})
to compute
a_{G}\in k[X_{n-s}]
.

3
Compute
\rho_{s}:=\mathrm{disc}_{T}M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)

modulo
a_{F_{s+1}}
.
***[
\rho_{s}\in k[X_{n-s}]\setminus\{0\}
]***

4
Compute
m_{s+1}(X_{n-s}):=a_{F_{s+1}}/\mathrm{gcd}(a_{F_{s+1}},a_{G}\cdot\rho_{s})
.

5
For
i=1,2
, do

5.1
Choose
\lambda_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
at random;

5.2
Set
\mathcal{L}_{i}:=X_{n-s}+\lambda_{i}X_{n-s+1}
;

5.3
Perform the change of variables

X_{n-s}\to\mathcal{L}_{i}-\lambda_{i}X_{n-s+1}
.

5.4
Apply steps
1
–
4
to compute
the minimal polynomial
m_{\lambda_{i}}\in k[\mathcal{L}_{i}]
of

\mathcal{L}_{i}
in
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
.

6
Apply Algorithm
47

to compute the polynomial
v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}
.

Remark 51
.

We note that even if the choices in step
5.2
are made in a
proper finite field extension of
k
, the output remains defined
over
k
. Indeed,
m_{s+1}
is defined over
k
(see Remark

42
), and
v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}
is
defined over
k
, because it parametrizes
X_{n-s+1}
in terms of

X_{n-s}
in
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
, which is itself defined
over
k
.

Lemma 52
.

Assume that
k
has cardinality at least at least

2\varepsilon^{-1}\delta^{4}
. For a lucky choice of

\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
in step
5.1
, Algorithm
50
works as specified. Such a choice is
successful with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Steps
1
and
2
can be carried out by Proposition

39
, and their outputs
are the polynomials
a_{F_{s+1}}
and
a_{G}
, respectively, due to
Lemma
43
. Step

4
yields the polynomial
m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})
by Lemma

46
. A random choice of

\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{S}
produces linear forms

\mathcal{L}_{i}:=X_{n-s}+\lambda_{i}X_{n-s+1}
(
i=1,2
) satisfying
the input requirements of Algorithm
47
. Thus, the polynomial
v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}
is obtained in step
6
.

For the probability estimate, for
i\in\{1,2\}
and

\lambda_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
, the linear form

\mathcal{L}_{i}:=X_{n-s}+\lambda_{i}X_{n-s+1}
must separate the points
of
(s+1)
th lifting fiber
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
. Moreover,

\mathcal{L}_{2}
must separate the points of the zero-dimensional
variety

\mathcal{V}:=\{m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})=0,m_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{1})=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}.

We observe that
\mathcal{L}_{i}
separates the points of

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
if and only if

\prod_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\not=\boldsymbol{\psi}}\big(\mathcal{L}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\xi})-\mathcal{L}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\psi})\big)\not=0,

where the product ranges over all distinct pairs
\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\psi}

in
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
. This expression is a nonzero
polynomial in
\lambda_{i}
of degree
{\delta_{s+1}\choose 2}
.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{L}_{2}
must separate the points of

\mathcal{V}
, which has degree at most
\delta_{s+1}^{2}
. Therefore,
this condition can be expressed as the nonvanishing of a polynomial
in
\lambda_{2}
of degree at most
{\delta_{s+1}^{2}\choose 2}
. Since

\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
are randomly chosen in a set of cardinality

\varepsilon^{-1}\delta^{4}
, the probability that any choice fails is
bounded by

\frac{{\delta_{s+1}\choose 2}}{\varepsilon^{-1}\delta^{4}}+\frac{{\delta_{s+1}\choose 2}+{\delta_{s+1}^{2}\choose 2}}{\varepsilon^{-1}\delta^{4}}\leq\varepsilon,

completing the proof.
∎

It remains to analyze the cost and probability of success of
Algorithm
50
.

Lemma 53
.

Algorithm
50
can be
implemented to run using
\mathcal{O}\big((L+s\,\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)

arithmetic operations in
k
,

\mathcal{O}\big((L+s\,\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\big)
identity tests in
k
and
\mathcal{O}(\delta)

extractions of
p
–th roots in
k
(if
k
has characteristic

p>0
). It succeeds with probability
1-7\varepsilon
.

Proof.

From the straight–line program computing
F_{s+1}
and
G
, we
readily obtain a straight–line program of length
L
that computes

F_{s+1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})
and
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1},X_{n-s},\ldots,X_{n})
. By Lemma
44
, steps
1
and
2
can be performed with

\mathcal{O}((L+s\,\log_{2}\delta_{s})d\,\delta_{s}\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s}))

arithmetic operations and identity tests in
k
, and

\mathcal{O}(\delta_{s})
extractions of
p
–th roots in
k
(if
k

has characteristic
p>0
).

To compute the dense representation of
\rho_{s}
modulo
a_{F_{s+1}}

we calculate the discriminant of
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
over the ring

k[X_{n-s}]/\big(a_{F_{s+1}}(X_{n-s})\big)
. This is done using
dynamical evaluation, as in step
2
of Algorithm
47
. Specifically, we compute the
discriminant of
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
with respect to
T
, carrying out
arithmetic operations in
k[X_{n-s}]
modulo
a_{F_{s+1}}
. Each
equality test eventually leads to a splitting of the computation,
followed by a step of Chinese remaindering to reconstruct the dense
representation of
\rho_{s}
modulo
a_{F_{s+1}}
, as in the proof of
Lemma
49
.

Computing
\mathrm{disc}_{T}M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
modulo
a_{F_{s+1}}

requires
\mathcal{O}\big({\sf M}(\delta_{s})\log_{2}\delta_{s}\big)

arithmetic operations and equality tests in

k[X_{n-s}]/\big(a_{F_{s+1}}(X_{n-s})\big)
. Since
a_{F_{s+1}}
has
degree at most
d\delta_{s}
, each arithmetic operation modulo

a_{F_{s+1}}
costs
\mathcal{O}\big({\sf M}(d\delta_{s})\big)

arithmetic operations in
k
. Therefore, this step can be completed
using
\mathcal{O}\big({\sf M}(\delta_{s}){\sf M}(d\delta_{s})\log_{2}\delta_{s}\big)
arithmetic operations and equality
tests in
k
.

Step
4
requires
\mathcal{O}\big({\sf M}(d\delta_{s})\log_{2}\delta_{s}\big)
arithmetic operations and identity
tests in
k
. Thus, the total cost for steps
1
–
4
is

\mathcal{O}\big((L+s\,\log_{2}\delta_{s})d\,\delta_{s}\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s})+{\sf M}(\delta_{s}){\sf M}(d\delta_{s})\log_{2}\delta_{s}\big)\subset\mathcal{O}\big((L+s\,\log_{2}\delta_{s})\,{\sf M}(d\delta_{s})\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s})\big)

arithmetic operations,

\mathcal{O}\big((L+s\,\log_{2}\delta_{s})d\,\delta_{s}\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s})+{\sf M}(\delta_{s})\,{\sf M}(d\delta_{s})\log_{2}\delta_{s}\big)
identity tests in
k
, and

\mathcal{O}(\delta_{s})
extractions of
p
–th roots in
k
(if
k

has characteristic
p>0
).

In step
5
, we compute the dense representations of

M_{s}\big(\mathcal{L}_{i}-\lambda_{i}X_{n-s+1},X_{n-s+1}\big)
for

i=1,2
. We apply a similar process as for the computation of the
dense representation of
m_{i}(X+\lambda_{i}Y)
in step
1
of
Algorithm
47
, which requires

\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta_{s})^{2}\log_{2}\delta_{s})
arithmetic
operations and equality tests in
k
. We then apply steps
1
–
4
again for
i=1,2
