, with the same asymptotic cost.

Finally, the cost of step
6
is bounded by Lemma
49
, with
\#\mathcal{V}=\delta_{s+1}
.
Combining the costs of all step yields the claimed complexity.

For the probability of success, failure can occur during calls to
Algorithms
41
and
47
. Algorithm
41
is called in steps
1
,
2
, and twice in step

5.4
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. With the input conditions of
Algorithm
41
, each invocation fails
with probability at most
\varepsilon
, so the overall failure
probability is at most
6\varepsilon
. Additionally, Algorithm

47
is called in step
6
and
may fail with probability at most
\varepsilon
. This proves the
lemma.
∎

Finally, it remains to parametrize the variables
X_{n-s+2},\ldots,X_{n}

in terms of
X_{n-s}
in
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
. Recall that

\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})\cdot X_{n-s+i}\equiv W_{n-s+i}^{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})

holds on
C_{s}
, and thus on
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
, for
2\leq i\leq s
. We have the following result.

Lemma 54
.

With the notations above, the polynomial

h_{s}:=\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(X_{n-s}))

is coprime with
m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})
.

Proof.

Since
M_{s}(X_{n-s},X_{n-s+1})
vanishes on
C_{s}
, it also vanishes
on
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
. Therefore,

M_{s}(X_{n-s},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(X_{n-s}))
vanishes on

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
, and thus it is divisible by

m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})
. Moreover, since
\rho_{s}
belongs to the ideal of

k[X_{n-s},T]
generated by
M_{s}(X_{n-s},T)
and
(\partial M_{s}/\partial T)(X_{n-s},T)
, we have

\displaystyle\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})

\displaystyle=b(X_{n-s})\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(X_{n-s}))+c(X_{n-s})M_{s}(X_{n-s},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(X_{n-s}))

\displaystyle\equiv b(X_{n-s})\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(X_{n-s}))\mod m_{s+1}(X_{n-s}),

for some
b,c\in k[X_{n-s}]
. If
m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})
and
h_{s}
shared
a nontrivial common factor in
k[X_{n-s}]
, then
m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})

and
\rho_{s}(X_{n-s})
would also share a nontrivial common factor,
contradicting Lemma
46
.
∎

As a consequence, there exists a well–defined inverse
g_{s}\in k[T]

of
h_{s}
modulo
m_{s+1}(T)
. Therefore, for
2\leq i\leq s
, the
following holds on
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
:

X_{n-s+i}\equiv g_{s}(X_{n-s})\cdot W_{n-s+i}^{s}(X_{n-s},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(X_{n-s})).

(5.2)

Reducing the right–hand side modulo
m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})
for
2\leq j\leq s
yields the remaining polynomials
v_{n-s+2}^{s+1},\ldots,v_{n}^{s+1}\in k[T]
, completing the Kronecker representation of

\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
.

Algorithm 55

(Conclusion of the recursive step)
.

Input:

1.

A Kronecker representation of
C_{s}
, as provided by the output of
Algorithm
32
;

2.

Polynomials

m_{s+1},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}\in k[T]
, as provided by the output of
Algorithm
50
.

Output:
Polynomials
v_{n-s+2}^{s+1},\ldots,v_{n}^{s+1}\in k[T]
satisfying the
following properties:

1.

\deg v_{n-s+j}^{s+1}<\delta_{s+1}
for
2\leq j\leq s
;

2.

X_{n-s+i}-v_{n-s+i}^{s+1}(X_{n-s})\equiv 0
in
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{*})
for
2\leq i\leq s
.

1
Compute
h_{s}:=\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(X_{n-s}))\mod m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})
.

2
Compute
g_{s}:=h_{s}^{-1}\mod m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})
.

3
For
i=2,\ldots,s
, do

3.1
Compute
w_{n-s+i}^{s+1}:=W_{n-s+i}^{s}(X_{n-s},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(X_{n-s}))\mod m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})
;

3.2
Compute

v_{n-s+i}^{s+1}:=g_{s}(X_{n-s})\cdot w_{n-s+i}^{s+1}(X_{n-s})\mod m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})
.

Remark 56
.

The correctness of Algorithm
55

follows directly from Lemma
54

and (
5.2
).

We analyze the cost of Algorithm
55
in the next lemma.

Lemma 57
.

Algorithm
55
can be executed
using
\mathcal{O}\big(s\,\delta\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)
arithmetic
operations in
k
, and
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta))
identity tests
in
k
.

Proof.

Considering
\frac{\partial M_{s}}{\partial T}(X_{n-s},T)
as an
element of
k[X_{n-s}][T]
, its evaluation at

T=v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(X_{n-s})
can be carried out using the well-known
Horner scheme with
\mathcal{O}(\delta_{s})
arithmetic operations in

k[X_{n-s}]
. Thus, computing
h_{s}
in step
1
requires

\mathcal{O}(\delta_{s})
arithmetic operations in
k[X_{n-s}]
modulo

m_{s+1}(X_{n-s})
, amounting to
\mathcal{O}\big(\delta_{s}{\sf M}(\delta_{s+1})\big)
arithmetic operations in
k
. Similarly, each
polynomial
w_{n-s+i}^{s+1}
in step
3.1
can be computed
within the same cost.

The modular inversion in step
2
requires
\mathcal{O}\big({\sf M}(\delta_{s+1})\big)
arithmetic operations in
k
and identity
tests in
k
. Finally, step
3.2
costs
\mathcal{O}\big(s\,{\sf M}(\delta_{s+1})\big)
arithmetic operations in
k
. The lemma then
follows immediately.
∎

6
The whole algorithm and its complexity analysis

Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G

(r\leq n)
be polynomials in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
of
degree at most
d
, such that
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
form a reduced
regular sequence on the open set
\{G\not=0\}
, that is, satisfying
hypotheses (
{\sf H}_{1}
)–(
{\sf H}_{2}
) of Section
3
. Denote by
V_{s}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
the Zariski closure
of
\{F_{1}=0,\ldots,F_{s}=0,G\not=0\}
for
1\leq s\leq r
, and let

V:=V_{r}
. Recall that
V_{s}
has pure dimension
n-s
for
1\leq s\leq r
, and denote by
\delta_{s}
the degree of
V_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq r
.
We denote
\delta:=\max\{\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}\}
and assume that

\delta>d
.

In this section, we describe the whole algorithm for computing a
Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber of
V
and analyze its
cost over a general perfect field
k
. This analysis is expressed in
terms of the number of arithmetic operations, equality tests,
and extractions of
p
–th roots in
k
(if
k
has characteristic

p>0
). We then explain how this algorithm applies in the cases
where
k
is a finite field or the field of rational numbers
\mathbb{Q}
.

6.1
The algorithm for a general field
k

With the notations and assumptions as above, the following algorithm computes a
Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber of the variety
V
.

Algorithm 58

(Main algorithm)
.

Input:

1.

Polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
satisfying
(
{\sf H}_{1}
)–(
{\sf H}_{2}
);

2.

Access to uniformly random
elements of a finite set
\mathcal{S}\subset k
of size at least

\varepsilon^{-1}2n^{2}r\delta^{4}
, where
0<\varepsilon<1
.

Output:

1.

Linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
,

2.

A point
\boldsymbol{p}^{r}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-r})\in k^{n-r}
,

3.

Polynomials
m,w_{n-r+2},\ldots,w_{n}\in k[T]
,

with the
following properties:

1.

Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-r+1}
and
\boldsymbol{p}^{r}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-r})
satisfy the conditions
of Theorem
23
;

2.

The linear map
\pi_{r}:\mathbb{A}^{n}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-r}
defined by
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-r}
, the linear form
Y_{n-r+1}
, and
the polynomials
m,w_{n-r+2},\ldots,w_{n}
yield a Kronecker
representation of the lifting fiber
\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
of
V
.

1
Randomly choose the coefficients of linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
from
\mathcal{S}^{n\times n}
. Set
\boldsymbol{Y}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})
.

2
Randomly choose the coefficients of a point
\boldsymbol{p}\in k^{n-1}

from
\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
. Set
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})
for
1\leq s\leq r
.

3
Compute the polynomials

F_{i}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=F_{i}(\boldsymbol{Y})

(1\leq i\leq r)
and
G^{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=G(\boldsymbol{Y})
.

4
Compute the dense representation
m_{1}\in k[T]
of

m_{1}=F_{1}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T)/\gcd\big(F_{1}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T),G^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T)\big).

5
For
s=1,\ldots,r-1
, do

5.1
Apply Algorithm
32
to compute
the Kronecker representation of

C_{s}:=\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{Y_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s-1}=p_{n-s-1}\}),

with

Y_{n-s+1}
as primitive element;

5.2
Apply Algorithm
50
to compute polynomials

m_{s+1},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}\in k[T]
such that
m_{s+1}(Y_{n-s})\equiv 0
and

Y_{n-s+1}-v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(Y_{n-s})\equiv 0
in
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
, with
\deg m_{s+1}=\delta_{s+1}
and
\deg v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}<\delta_{s+1}
.

5.3
Apply
Algorithm
55
to compute polynomials
v_{n-s+2}^{s+1},\ldots,v_{n}^{s+1}\in k[T]

such that
Y_{n-s+j}-v_{n-s+j}^{s+1}(Y_{n-s})\equiv 0
in
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
for
2\leq j\leq s
,
with
\deg v_{n-s+j}^{s+1}<\delta_{s+1}

for
2\leq j\leq s
.

6
Output
m:=m_{r}
and
w_{n-r+j}:=\frac{\partial m}{\partial T}\cdot v_{n-r+j}^{r}\mod m

for
2\leq j\leq r
.

First we analyze the probability that Algorithm
58
correctly computes a Kronecker representation of a
lifting fiber of the input variety
V
.

Theorem 59
.

Assume that
k
has cardinality at least

\varepsilon^{-1}2n^{2}r\delta^{4}
. Then Algorithm
58
computes a Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber of
V

with probability at least
1-8r\varepsilon
.

Proof.

First, we analyze the probability that the linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
chosen in step
1
and the point
\boldsymbol{p}
chosen in step

2
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
23
for
1\leq s<r
. According to Corollary
24
, this holds if the coefficients
of
\boldsymbol{Y}
and
\boldsymbol{p}
do not annihilate the polynomial
B
from
Corollary
24
. Recall that

\deg B\leq 2n^{2}rd\delta^{3}
.
By Lemma
16
, we conclude that a random
choice of the coefficients of
\boldsymbol{Y}
and
\boldsymbol{p}
is successful
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.

Assume that the linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
chosen in step
1

and the point
\boldsymbol{p}
chosen in step
2
satisfy the conditions
of Theorem
23
for
1\leq s\leq r
.

By definition,
V_{1}
is the Zariski closure of
V(F_{1})\setminus V(G)
, i.e.,
V_{1}
is the union of the irreducible components of

V(F_{1})
not contained in
V(G)
. Since the linear forms

Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
and the point
\boldsymbol{p}^{1}
satisfy the hypotheses of
Theorem
23
, the map

\pi_{1}:V_{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
defined by
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-1}
is a finite
morphism,
\boldsymbol{p}^{1}
is a lifting point of
\pi_{1}
, and
Y_{n}
is a
primitive element of
\pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1})
. In particular, if

\mathcal{C}
is an irreducible component of
V_{1}
and

F_{\mathcal{C}}
is the irreducible factor of
F_{1}
corresponding to

\mathcal{C}
,

\#\big(\mathcal{C}\cap\pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1})\big)=\deg\mathcal{C}=\#\{F_{\mathcal{C}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},Y_{n})=0\}.

For any irreducible component
\mathcal{C}
of

V(F_{1})
not included in
V_{1}
, if
F_{\mathcal{C}}
is the
irreducible factor of
F_{1}
corresponding to
\mathcal{C}
, then

\mathcal{C}\subset V(G)
, and thus
F_{\mathcal{C}}

divides
G
. It follows that
\gcd(F_{1}^{\boldsymbol{Y}},G^{\boldsymbol{Y}})
is the
defining polynomial for the union
V_{1}^{c}
of the irreducible
components of
V(F_{1})
contained in
V(G)
, and
\gcd\big(F_{1}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},Y_{n}),G^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},Y_{n})\big)
defines
the zero-dimensional variety
V_{1}^{c}\cap\{Y_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-1}=p_{n-1}\}
. As a consequence, the
quotient
F_{1}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T)/\gcd\big(F_{1}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T),G^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T)\big)
is the defining polynomial for the zero-dimensional
variety
V_{1}\cap\{Y_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-1}=p_{n-1}\}=V_{1}\cap\pi_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1})
.
This justifies the correctness of the expression of
m_{1}
of step
4
. Since no parametrized variables appear in
the Kronecker representation of the lifting fiber of
V_{1}
under consideration,
the polynomials
m_{1}
itself provides the complete description of
the Kronecker representation of
V_{1}
we seek.

It follows that Algorithm
58
proceeds
into the loop of step
5
as expected. Then,
Theorem
35
establishes the
correctness of the application of Algorithm
32

in step
5.1
. According to Lemmas

52
and

53
, the application of Algorithm

50
in step
5.2

is correct with probability at least
1-7\varepsilon
for
1\leq s<r
,
and thus with probability at least
1-7(r-1)\varepsilon
for the
entire loop.
Finally, the correctness of the application of Algorithm
55
in step
5.3
is justified in Remark

56
. The
conclusion of the theorem then readily follows.
∎

Finally, we analyze the cost of Algorithm
58
.

Theorem 60
.

Suppose that
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
are given by a
straight–line program
\beta
in
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
of length
L
.
Then Algorithm
58
can be executed using

\mathcal{O}\big(n^{4}+r^{2}(L+n^{2}+r^{3}+\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)

arithmetic operations in
k
,

\mathcal{O}\big(r(L+n^{2}+r\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)

identity tests in
k
, and
\mathcal{O}(r\delta)

extractions of
p
–th roots in
k
(if
k
has characteristic

p>0
).

Proof.

Given the coefficients of the linear forms
\boldsymbol{Y}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})
from step
1
, the computation in step
3
consists of performing the change of variables
\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{Y}

in the polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G
. Recall that these polynomials
are given by a straight-line program
\beta
of length
L
. If

\mathcal{M}\in k^{n\times n}
is the matrix defining this change of
variables, namely
\boldsymbol{Y}=\mathcal{M}\boldsymbol{X}
, then the transformed polynomials
are
F_{i}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=F_{i}(\mathcal{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Y})

(1\leq i\leq r)
and
G^{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=G(\mathcal{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Y})
.

Thus, we compute the inverse
\mathcal{M}^{-1}
. Using the
Samuelson-Berkowitz algorithm, this can be done with

\mathcal{O}(n^{4})
arithmetic operations in
k
. Next, we construct a
straight-line program
\beta^{\prime}
to evaluate

F_{1}^{\boldsymbol{Y}},\ldots,F_{r}^{\boldsymbol{Y}},G^{\boldsymbol{Y}}
. For this, we
simply prepend the multiplication
\mathcal{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{Y}
to the
straight-line program
\beta
. Since this
multiplication requires
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
arithmetic operations in
k
,
the straight-line program
\beta^{\prime}
has length
L^{\prime}:=\mathcal{O}(L+n^{2})
.

Step
4
consist of computing the dense representation
of the polynomial
m_{1}
. This involves computing the dense
representations of
F_{1}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T)
and
G^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T)
. As these polynomials have degree at most
d
, their dense
representation can be obtained by executing the
straight-line program
\beta^{\prime}
in the ring
k[T]/(T^{d+1})
.
Specifically, we initialize the variables
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
in
\beta^{\prime}

with
(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T)
and perform all the arithmetic
operations modulo
(T^{d+1})
. Then, given the dense representation
of
F_{1}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T)
and
G^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T)
, we
compute their gcd and finally obtain
m_{1}
by division. All these
computations can be performed with
\mathcal{O}\big(L^{\prime}{\sf M}(d)+{\sf M}(d)\log_{2}d\big)
arithmetic operations in
k
.

Now we consider the cost of the
s
th step of the main loop
5
.
According to Theorem
38
, the step
5.1
can be implemented to run with

\mathcal{O}\big((L^{\prime}s+s^{4}+\log_{2}\delta_{s}){\sf M}(\delta_{s})^{2}\big)

operations in
k
. On the other hand, Lemma
53
asserts that Algorithm
50
requires

\mathcal{O}\big((L^{\prime}+s\,\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)

arithmetic operations in
k
,

\mathcal{O}\big((L^{\prime}+s\,\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)
identity tests in
k
, and
\mathcal{O}(\delta)

extractions of
p
–th roots in
k
. Finally, Lemma
57
shows that Algorithm
55
can be executed with

\mathcal{O}\big(s\,\delta\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)

arithmetic
operations in
k
, and
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta))
identity tests
in
k
. We conclude that the
s
th iteration of the main loop
requires

\mathcal{O}\big((L^{\prime}s+s^{4}+s\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)

arithmetic operations in
k
,

\mathcal{O}\big((L^{\prime}+s\,\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)

identity tests in
k
, and
\mathcal{O}(\delta)

extractions of
p
–th roots in
k
.

Step
6
has a negligible cost in comparison.

By summing the costs, the number of
identity tests, and the number of extractions of
p
–th roots over

1\leq s\leq r
, we readily deduce the theorem.
∎

6.2
The algorithm for a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}

Next, we discuss how Algorithm
58
is
applied when
k
is the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
of
q
elements, where
q

is a power of a prime
p
. Given
r,n
with
r\leq n
, we assume that we
have polynomials

F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
of
degree at most
d
satisfying hypotheses (
{\sf H}_{1}
)–(
{\sf H}_{2}
) of
Section
3
. Our goal is
to compute a Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber
of the Zariski closure
V
of
\{F_{1}=0,\ldots,F_{r}=0,G\not=0\}
.

If the cardinality
q
is “small”, it may happen that no
Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber is defined over

\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
. In that case, we work over a finite extension of
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}

with sufficiently large cardinality, applying
Algorithm
58

mutatis mutandis

over this extension.

On the other hand, for the case where the field

\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
has sufficiently large cardinality so that there
exists a Kronecker representation of a lifting
fiber of
V
defined over
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
, a more detailed discussion is
needed. Specifically, given
0<\varepsilon<1/2
, we
assume from now on that

q>2\varepsilon^{-1}n^{2}rd\delta^{3}
. The algorithm is as
follows.

Algorithm 61

(Main algorithm for
k=\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
)
.

Input:

1.

Polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
satisfying
(
{\sf H}_{1}
)–(
{\sf H}_{2}
);

2.

Access to uniformly random elements of a finite set

\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
of size at least

\varepsilon^{-1}2n^{2}rd\delta^{3}
, where
0<\varepsilon<1/2
.

Output:

1.

Linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
,

2.

A point
\boldsymbol{p}^{r}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-r})\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}^{n-r}
,

3.

Polynomials
m,w_{n-r+2},\ldots,w_{n}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[T]
,

with the following properties:

1.

The linear map
\pi_{r}:V\to\mathbb{A}^{n-r}
defined by
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-r}

is a finite morphism;

2.

\boldsymbol{p}^{r}
is a lifting point of
\pi_{r}
;

3.

The map
\pi_{r}:V\to\mathbb{A}^{n-r}
, together with the linear form
Y_{n-r+1}
and
the polynomials
m,w_{n-r+2},\ldots,w_{n}
, forms a Kronecker
representation of
\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
.

1
Randomly choose the coefficients of linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
from
\mathcal{S}^{n\times n}
. Set
\boldsymbol{Y}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})
.

2
Randomly choose the coefficients
of a point
\boldsymbol{p}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
from
\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
. Set

\boldsymbol{p}^{s}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})
for
1\leq s\leq r
.

3
Compute the polynomials

F_{i}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=F_{i}(\boldsymbol{Y})

(1\leq i\leq r)
and
G^{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=G(\boldsymbol{Y})
.

4
Compute the dense representation
m_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[T]
of

m_{1}=F_{1}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T)/\gcd\big(F_{1}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T),G^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{1},T)\big).

5
For
e:=\max\{1,\log_{q}(12r\varepsilon^{-1}\delta^{4})\}
, set

k:=\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq^{e}}
.

6
For
s=1,\ldots,r-1
, do

6.1
Apply Algorithm
32

to compute a Kronecker representation of

C_{s}:=\pi_{s}^{-1}(\{Y_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s-1}=p_{n-s-1}\}),

with

Y_{n-s+1}
as primitive element;

6.2
Apply Algorithm
50
over
k

to compute polynomials
m_{s+1},v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[T]
such that

m_{s+1}(Y_{n-s})\equiv 0
and

Y_{n-s+1}-v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}(Y_{n-s})\equiv 0
in
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
, with
\deg m_{s+1}=\delta_{s+1}
and
\deg v_{n-s+1}^{s+1}<\delta_{s+1}
.

6.3
Apply
Algorithm
55
to compute
polynomials
v_{n-s+2}^{s+1},\ldots,v_{n}^{s+1}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[T]

such that
Y_{n-s+i}-v_{n-s+i}^{s+1}(Y_{n-s})\equiv 0
in
\pi_{s+1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1})
for
2\leq i\leq s
,
with
\deg v_{n-s+i}^{s+1}<\delta_{s+1}
for
2\leq i\leq s
.

7
Output
m:=m_{r}
and
w_{n-r+j}:=\frac{\partial m}{\partial T}\cdot v_{n-r+j}^{r}\mod m

for
2\leq j\leq r
.

Algorithm
61
is similar to Algorithm

58
, with one important difference: in
the main loop of step
6
we work over an extension
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq^{e}}
of

\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
to ensure that random choices in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq^{e}}
are successful with
high probability. It is worth noting that, in the finite field
setting, the required condition on the cardinality of the base
field
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
is significantly less restrictive than in the case of a
general perfect field
k
. The key point is that, although the
computations in step
6
are carried out over the extension

\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq^{e}}
, the resulting output remains defined over
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
. The bit
complexity and probability of success are analyzed in the following
result.

Theorem 62
.

Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
be polynomials of maximum
degree
d>0
satisfying hypotheses
({\sf H}_{1})
–
({\sf H}_{2})
. For

1\leq s\leq r
, denote by
V_{s}
the Zariski closure of

\{F_{1}=\cdots=F_{s}=0,G\not=0\}
and by
\delta_{s}
its degree. Let

\delta:=\max\{\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}\}
, and assume that

\delta>d
. Suppose that
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
are
given by a straight–line program
\beta
in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
of
length
L
. Given
0<\varepsilon<1/2
, assume that

q>4\varepsilon^{-1}n^{2}rd\delta^{3}
. Then there exists a finite
morphism
\pi:V_{r}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-r}
defined over
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
, and a lifting
fiber
\pi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
of
V_{r}
defined over
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
, with a
Kronecker representation defined over
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
, which can be computed
with bit complexity

\mathcal{O}\Big(\big((n^{4}+r^{2}(L+n^{2}+r^{3}+\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\,{\sf M}(\log_{2}(r\delta))\big)\,{\sf M}(\log_{2}q)\Big)

and probability of success at least
1-2\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Let
B
be the (nonzero) polynomial from Corollary

24
. Since

B\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}]
has degree at most

2n^{2}rd\delta^{3}
, we conclude (by, e.g.,
(
Cafure and Matera 2006
, Lemma 2.1)
)
that
B
has at most

\deg B\cdot q^{n^{2}+n-2}\leq\varepsilon q^{n^{2}+n-1}<q^{n^{2}+n-1}

zeros in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}^{n^{2}+n-1}
. It follows that
there exists
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}^{n^{2}+n-1}
such that

B(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\not=0
. Setting
\boldsymbol{Y}=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
and
\boldsymbol{p}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1})
,
Theorem
23
guarantees that the linear
map
\pi:V\to\mathbb{A}^{n-r}
defined by
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-r}
is a finite
morphism, the fiber
\pi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
defined by
\boldsymbol{p}^{r}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-r})
is a lifting fiber of
\pi
, and
a Kronecker representation of
\pi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
exists over
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
.

Now we explain why Algorithm
61

computes a Kronecker representation of such a lifting fiber
\pi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
with the stated complexity and probability of success.

From the proof of Theorem
60
, we know that
steps
3
and
4
can be executed with

\mathcal{O}\big(L^{\prime}{\sf M}(d)+{\sf M}(d)\log_{2}d\big)
arithmetic
operations in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
, where
L^{\prime}:=\mathcal{O}(L+n^{2})
. We next analyze
the cost of the
s
th iteration of the main loop
6
. According to
Theorem
38
, the step
6.1
can be carried out with

\mathcal{O}\big((L^{\prime}s+s^{4}+\log_{2}\delta_{s}){\sf M}(\delta_{s})^{2}\big)

operations in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
. For step
6.2
, Lemma
53
shows that it requires

\mathcal{O}\big((L^{\prime}+s\,\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)

arithmetic operations in
k:=\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq^{e}}
,

\mathcal{O}\big((L^{\prime}+s\,\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)
identity tests in
k
, and
\mathcal{O}(\delta)

extractions of
p
–th roots in
k
. Since each arithmetic operation or
identity test in
k
requires
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(e))
arithmetic
operations in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
, and each extraction of a
p
–th root in
k

costs
\mathcal{O}(\log(q^{e}/p))
arithmetic operations
in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
, we conclude that the overall cost of step
6.2

is

\mathcal{O}\big((L^{\prime}+s\,\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\,{\sf M}(e)\big)

arithmetic operations in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
.

Finally, by Lemma
57
,
step
6.3
can be executed with

\mathcal{O}\big(s\,\delta\,{\sf M}(\delta)\big)

arithmetic operations in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
. Therefore, the total cost
of the
s
th step of the main loop is

\mathcal{O}\big(s(L^{\prime}+s^{3}+\log_{2}\delta)\,{\sf M}(d\delta)\,{\sf M}(\delta)\,{\sf M}(e)\big)

arithmetic operations in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}
. Summing over
1\leq s\leq r

and noting that each arithmetic operation can be
performed with
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\log_{2}q))
bit operations, we
directly obtain the stated overall complexity bound.

We now analyze the probability of success. The first step is a selection
of
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq}^{n^{2}+n-1}
. By our assumption

q>2\varepsilon^{-1}n^{2}rd\delta^{3}
, we know that

B(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\not=0
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.

Let
\boldsymbol{Y}=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}
and
\boldsymbol{p}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1})
.
Recall
that
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s})
and
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})

satisfy all the conditions of Theorem
23
for
1\leq s<r
. Steps
3
,
4
,
6.1

and
6.3
are deterministic.
Step
6.2
, however, involves random choices through Algorithms
41
and
47
.
Algorithm
41
is invoked six times
per iteration, each call requiring a random choice in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptq^{e}}
.
Using the bound

q^{e}\geq 24r\varepsilon^{-1}\delta^{4}\geq 12r\varepsilon^{-1}(D_{s}+1)(\delta_{s}^{2}+D_{s}),

we deduce that each such random
choice fails with probability at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{12r}
,
yielding an overall failure
probability of at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{2r}
.
Additionally, Algorithm
47
makes
two random choices per iteration. By

q^{e}\geq 2r\varepsilon^{-1}\delta^{4}\geq 2r\varepsilon^{-1}\delta_{s}^{4},

these choices fail with probability at most

\frac{\varepsilon}{2r}
.
Consequently, the total probability of failure in step
6.2
per
iteration is bounded by
\frac{\varepsilon}{r}
, and the probability
of success over all iterations is at least

1-\varepsilon
.

Combining this with the probability of
success for selecting
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})
yields an overall
probability of success of at least
1-2\varepsilon
for Algorithm
61
. This completes the proof.
∎

7
Solving systems defined over
\mathbb{Q}

In this section we consider polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G

(r\leq n)

of
\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
of degree at most
d
, with coefficients
of bit length most
h
, satisfying conditions (
{\sf H}_{1}
)–(
{\sf H}_{2}
) of Section

3
, and describe an algorithm for
computing a Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber
of the Zariski closure
V
of
\{F_{1}=0,\ldots,F_{r}=0,G\neq 0\}
.

A direct application of Algorithm
58

for
k=\mathbb{Q}
may lead to exponential growth in the bit length of the
integers involved in intermediate computations. To avoid this issue,
a modular approach is proposed in
Giménez and Matera 2019
, which consists of
performing the computations modulo a prime number
p
, followed by a
step of
p
–adic lifting and rational reconstruction to recover the
coefficients of the polynomials defining the output Kronecker
representation over
\mathbb{Q}
.

This approach requires, on the one hand, the choice of a “lucky”
prime
p
—that is, one that yields a good modular reduction
with relatively small bit length—and, on the other hand, prior
knowledge of the bit length of the integers appearing in the output
Kronecker representation. In the following, we describe the main
algorithmic aspects of these two steps, referring the reader to

Giménez and Matera 2019
for details and complete proofs.

In the following, the height
{\sf h}(\alpha)
of
\alpha:=p/q\in\mathbb{Q}
,
with
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
coprime, is
{\sf h}(\alpha):=\max\{\log_{2}|p|,\log_{2}|q|\}
; the height of
0
is
defined as
0
. By the bit length of an integer we mean its height.
We define the height
{\sf h}(F)
of a polynomial
F
with
coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}
as the maximum of the heights of its
coefficients. For a prime
p
, we denote by
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
the subring
of
\mathbb{Q}
defined by
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}:\alpha=a/b,\text{
with }a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }p\nmid b\}
. For a polynomial
M

with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
,
M_{p}
denotes the polynomial with
coefficients in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}:=\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
obtained by reduction modulo
p
. We
denote by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
the ring of
p
-adic integers. Given a
p
-adic
integer
\alpha
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, by the
p
-adic
approximation of order
k
of
\alpha
we mean the element
m\in\mathbb{Z}

with
0\leq m<p^{k}
such that
\alpha\equiv m\mod p^{k}
. Furthermore,
for a univariate polynomial
f:=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}T^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T]
of
formal degree
n
, by its
p
-adic approximation of order
k
we
mean the polynomial
g:=\sum_{i=0}^{n}b_{i}T^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}[T]
with
0\leq b_{i}<p^{k}
and
a_{i}\equiv b_{i}\mod p^{k}
for
0\leq i\leq n
.

7.1
The choice of a lucky prime
p
for a good modular reduction

The first task is to make a “lucky” choice of an integer matrix

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n^{2}}
and an integer point
\boldsymbol{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
,
which will serve as the
matrix of coefficients for the linear forms defining our change of
variables, and the coordinates of our lifting points. We seek such
integers to have relatively small bit length.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}:=(\Lambda_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
denote a set of
n^{2}

indeterminates over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
. According to
Giménez and Matera 2019
,
there exists a nonzero polynomial
\textsf{R}\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}]
such that

\deg\textsf{R}\leq D:=r(n+1)((n+1)d\delta^{2}+2\delta^{3}+n^{2}2^{n-1}d\delta^{2}),

(7.1)

which satisfies the following property: for every
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n^{2}}
such that
\textsf{R}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\neq 0
, there exists a
nonzero polynomial
\textsf{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{Z}[Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n-1}]
with
\deg\textsf{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\leq D
such that, if

\mathfrak{N}:=\det(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\textsf{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{p})\neq 0

for some
\boldsymbol{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
, then

\mathfrak{N}
is divisible by every “unlucky” prime. We will call
such a pair
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})
and any prime
p
not dividing
\mathfrak{N}

“lucky”.

More precisely, the polynomials
R
and

\textsf{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
are constructed
so that, for a lucky pair
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})
and a
lucky prime
p
, the hypotheses (
{\sf H}_{1}
)–(
{\sf H}_{2}
) of Section

3
, as well as conditions

(1)
–
(4)
of Theorem
23
, remain
satisfied after reducing
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G
modulo
p
. To state
this precisely, we introduce the following notations.

For

(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n^{2}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
, define

Y_{i}:=\lambda_{i1}X_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{in}X_{n}
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{s}:=(\lambda_{ij})_{\scriptscriptstyle 1\leq i\leq n-s+1,1\leq j\leq n}
, and
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})
for
1\leq s\leq r
. Let
\pi_{s}:V_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
denote the linear map
defined by
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}
. Given a prime
p
, denote by

V_{s,p}
the Zariski closure in
\mathbb{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}}^{n}:=\mathbb{A}^{n}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp})
of the set

\{F_{1,p}=0,\ldots,F_{s,p}=0,G_{p}\neq 0\}
for
1\leq s\leq r
.

With this notation, we can state the first result on the properties of lucky
pairs
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})
and lucky primes
p
(see

(
Giménez and Matera 2019
, Theorem 5.10)
).

Theorem 63
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n^{2}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
be a lucky pair, and let

\mathfrak{N}:=\det(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\textsf{N}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{p})
.
Then conditions
(1)
–
(4)
of Theorem
23
hold for
1\leq s<r
. Furthermore,
if
p
is a prime such that
p\nmid\mathfrak{N}
, the linear
forms
Y_{1,p},\ldots,Y_{n,p}
define a new set of variables in

\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, and the following hold for

1\leq s<r
, with
\boldsymbol{q}:=\boldsymbol{p}^{s}
and
\boldsymbol{q}^{*}:=\boldsymbol{p}^{s+1}
:

1.

The polynomials
F_{1,p},\ldots,F_{s,p}
and
G_{p}
satisfy
hypotheses
({\sf H}_{1})
–
({\sf H}_{2})
of Section
3

for
k:=\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}
. Moreover,
V_{s,p}\subset\mathbb{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}}^{n}

has degree
\delta_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq r
.

2.

The map
\pi_{s,p}:V_{s,p}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}}^{n-s}

defined by
Y_{1,p},\dots,Y_{n-s,p}
is a finite morphism;

\boldsymbol{q}_{p}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}^{n-s}
is a lifting point of
\pi_{s,p}

with
\pi_{s,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{q}_{p})\subset\{G_{p}\neq 0\}
;
and
Y_{n-s+1,p}
is a primitive element of
\pi_{s,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{q}_{p})
.

3.

The map
\pi_{s+1,p}:V_{s+1,p}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}}^{n-s-1}

defined by
Y_{1,p},\dots,Y_{n-s-1,p}
is a finite morphism;

\boldsymbol{q}^{*}_{p}
is a lifting point of
\pi_{s+1,p}

with
\pi_{s+1,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{q}_{p}^{*})\subset\{G_{p}\neq 0\}
; and

Y_{n-s,p}
is a primitive element of
\pi_{s+1,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{q}^{*}_{p})
.

4.

Any
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\pi_{s,p}\bigl(\pi_{s+1,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{q}^{*}_{p})\bigr)

is a lifting point of
\pi_{s,p}
, and
Y_{n-s+1,p}
is a primitive element
of
\pi_{s,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
.

5.

No point of
\pi_{s,p}(W_{\boldsymbol{q}_{p}^{*}}\cap\{G_{p}=0\})

belongs to
\pi_{s,p}\bigl(\pi_{s+1,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{q}^{*}_{p})\bigr)
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n^{2}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
be a
lucky pair. Then the linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
define a new set
of variables for
\mathbb{Q}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, the map

\pi_{s}:V_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
defined by
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s}
is a finite
morphism, and
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}
is a lifting point of
\pi_{s}
, for
1\leq s\leq r
. Let
m_{s}

w^{s}_{n-s+2},\ldots,w^{s}_{n}
be the polynomials of

\mathbb{Q}[T]
which, together with the linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
, the map

\pi_{s}:V_{s}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n-s}
, and the point
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}
, define a
Kronecker representation of the fiber
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, with

Y_{n-s+1}
as primitive element, for
1\leq s\leq r
. Thus,
m_{s}
is
the minimal polynomial of
Y_{n-s+1}
in
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, and we have

m^{\prime}_{s}(Y_{n-s+1})Y_{n-s+i}\equiv w^{s}_{n-s+i}(Y_{n-s+1})\textrm{ in }\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})

for
2\leq i\leq s
, with
\deg m_{s}=\delta_{s}
and

\deg w^{s}_{n-r+i}<\delta_{s}
for
2\leq i\leq s
.

The following result asserts that the output obtained
modulo a lucky prime
p
in Algorithm
78

corresponds to the first-order
p
-adic approximations of the polynomials
in the Kronecker representation over
\mathbb{Q}
(see
(
Giménez and Matera 2019
, Proposition
5.12)
).

Theorem 64
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n^{2}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
be a lucky
pair, and let
\mathfrak{N}
be the integer from Theorem
63
. For
1\leq s\leq r
, let:

•

m_{s},w^{s}_{n-s+2},\ldots,w^{s}_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}[T]
denote a
Kronecker representation
of the lifting fiber
\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, with
Y_{n-s+1}
as
primitive element;

•

M_{s},W^{s}_{n-s+2},\ldots,W^{s}_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}[Y_{n-s},T]

denote a Kronecker representation
of the lifting curve
C_{s}=\pi_{s}^{-1}(Y_{1}=p_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-s-1}=p_{n-s-1})
, with
Y_{n-s+1}
as primitive element.

If
p
is a prime such that
p\nmid\mathfrak{N}
, then all these
polynomials have coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
, and their reductions
modulo
p
yield:

•

a Kronecker representation
m_{s,p},w^{s}_{{n-s+2},p},\ldots,w^{s}_{n,p}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]

of the lifting fiber
\pi_{s,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s}_{p})
, with

Y_{{n-s+1},p}
as a primitive element; and

•

a Kronecker representation
M_{s,p},W^{s}_{{n-s+2},p},\ldots,W^{s}_{n}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[Y_{n-s},T]

of the lifting curve
C_{s,p}:=\pi_{s,p}^{-1}(\{Y_{1,p}=p_{1,p},\ldots,Y_{n-s-1,p}=p_{n-s-1,p}\})
, with
Y_{{n-s+1},p}
as a primitive
element.

Finally, an additional property of a lucky prime
p
is required for the

p
-adic lifting step in Algorithm
78
to
succeed (see Proposition
73
): namely,
p
must not divide either the integer

\alpha_{s}
defined in
(
Giménez and Matera 2019
, (5.4))
, or the integer
A_{s}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{s+1})
. Consequently, we have the following result.

Proposition 65
.

Under the hypotheses and notations of Theorem
64
,
for
1\leq s\leq r
the polynomial
m_{s}
belongs to
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}[T]
, and
the polynomial
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},Y_{n-s+1},\ldots,Y_{n})m_{s}(Y_{n-s+1})
belongs to the
radical of the ideal generated in
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}[Y_{n-s+1},\ldots,Y_{n}]
by the
polynomials

F_{1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},Y_{n-s+1},\ldots,Y_{n}),\ldots,F_{s}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s},Y_{n-s+1},\ldots,Y_{n})
.

Next we analyze the probability of success in selecting a lucky
pair
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})
and a lucky prime
p
.
Let
0<\varepsilon<1
, and define the set
\mathcal{S}:=\{0,\dots,\lfloor 2\varepsilon^{-1}D\rfloor\}
, where
D
is defined as
in (
7.1
). The following result shows that a
pair
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})
chosen uniformly at random in

\mathcal{S}^{n^{2}}\times\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
is lucky with high
probability (see
(
Giménez and Matera 2019
, Lemma 6.1)
).

Lemma 66
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})

be a pair chosen uniformly at random in
\mathcal{S}^{n^{2}}\times\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
. Then the probability that
\textsf{R}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cdot\textsf{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{p})\neq 0
is greater than
1-\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Since
\deg\textsf{R}\leq D
, it follows from Lemma
16

that for a random choice of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
in

\mathcal{S}^{n^{2}}
, the probability that
\textsf{R}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\neq 0
is greater that
1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Similarly, as

\deg\textsf{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\leq D
, for a
point
\boldsymbol{p}
chosen uniformly at random in
\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
,
the conditional probability that
\textsf{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{p})\neq 0
, given that
\textsf{R}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\neq 0
, is greater than
1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Therefore, the
probability that
\textsf{R}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cdot\textsf{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{p})\neq 0
for a
random
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})
in
\mathcal{S}^{n^{2}}\times\mathcal{S}^{n-1}

is at least

(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2})^{2}\geq 1-\varepsilon
.
∎

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathcal{S}^{n^{2}}\times\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
be a lucky pair. Using height estimates from

D’Andrea et al. 2013
, we obtain upper bounds for the heights of the
polynomials defining a Kronecker representation of the lifting fiber

\pi_{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{s})
, with
Y_{n-s+1}
as primitive element (see

(
Giménez and Matera 2019
, Theorem A.7)
), and the integer
\mathfrak{N}
(see

(
Giménez and Matera 2019
, Theorem A.20)
).

Proposition 67
.

With the notation of Lemma
66
and
Theorem
63
, let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathcal{S}^{n^{2}}\times\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
be a lucky pair. Then
the maximum height
\eta_{s}
of the polynomials
m_{s},w^{s}_{n-s+2},\ldots,w^{s}_{n}
satisfies
\eta\in\mathcal{O}^{\sim}\bigl(nd^{s-1}(h+nd)\bigr)
.
Moreover, there exists an integer
\mathfrak{H}
such that

{\sf h}(\mathfrak{N})\leq\mathfrak{H}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\log_{2}\mathfrak{H}\in\mathcal{O}^{\sim}\bigr(\log_{2}(d^{r}2^{n}h\log_{2}\varepsilon^{-1})\bigl).

(7.2)

For the computation of a lucky prime, we rely on the following
well–known result for finding a small prime not dividing
a given integer.

Lemma 68
.

Let
B
,
\ell
be positive integers, and let
M
be a nonzero
integer such that
\log_{2}|M|\leq\frac{B}{\ell}
. Then there exists
a randomized algorithm which, given
B
and any positive integer

k
, returns a prime
p
with
B<p\leq 2B
such that
p\nmid M
. The
algorithm uses an expected number of
\mathcal{O}^{\sim}(k\,\log_{2}^{2}B)
bit operations and returns a correct result with probability at
least

\Bigl(1-\frac{\log_{2}B}{2^{k-1}}\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{2}{\ell}\Bigr).

Proof.

According to, e.g.,
(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Theorem 18.8)
, there exists a
randomized algorithm that computes a random prime
p
with
B<p\leq 2B
using an expected number of
\mathcal{O}^{\sim}(k\,\log_{2}^{2}B)

bit operations and with probability at least
1-{\log_{2}B}/{2^{k-1}}
. Furthermore, a randomly chosen prime
p
in this
interval satisfies
p\nmid M
with probability at least

1-{2}/{\ell}
. Combining these two probabilities yields the result.
∎

We conclude with the following lemma, which guarantees the computation of
a lucky prime of small bit length.

Lemma 69
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
63
and
Proposition
67
, there exists a
randomized algorithm that, given
\mathfrak{H}
, computes a prime

p
satisfying
4\varepsilon^{-1}\mathfrak{H}<p\leq 10\varepsilon^{-1}\mathfrak{H}
such that
p\nmid\mathfrak{N}
.
The algorithm uses an expected number of
\mathcal{O}^{\sim}\bigr(\log_{2}^{2}(d^{r}2^{n}h\varepsilon^{-1})\log_{2}(\varepsilon^{-1})\bigl)
bit operations
and succeeds with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.

Proof.

The lemma follows applying Lemma
68
with

\ell:=\lceil 4\varepsilon^{-1}\rceil
,
B:=\ell\mathfrak{H}
and

k:=\lceil\log_{2}\ell+\log_{2}(\log_{2}\ell\mathfrak{H})\rceil
, and
by invoking inequality
(
7.2
)
.
∎

7.2
Reconstruction of a Kronecker representation over
\mathbb{Q}

Let
m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}[T]
be a Kronecker
representation of the lifting fiber
\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
, with

Y_{n-r+1}
as primitive element. Let
p
be a lucky prime as in
Theorems
63
and
64
. By
Theorem
64
, the reduction modulo
p
of these
polynomials yields a Kronecker representation
m_{r,p},w^{r}_{n-r+2,p},\ldots,w^{r}_{n,p}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]
of the lifting fiber

\pi_{r,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{r})
, with
Y_{n-r+1,p}
as primitive
element.

In this section, we describe a
p
-adic lifting procedure to
reconstruct the polynomials
m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}
from

m_{r,p},w^{r}_{n-r+2,p},\ldots,w^{r}_{n,p}
. Briefly, this procedure
computes
p
-adic approximations of these polynomials to sufficient
precision, followed by rational reconstruction of their coefficients
(see Lemma
75
).

The key tool enabling this
p
-adic lifting is the following
algorithm (see
(
Giusti et al. 2001
, Theorem 2)
). This algorithm is a
version of Algorithm
32
, but defined over

\mathbb{Z}
instead of
k[\![X_{n-s}-p_{n-s}]\!]
. The correctness of
Algorithm
70
follows as that of
Proposition
33
by analogous
arguments.

Algorithm 70

(Global Newton Iterator)
.

Input:

1.

Polynomials
G_{1},\ldots,G_{r}
in
\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}]
;

2.

A monic polynomial

m\in\mathbb{Z}[T]
and polynomials
\boldsymbol{v}:=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})\in\mathbb{Z}[T]^{r}
with

\deg v_{i}<\deg m
for
1\leq i\leq r
;

3.

An ideal
{\sf I}\subset\mathbb{Z}
;

such that the following conditions are satisfied in
(\mathbb{Z}/{\sf I})[T]
:

–

v_{1}=T
;

–

G_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})\equiv 0\mod m
for
1\leq i\leq r
;

–

If
J\in\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}]^{r\times r}
is the Jacobian
matrix of
G_{1},\ldots,G_{r}
with respect to
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}
, then
J(\boldsymbol{v})
is invertible modulo
m
.

Output:

A monic polynomial
M\in\mathbb{Z}[T]
and
\boldsymbol{V}:=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{r})\in\mathbb{Z}[T]^{r}
with
\deg M=\deg m
and
\deg V_{i}<\deg M
for
1\leq i\leq r
, such that
M\equiv m
and
V_{i}\equiv v_{i}\mod{\sf I}
for

1\leq i\leq r
, and satisfying in
(\mathbb{Z}/{\sf I}^{\,2})[T]
:

–

V_{1}=T
;

–

G_{i}(\boldsymbol{V})\equiv 0\mod(M)
for
1\leq i\leq r
.

1.
Compute
J(\boldsymbol{v})^{-1}\in(\mathbb{Z}/{\sf I})[T]^{r\times r}
, the inverse of
J(\boldsymbol{v})
in
(\mathbb{Z}/{\sf I})[T]/(m)
.

2.
In
(\mathbb{Z}/{\sf I}^{\,2})[T]/(m)
, perform:

2.1.

Compute

\boldsymbol{w}^{t}:=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{r}):=\boldsymbol{v}^{t}-J(\boldsymbol{v})^{-1}\cdot(F_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,F_{r}(\boldsymbol{v}))^{t}.

2.2.
Compute
\Delta:=w_{1}-T
.

2.3.
For
i=1,\ldots,r
, do

2.3.1.
Compute
\Delta_{i}:=\Delta\frac{\partial w_{i}}{\partial T}
.

2.3.2.
Set
V_{i}:=w_{i}-\Delta_{i}
.

2.4
Compute
\Delta_{m}:=\Delta\frac{\partial m}{\partial T}
.

2.5
Set
M:=m-\Delta_{m}
.

3.
Return
M
and
\boldsymbol{V}:=(V_{1},\dots,V_{r})
.

Remark 71
.

The polynomial matrix
J(\boldsymbol{V})
is invertible modulo
M
in

(\mathbb{Z}/{\sf I^{2}})[T]^{r\times r}
. Indeed, if
g\in(\mathbb{Z}/{\sf I})[T]^{r\times r}
denotes the inverse of
J(\boldsymbol{v})
in
((\mathbb{Z}/{\sf I})[T]/(m))^{r\times r}
, then
2g-J(\boldsymbol{V})g^{2}
is the inverse of

J(\boldsymbol{V})
in
((\mathbb{Z}/{\sf I}^{2})[T]/(M))^{r\times r}
(see, e.g.,

(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, Theorem 9.2)
). This fact ensures that Algorithm

70
can be applied iteratively.
Furthermore, if
m^{\prime}
is invertible modulo
m
in
(\mathbb{Z}/{\sf I})[T]
,
then, by a similar argument, it follows that
M^{\prime}
is invertible
modulo
M
in
(\mathbb{Z}/{\sf I^{2}})[T]
.

Now assume that a lucky choice of
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathcal{S}^{n^{2}}\times\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
has been made as in Lemma

66
. Let
m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}[T]
denote the corresponding Kronecker representation of
the lifting fiber
\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
, with
Y_{n-r+1}
as
primitive element. Assume further that a lucky prime
p
has been
computed as in Lemma
69
, and
let
m_{r,p},w^{r}_{{n-r+2},p},\ldots,w^{r}_{n,p}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]
be the
Kronecker representation of the lifting fiber
\pi_{r,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{r})
, with
Y_{{n-r+1},p}
as primitive element.

Define
v^{r}_{n-r+1,p}:=T
, and

v^{r}_{n-r+i,p}:=(m^{\prime}_{r,p})^{-1}w^{r}_{n-r+i,p}\mod m_{r,p}
for
2\leq i\leq r
. Moreover, denote
F_{i}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=F_{i}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})
as
the polynomial obtained by rewriting
F_{i}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
in terms
of the new variables
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
, and let
J_{r}:=({\partial F_{i}}/{\partial Y_{n-r+j}})_{1\leq i,j\leq r}
. Since
\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{r}
is
a lifting point of
\pi_{r,p}:V_{r,p}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}}^{n-r}
,
the Jacobian determinant
\det J_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1,p},\ldots,Y_{n,p})
of the polynomials
F_{1,p}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1,p},\ldots,Y_{n,p}),\dots,F_{r,p}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1,p},\ldots,Y_{n,p})
with
respect to the variables
Y_{n-r+1,p},\ldots,Y_{n,p}
induces an
invertible element in the coordinate ring
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[\pi_{r,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p})]
of the lifting fiber.

Thus, letting
\boldsymbol{v}_{p}:=(v^{r}_{n-r+1,p},\ldots,v^{r}_{n,p})
, we have in

(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})[T]
:

•

v^{r}_{n-r+1,p}=T
;

•

F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{v}_{p})\equiv 0\mod m_{r,p}
for
1\leq i\leq s
;

•

J_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{v}_{p})
is invertible modulo
m_{r,p}
.

We can therefore apply Algorithm
70

with input
G_{i}:=F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n})
for
1\leq i\leq r
,
I=p\mathbb{Z}
,
m:=m_{r,p}
, and
\boldsymbol{v}:=\boldsymbol{v}_{p}:=(T,v^{r}_{{n-r+2},p},\ldots,v^{r}_{n,p})
. Iterating this procedure yields
the following algorithm to compute
p
-adic approximations of
arbitrary order of the polynomials
m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}
.

Algorithm 72

(
p
-adic lifting)
.

Input:

1.

The polynomials
F_{1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n}),\ldots,F_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n})
;

2.

A lucky prime
p
as in Theorem
63
;

3.

A Kronecker representation
m_{r,p},w^{r}_{{n-r+2},p},\ldots,w^{r}_{n,p}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]

of the lifting fiber
\pi_{r,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r}_{p})
, with
Y_{{n-r+1},p}
as primitive element;

4.

An integer
k:=2^{j_{0}}
.

Output:
The
p
–adic approximations of order
k
of the polynomials

m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}[T]
which form a
Kronecker representation of the lifting fiber
\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
, with

Y_{n-r+1}
as primitive element.

1
Compute
v_{n-r+i,p}:=(m^{\prime}_{r,p})^{-1}w^{r}_{n-r+i,p}\mod m_{r,p}
for
2\leq i\leq r
.

2
Set
M^{0}:=m_{r,p}
,
V^{0}:=T
and
V^{0}_{n-r+i}:=v^{r}_{n-r+i,p}
for
2\leq i\leq r
.

3.
While
0\leq j<j_{0}
, do

3.1
Apply Algorithm
70
on input

G_{i}:=F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1,\ldots,Y-n})
for
1\leq i\leq r
,
m:=M^{j}
and

\boldsymbol{v}:=(V^{j}_{n-r+1},\ldots,V^{j}_{n})
, with
{\sf I}:=p^{2^{j}}\mathbb{Z}
.

3.2
Set
M^{j+1}
,
V^{j+1}_{n-r+1},\ldots,V^{j+1}_{n}
as the output of the previous step.

3.3
Set
j:=j+1
.

4.
Return
M^{j_{0}}
and
W^{j_{0}}_{n-r+i}:=(M^{j_{0}})^{\prime}V^{j_{0}}_{n-r+i}\mod M^{j_{0}}
for
2\leq i\leq r
.

Proposition 73
.

Algorithm
72
works correctly as
stated.

Proof.

By the correctness of Algorithm
70
,
we know that steps
1
–
3
of Algorithm
72
are well-defined. It remains to prove that
the algorithm indeed outputs the claimed
p
-adic approximations.

For each
j\geq 0
, let
M^{j}
,
V^{j}_{n-r+1},\ldots,V^{j}_{n}
be the
polynomials computed in steps
3.1
and
3.2
of the
algorithm. Define

W^{j}_{n-r+i}:=(M^{j})^{\prime}V^{j}_{n-r+i}\mod M^{j}

in

(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{j}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
for
2\leq i\leq r
and
j\geq 0
. Denote by

m^{j}_{r}
and
w^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}
the
p
-adic approximations of
order
2^{j}
of
m_{r}
and
w^{r}_{n-r+i}
, respectively. We aim to
prove that for every
j\geq 0
,

M^{j}\equiv m^{j}_{r}\textrm{ and }W^{j}_{n-r+i}\equiv w^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}\mod p^{2^{j}}

for
2\leq i\leq r
. This will establish the correctness of the
algorithm.

Define
\boldsymbol{V}^{j}:=(V^{j}_{n-r+1},\ldots,V_{n}^{j})
for
j\geq 0
. Then, for
each
j\geq 0
, the following hold in
(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{j}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
:

\displaystyle F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})

\displaystyle\equiv 0\mod M^{j}\textrm{ for }1\leq i\leq r;

(7.3)

\displaystyle\textrm{Both }(M^{j})^{\prime}\textrm{ and }

\displaystyle J_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})\textrm{
are invertible modulo }M^{j}.

(7.4)

Since
M^{j+1}\equiv M^{j}
and
V^{j+1}_{n-r+i}\equiv V^{j}_{n-r+i}\mod p^{2^{j}}
for
1\leq i\leq r
and all
j\geq 0
, the sequences

(M^{j})_{j\geq 0}
and
(V^{j}_{n-r+i})_{j\geq 0}
converge in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T]

under the
p
-adic topology, where
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is the ring of
p
-adic
integers. Let
M^{\infty}
and
V^{\infty}_{n-r+i}
denote their
limits for
1\leq i\leq r
, and write
\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty}:=(V^{\infty}_{n-r+1},\ldots,V^{\infty}_{n})
.

Passing to the limit in
(
7.3
)
, we
have in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T]
:

F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})\equiv 0\mod M^{\infty}\textrm{ for }1\leq i\leq r.

(7.5)

Moreover, since
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
is a subring of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, by Proposition

65
there exists an integer
\mu>0
such that

(G(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n})m_{r}(Y_{n-r+1}))^{\mu}\in(F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n}):1\leq i\leq r)\mathbb{Z}_{p}[Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n}].

Substituting
\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty}
for
(Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n})
, and using
(
7.5
) we obtain in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T]
:

(G(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})m_{r})^{\mu}\equiv 0\mod M^{\infty}.

(7.6)

We claim that
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})
is invertible modulo

M^{\infty}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T]
. Indeed, since
M^{\infty}\equiv M^{0}
and

\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty}\equiv\boldsymbol{V}^{0}\mod p
,

\mathrm{Res}\big(G(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty}),M^{\infty}\big)\equiv\mathrm{Res}\big(G(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{0}),M^{0}\big)\mod p.

We know that
M^{0}=m_{r,p}
is the minimal polynomial of

Y_{n-r+1,p}
in
\pi^{-1}_{r,p}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r}_{p})
and
\boldsymbol{V}^{0}=(T,v^{r}_{n-r+2,p},\ldots,v^{r}_{n,p})
parametrizes this fiber. Therefore,

\pi^{-1}_{r,p}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r}_{p})=\{(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{0}(\alpha)):m_{r,p}(\alpha)=0\}.

Since
\pi^{-1}_{r,p}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r}_{p})\subset\{G_{p}\neq 0\}
, the polynomials
m_{r,p}
and
G(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{0})
have no common roots in
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}
, and their resultant is
nonzero. It follows that
\mathrm{Res}_{T}\big(G(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty}),M^{\infty}\big)
is a unit of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, which implies the
claim.

From (
7.6
), we deduce

(m_{r})^{\mu}\equiv 0\mod M^{\infty}.

(7.7)

Since
M^{0}=m_{r,p}\in(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})[T]
is monic and square-free, we see
that
M^{\infty}
is monic and square-free in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T]
. Therefore,
by (
7.7
) it follows that
m_{r}\equiv 0\mod M^{\infty}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T]
. But as
\deg M^{\infty}=\deg m_{r}
and both polynomials are monic, we conclude

M^{\infty}=m_{r}.

(7.8)

As a consequence,

M^{j}\equiv M^{\infty}=m_{r}\equiv m^{j}_{r}\mod p^{2^{j}}\textrm{ for all
}j\geq 0,

as asserted.

Next, for each
j\geq 0
, let
v^{r,\,j}_{n-r+2},\ldots,v^{r,\,j}_{n}
be the
p
-adic approximations of order
2^{j}
of

v^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,v^{r}_{n}
. We will prove that
v^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}\equiv V^{j}_{n-r+i}\mod p^{2^{j}}
for every
j\geq 0
. We
proceed by induction on
k
for
0\leq k\leq j
, proving

v^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}\equiv V^{j}_{n-r+i}\mod p^{2^{k}}.

The base case
k=0
holds since by construction

v^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}\equiv v^{r}_{n-r+i,p}=V^{0}_{n-r+i}\equiv V^{j}_{n-r+i}\mod p.

Suppose that the claim holds for
k-1
. Define
\boldsymbol{v}:=(T,v^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,v^{r}_{n})
. Consider the Taylor expansion of
\boldsymbol{F}_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n})
around
\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty}
:

\boldsymbol{F}_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{v})-\boldsymbol{F}_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})=J_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})\cdot(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})+\mathcal{O}\big((\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})^{2}\big).

(7.9)

Since
m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}
form a Kronecker representation
of
\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
, with
Y_{n-r+1}
as primitive element,

F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{v})\equiv 0\mod m_{r}
in
\mathbb{Q}[T]
for
1\leq i\leq r
. Moreover, as
m_{r}
and
v^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,v^{r}_{n}
belong to

\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}[T]
, and
m_{r}
is monic, we deduce that
F_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{v})\equiv 0\mod m_{r}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}[T]
, and hence in

\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T]
, for
1\leq i\leq r
. Together with (
7.5
), (
7.8
)
and (
7.9
), this yields

0\equiv J_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})\cdot(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})+\mathcal{O}\big((\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{V}^{\infty})^{2}\big)\mod M^{\infty}

in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T]
. Let
\boldsymbol{v}^{j}:=(T,v^{r,\,j}_{n-r+2},\ldots,v^{r,\,j}_{n})
. We deduce that

0\equiv J_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})\cdot(\boldsymbol{v}^{j}-\boldsymbol{V}^{j})+\mathcal{O}\big((\boldsymbol{v}^{j}-\boldsymbol{V}^{j})^{2}\big)\mod M^{j}

in
(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{j}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
and, since
k\leq j
,
a fortiori
in

(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{k}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
. By the induction hypothesis,
\boldsymbol{v}^{j}\equiv\boldsymbol{V}^{j}\mod p^{2^{k-1}}
, and thus
(\boldsymbol{v}^{j}-\boldsymbol{V}^{j})^{2}\equiv 0\mod p^{2^{k}}
, leading to

0\equiv J_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})\cdot(\boldsymbol{v}^{j}-\boldsymbol{V}^{j})\mod M^{j}

in
(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{k}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
. Since
J_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},\boldsymbol{V}^{j})
is
invertible modulo
M^{j}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{j}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
, it is invertible
modulo
M^{j}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{k}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
, and
v^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}\equiv V^{j}_{n-r+i}\mod M^{j}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{k}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
for
1\leq i\leq r
.
Since
\deg v^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i},\deg V^{j}_{n-r+i}<\deg M^{j}
, it
follows that
v^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}=V^{j}_{n-r+i}
in

(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{k}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
for
1\leq i\leq r
, proving the claim.

Finally, since
M^{j}\equiv m^{j}_{r}
and
V^{j}_{n-r+i}\equiv v^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}\mod p^{2^{j}}
, we obtain

W^{j}\equiv(m^{j}_{r})^{\prime}v^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}\equiv w^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}\mod m^{j}_{r}

in

(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{j}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
. As
\deg W^{j}_{n-r+i},\deg w^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}<\deg m^{j}_{r}
, this implies

W^{j}_{n-r+i}\equiv w^{r,\,j}_{n-r+i}\mod p^{2^{j}}

for all
j\geq 0
. This completes the proof.
∎

We have the following result regarding the computational complexity
of these computations.

Proposition 74
.

Suppose that
F_{1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n}),\ldots,F_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n})
are given by
a straight–line program of length
L
. Then Algorithm
72
can be performed using

\mathcal{O}\Bigl(\bigl((Lr+r^{4}+\log_{2}(\delta_{r})\log_{2}\log_{2}p){\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}p)+(Lr+r^{3}){\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(k\log_{2}p\bigr)\Bigr)

bit
operations.

Proof.

Step
1
consists of one inversion and
r-1
multiplications in
the ring
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]/(m_{r,p})
. The inversion can be performed using

\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\delta_{r})\log_{2}\delta_{r})
additions and
multiplications, plus
\mathcal{O}(\delta_{r})
inversions in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}
,
amounting to a total cost of

\mathcal{O}\bigl({\sf M}(\delta_{r})\log_{2}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}p)+\delta_{r}{\sf M}(\log_{2}p)\log_{2}\log_{2}p\bigr)=\mathcal{O}\bigl({\sf M}(\delta_{r})\log_{2}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}p)\log_{2}\log_{2}p\bigr)

bit operations. The
r-1
multiplications each cost

\mathcal{O}\bigl({\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}p)\bigr)
, so step

1
requires in total

\mathcal{O}\bigl((r+\log_{2}(\delta_{r})\log_{2}(\log_{2}p)){\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}p)\bigr)

bit operations.

We now analyze step
3
. We begin with
j=0
and apply Algorithm

70
with input
G_{i}:=F_{i}({\boldsymbol{p}^{r}},Y_{n-r+1},\dots,Y_{n})
for
1\leq i\leq r
,
m:=M^{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{v}}:={\boldsymbol{V}^{0}}:=(V^{0}_{n-r+1},\dots,V^{0}_{n})
with
{\sf I}:=p\mathbb{Z}
.

Step
1
in Algorithm
70

computes the inverse
(J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{0}))^{-1}
in
\bigl(\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]/(M^{0})\bigr)^{r\times r}
. According to Lemma
36
, the determinant and the adjoint matrix of

J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{0})
can be computed with
\mathcal{O}(Lr+r^{4})
arithmetic operations
in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]/(M^{0})
, and thus with
\mathcal{O}((Lr+r^{4}){\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}p)\bigr)
bit operations. The inversion of

\det(J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{0}))
in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]/(M^{0})
requires an additional

\mathcal{O}\bigl({\sf M}(\delta_{r})\log_{2}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}p)\log_{2}\log_{2}p\bigr)

bit operations. Then,
(J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{0}))^{-1}=(\det(J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{0})))^{-1}\mathrm{Adj}(J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{0}))

can be computed with
r^{2}
additional multiplications in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]/(M^{0})
, which amounts to
\mathcal{O}\bigl(r^{2}{\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}p)\bigr)
bit operations. Thus the computation of

(J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{0}))^{-1}
requires a total of

\mathcal{O}\bigl((Lr+r^{4}+\log_{2}(\delta_{r})\log_{2}(\log_{2}p)){\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}p)\bigr)

bit operations.

In step
2.1
, we compute

{\boldsymbol{w}^{t}}:=({\boldsymbol{V}^{0}})^{t}-(J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{0}))^{-1}\cdot(F_{1}({\boldsymbol{p}^{r}},\boldsymbol{V}^{0}),\cdots,F_{1}({\boldsymbol{p}^{r}},\boldsymbol{V}^{0}))

in
(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2}\mathbb{Z})[T]/(M^{0})
. Evaluating the polynomials
F_{1}({\boldsymbol{p}^{r}},\boldsymbol{V}^{0}),\ldots,F_{r}({\boldsymbol{p}^{r}},\boldsymbol{V}^{0})
requires

\mathcal{O}\bigl(L\bigr)
arithmetic operations in
(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2}\mathbb{Z})[T]/(M^{0})
, and computing the matrix-vector product and subtraction adds
another
\mathcal{O}(r^{2})
arithmetic operations in
(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2}\mathbb{Z})[T]/(M^{0})
. Hence, step
2.1
costs

\mathcal{O}\bigl((Lr+r^{2}){\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}(p^{2}))\bigr)

bit operations.

Steps
2.2
through
2.5
involve
\mathcal{O}(r)
additions
and multiplications in
(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2}\mathbb{Z})[T]/(M^{0})
, with cost

\mathcal{O}\bigl(r{\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}(p^{2}))\bigr)
bit
operations. Therefore, the total cost of Algorithm
70
for
j=0
is dominated by the computation of

(J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{0}))^{-1}
in step
1
.

Now, let
0<j<j_{0}
and suppose, by induction, that step
3

of Algorithm
72
has been executed
for all
0\leq j^{\prime}<j
. In particular, assume we have already
computed the polynomials
M^{j}
,
\boldsymbol{V}^{j}:=(V^{j}_{n_{r}+1},\cdots,V^{j}_{n})
of
(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{j}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
, and a matrix
C\in(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{j-1}})[T]^{r\times r}
such that
C
is the inverse of

J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{j-1})
in

\bigl((\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{j-1}}\mathbb{Z})[T]/(M^{j-1})\bigr)^{r\times r}
.

To compute the polynomials
M^{j+1}
,
\boldsymbol{V}^{j+1}:=(V^{j+1}_{n-r+1},\cdots,V^{j+1}_{n})
of

(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{j+1}}\mathbb{Z})[T]
, we apply Algorithm
70
with input
m:=M^{j}
,
{\boldsymbol{v}}:={\boldsymbol{V}^{j}}
, and
{\sf I}=p^{2^{j}}\mathbb{Z}
. According to Remark
71
, the inverse
(J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{j}))^{-1}
can be obtained via
the identity

(J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{j}))^{-1}=2C-J_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}^{j})C^{2}\in(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2^{j}})[T]^{r\times r},

which requires
\mathcal{O}\bigl(r^{3}{\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}(p^{2^{j}}))\bigr)
bit operations. Step
2
of Algorithm

70
involves evaluations of the

F_{i}
’s and various updates, requiring
\mathcal{O}\bigl((Lr+r^{2}){\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}(p^{2^{j+1}}))\bigr)
bit operations.

Therefore, the total cost of step
3
of Algorithm
72
for fixed
j
is

\mathcal{O}\bigl((Lr+r^{3}){\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(\log_{2}(p^{2^{j+1}}))\bigr)

bit operations. Summing over
1\leq j<j_{0}-1
, we obtain a total cost of

\mathcal{O}\bigl((Lr+r^{3}){\sf M}(\delta_{r})\sum_{j=1}^{j_{0}-1}{\sf M}(\log_{2}(p^{2^{j+1}}))\bigr)=\mathcal{O}\bigl((Lr+r^{3}){\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(k\log_{2}(p)\bigr)

bit operations.

Finally, step
4
of Algorithm
72
consists of
r-1
multiplications in

(\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}\mathbb{Z})[T]/M^{j_{0}}
, requiring
\mathcal{O}\bigl(r{\sf M}(\delta_{r}){\sf M}(k\log_{2}p)\bigr)
bit operations.
∎

For the final step of reconstructing the output Kronecker
representation over
\mathbb{Q}
, we employ the well-known
Rational
reconstruction
algorithm (see, for example,
(
von zur Gathen and Gerhard 1999
, §5.10)
). Its complexity is summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 75
.

Let
\alpha:=p/q\in\mathbb{Q}
, with
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
coprime, and
let
f,g\in\mathbb{Z}
with
0\leq g<f
, satisfying the following
conditions:

•

The height of
\alpha
satisfies
{\sf h}(\alpha)\leq\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}(f/2)
.

•

\gcd(q,f)=1
, and
pq^{-1}\equiv g\mod(f)
, where
q^{-1}
denotes the inverse of
q
modulo
f
.

Given
g
and
f
, the integers
p
and
q
can be deterministically
computed with
\mathcal{O}({\sf M}(\log_{2}f))
bit operations.

By combining Algorithm
72
with the
rational reconstruction algorithm from the lemma above, we obtain
the following procedure for reconstructing the output Kronecker
representation over
\mathbb{Q}
from its modular reduction.

Algorithm 76

(Reconstruction of a Kronecker representation)
.

Input:

1.

The polynomials
F_{1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n}),\ldots,F_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n})
.

2.

A lucky prime
p
as in Theorem
63
.

3.

The polynomials
m_{r,p},w^{r}_{{n-r+2},p},\ldots,w^{r}_{n,p}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]

forming a Kronecker representation of
\pi_{r,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r}_{p})
,
with
Y_{{n-r+1},p}
as primitive element,

4.

An upper bound
\eta\in\mathbb{N}
for the heights of the coefficients
of
m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}
in the Kronecker representation of

\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
over
\mathbb{Q}
, with
Y_{n-r+1}
as primitive
element.

Output:
The polynomials
m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}
forming
a Kronecker representation of
\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
, with

Y_{n-r+1}
as primitive element.

1
Apply Algorithm
72
with
k

equal to the smallest power of
2
such that
\eta\leq(1/2)\log_{2}(p^{k}/2)
, to compute
p
-adic approximations of order

k
of
m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}
.

2
Apply rational reconstruction to
determine the coefficients of
m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}
from the
approximations obtained in step
1
.

We then have the following result regarding the overall complexity:

Lemma 77
.

Suppose that
F_{1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n}),\ldots,F_{r}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r},Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n})
are given by a
straight–line program in
\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[Y_{n-r+1},\ldots,Y_{n}]
of length at most
L
.
Then Algorithm
76
can be
performed using

\mathcal{O}^{\sim}\bigl((Lr+r^{4})\delta_{r}\eta+r\delta_{r}\log_{2}p\bigr)

bit operations.

Proof.

If
k=1
, then
\eta\leq\log_{2}p
, and step
1
does not involve
any computations. By Lemma
75
, step
2
requires

\mathcal{O}^{\sim}(r\delta_{r}\log_{2}p\bigr)
bit operations.

On the other hand, if
k>1
, then we have
k\log_{2}p\in\mathcal{O}(\eta)
. According to Proposition
74
, step
1
requires

\mathcal{O}^{\sim}((Lr+r^{4})\delta_{r}\eta)
bit operations, and step
2
adds
another
\mathcal{O}^{\sim}(r\delta_{r}\eta)
bit operations. This completes the
proof.
∎

7.3
The whole algorithm for
k=\mathbb{Q}

By combining the algorithms for computing a Kronecker representation
over a finite field,
p
-adic lifting, and reconstruction over
\mathbb{Q}
,
we obtain an algorithm to compute a Kronecker representation over

\mathbb{Q}
of a lifting fiber of the input variety
V
.

We proceed as follows. Assume that the input polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G
are provided by a straight–line program
\beta
of length at
most
L
with integer parameters. First, we randomly choose a lucky
pair
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})\in\mathcal{S}^{n^{2}}\times\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
, where
\mathcal{S}
is defined as earlier. Then,
we compute a “lucky” prime
p
according to Lemma
69
. Reducing the parameters of
\beta

modulo
p
we obtain a straight–line program
\beta_{p}
of length at
most
L
, representing the polynomials
F_{1,p},\dots,F_{r,p},G_{p}
.
We can then apply Algorithm
58
with

k:=\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}
to compute a Kronecker representation
m_{r,p},w^{r}_{{n-r+2},p},\ldots,w^{r}_{n,p}
of the lifting fiber

\pi_{r,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r}_{p})
of

V_{r,p}:=\overline{V(F_{1,p},\ldots,F_{r,p})\setminus V(G_{p})}
,
with
Y_{n-r+1,p}
as primitive element. Finally, using the rational
reconstruction of Algorithm
76
, we lift these polynomials to
obtain the Kronecker representation
m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}
of
the lifting fiber
\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
of
V_{r}
over
\mathbb{Q}
, with

Y_{n-r+1}
as primitive element. This yields an efficient algorithm
to compute the desired Kronecker representation, provided that the
random choices of
(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{p})
and
p
are lucky.

We summarize
the procedure in the following algorithm.

Algorithm 78

(Kronecker representation over
\mathbb{Q}
)
.

Input:

1.

Polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
satisfying
(
{\sf H}_{1}
)–(
{\sf H}_{2}
);

2.

A parameter
\varepsilon
with

0<\varepsilon<1/2
;

3.

Access to uniformly random elements of the
finite set
\mathcal{S}:=\{0,\ldots,\lceil 2\varepsilon^{-1}D\rceil\}
, where
D
is defined as in (
7.1
).

Output:

1.

Linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
;

2.

A point
\boldsymbol{p}^{r}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-r})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-r}
;

3.

Polynomials
m,w_{n-r+2},\ldots,w_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}[T]
;

such that:

1.

The linear map
\pi_{r}:V\to\mathbb{A}^{n-r}
defined by
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n-r}

is a finite morphism;

2.

\boldsymbol{p}^{r}
is a lifting point of
\pi_{r}
;

3.

\pi_{r}:V\to\mathbb{A}^{n-r}
, the linear form
Y_{n-r+1}
, and
the polynomials
m,w_{n-r+2},\ldots,w_{n}
, form a Kronecker
representation of
\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
.

1
Randomly select the coefficients
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{S}^{n\times n}

of linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
. Set
\boldsymbol{Y}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})
.

2
Randomly select the coordinates of a point

\boldsymbol{p}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1})\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
. Set
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-s})
for
1\leq s\leq r
.

3
Compute a lucky prime
p
via
Lemma
69
.

4
Compute the reduced linear forms
Y_{1,p},\ldots,Y_{n,p}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
and the point
\boldsymbol{p}_{p}:=(p_{1,p},\ldots,p_{n-1,p})\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}^{n-1}
. Set
\boldsymbol{Y}_{p}:=(Y_{1,p},\ldots,Y_{n,p})

and
\boldsymbol{p}^{s}_{p}:=(p_{1,p},\ldots,p_{n-s,p})
for
1\leq s\leq r
.

5
Obtain a straight-line program for evaluating
the polynomials
F_{i,p}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=F_{i,p}(\boldsymbol{Y}_{p})

(1\leq i\leq r)
and
G_{p}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=G_{p}(\boldsymbol{Y}_{p})
.

6
Compute the dense representation
m_{1,p}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]
of

m_{1,p}=F_{1,p}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{1},T)/\gcd\big(F_{1,p}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{1},T),G_{p}^{\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{1},T)\big).

7
Set

e:=\max\{1,\log_{p}(12r\varepsilon^{-1}d^{4r})\}
; define
k:=\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp^{e}}
.

8
For
s=1,\ldots,r-1
, do

8.1
Apply Algorithm
32

to compute a Kronecker representation of

C_{s}:=\pi_{s,p}^{-1}(\{Y_{1,p}=p_{1,p},\ldots,Y_{n-s-1,p}=p_{n-s-1,p}\}),

with

Y_{n-s+1,p}
as primitive element;

8.2
Apply Algorithm
50
over
k

to compute polynomials
m_{s+1,p},v_{n-s+1,p}^{s+1}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]
satisfying
m_{s+1,p}(Y_{n-s,p})\equiv 0
and

Y_{n-s+1,p}-v_{n-s+1,p}^{s+1}(Y_{n-s,p})\equiv 0
in
\pi_{s+1,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{s+1})
, with
\deg m_{s+1,p}=\delta_{s+1}
,
\deg v_{n-s+1,p}^{s+1}<\delta_{s+1}
.

8.3
Apply
Algorithm
55
to compute polynomials
v_{n-s+2,p}^{s+1},\ldots,v_{n,p}^{s+1}\in\mathbb{F}_{\hskip-1.99168ptp}[T]

satisfying

Y_{n-s+i,p}-v_{n-s+i,p}^{s+1}(Y_{n-s,p})\equiv 0
in
\pi_{s+1,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{s+1})
for
2\leq i\leq s
, with
\deg v_{n-s+i,p}^{s+1}<\delta_{s+1}
for
2\leq i\leq s
.

9
Compute
w_{n-r+i,p}^{r}:=(m^{\prime}_{r,p})^{-1}v_{n-r+i,p}^{r}
for
2\leq i\leq r
.

10
Compute an upper bound
\eta\in\mathcal{O}^{\sim}(nd^{r-1}(h+nd))
for the heights of
m_{r},w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w^{r}_{n}
.

11
Set
m_{p}:=m_{r,p}
and
w_{n-r+2,p}:=w_{n-r+2,p}^{r},\ldots,w_{n,p}:=w_{n,p}^{r}
.

12

Apply Algorithm
76
using

m_{r,p}
,

w_{n-r+2,p}^{r},\ldots,w_{n,p}^{r}
and
\eta
to obtain
m_{r},w_{n-r+2}^{r},\ldots,w_{n}^{r}
.

13
Output
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
,
\boldsymbol{p}^{r}
,
m:=m_{r}
and
w_{n-r+2}:=w^{r}_{n-r+2},\ldots,w_{n}:=v^{r}_{n}
.

We establish the correctness of this algorithm.

Theorem 79
.

For a lucky choice of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
,
\boldsymbol{p}
and
p
, Algorithm

78
correctly computes a Kronecker representation
of a lifting fiber of
V
.

Proof.

Suppose the randomly chosen
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
\boldsymbol{p}
in steps

1
and
2
are lucky. Then, by Theorem
64
, the linear forms
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
and the point
\boldsymbol{p}^{r}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-r})
satisfy the output properties (1) and (2),
with
Y_{n-r+1}
is a primitive element of
\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
.
Let
m_{r}\in\mathbb{Q}[T]
denote the minimal polynomial of
Y_{n-r+1}
over

\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
, and let
w_{n-r+2}^{r},\ldots,w_{n}^{r}\in\mathbb{Q}[T]

be the polynomials satisfying
m^{\prime}_{r}(Y_{n-r+1})Y_{n-r+i}\equiv w_{n-r+i}(Y_{n-r+1})
over
\pi_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}^{r})
, with
\deg w_{n-r+i}<\deg m_{r}
, for
2\leq i\leq r
. Then
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
,
\boldsymbol{p}^{r}
,
m_{r},w_{n-r+2}^{r},\ldots,w_{n}^{r}
satisfy output condition
(3).

Assuming that the prime
p
computed in step
3
is lucky,
Theorem
63
ensures that conditions (1)–(5)
hold for
1\leq s<r
, and Theorem
64

guarantees that the linear forms
Y_{1,p},\ldots,Y_{n,p}
, the point

\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{s}
, and the polynomials
m_{s,p},w_{n-s+2,p}^{s},\ldots,w_{n,p}^{s}
, form a Kronecker representation of
\pi_{s,p}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p}^{s})
for
1\leq s\leq r
. Therefore, step
6
is well-defined
and, for
e
as in step
7
, step
8
is also well-defined.
By Proposition
67
, the height bound

\eta
in step
10
can be computed. Consequently, the
polynomials
m_{r,p},w_{n-r+2,p}^{r},\ldots,w_{n,p}^{r}
, the prime
p

and the integer
\eta
satisfy the input assumptions of Algorithm

76
, and the linear forms

Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
, the point
\boldsymbol{p}^{r}
and the polynomials
m,w_{n-r+2},\ldots,w_{n}
of step
13
form the correct Kronecker
representation over
\mathbb{Q}
.
∎

Finally, we analyze the computational cost.

Theorem 80
.

Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
be polynomials of degree at
most
d>0
with integer coefficients of bit length at most
h
,
satisfying hypotheses
({\sf H}_{1})
–
({\sf H}_{2})
. For
1\leq s\leq r
, let
V_{s}
denote the Zariski closure of

\{F_{1}=\cdots=F_{s}=0,G\not=0\}
, and
\delta_{s}
its degree. Define

\delta:=\max\{\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{s}\}
, and assume that

\delta>d
. Suppose
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r},G\in\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
are given by a
straight–line program in
\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
of length
L
with
parameters of bit length at most
h
. Let
0<\varepsilon<1/18r
and

m\in\mathbb{N}
be given. Then Algorithm
78
computes a Kronecker representation of a lifting fiber of
V_{r}

of degree
\delta_{r}
with bit complexity

\mathcal{O}^{\sim}\Big((n^{5}+n(L+n^{2})d\delta^{2})\log_{2}(\varepsilon^{-1})+n^{2}Ld^{r}\delta_{r}(h+\log(\varepsilon^{-1}))+\log_{2}^{3}(\varepsilon^{-1})\Big),

and probability of success at least

(1-2\varepsilon)(1-8r\varepsilon)\geq(1-9r\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
69
, step
3

requires

\mathcal{O}^{\sim}\big(\log_{2}^{2}(d^{r}2^{n}h\varepsilon^{-1})\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\big)

bit operations. By Theorem
62
, steps

6
–
8
require

\mathcal{O}^{\sim}\Big(\big(n^{4}+r^{2}(L+n^{2}+r^{3})d\delta^{2}\big)\log_{2}p\Big)

bit operations, and since
\log_{2}p\in\mathcal{O}^{\sim}(\log_{2}(d^{r}2^{n}h\varepsilon^{-1}))
, these steps need

\mathcal{O}^{\sim}\Big(\big(n^{5}+r^{2}n(L+n^{2}+r^{3})d\delta^{2}\big)\log_{2}(h\varepsilon^{-1})\Big)

bit operations. By Lemma
77
, step

12
adds
\mathcal{O}^{\sim}\big((Lr+r^{4})\delta_{r}\eta+n\delta_{r}\log_{2}p\big)

bit operations. Taking into account the height of
p
and the
estimate for
\eta
of Proposition
67
, this is

\mathcal{O}^{\sim}\big(n^{2}d^{r}L\delta_{r}(h+\log_{2}(\varepsilon^{-1}))\big)

bit operations, which yields the total cost as claimed. The
probability of success follows from Lemmas
66
and
69
and
the probability of success of the main loop of Algorithm
58
.
∎
